Google 



This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project 

to make the world's books discoverable online. 

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain. A public domain book is one that was never subject 

to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books 

are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that's often difficult to discover. 

Marks, notations and other maiginalia present in the original volume will appear in this file - a reminder of this book's long journey from the 

publisher to a library and finally to you. 

Usage guidelines 

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the 
public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing tliis resource, we liave taken steps to 
prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying. 
We also ask that you: 

+ Make non-commercial use of the files We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for 
personal, non-commercial purposes. 

+ Refrain fivm automated querying Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google's system: If you are conducting research on machine 
translation, optical character recognition or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the 
use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help. 

+ Maintain attributionTht GoogXt "watermark" you see on each file is essential for in forming people about this project and helping them find 
additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it. 

+ Keep it legal Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just 
because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other 
countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can't offer guidance on whether any specific use of 
any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book's appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner 
anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liabili^ can be quite severe. 

About Google Book Search 

Google's mission is to organize the world's information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers 
discover the world's books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web 

at |http: //books .google .com/I

THE AUTOMATON CHESS PLAYER. 

At this moment, when a successor to Kempeien's Automatic Chess Turk is 
exciting the curiosity of the public in London, the following account of the 
destruction of that fisunous mechanical enigma may not be uninteresting. It 
was written for the American Chess Monthly ^ a few years back: — 

THE LAST OF A VETERAN CHESS PLAYER. 

One of the most famous personages of the last hundred years has passed away, 
and it would, indeed, be neglectful to omit some notice of one whose long life 
has been a series of such strange vicissitudes and changing fortunes. A consti- 
tution of iron enabled him to endure with patience long voyages, changing 
climates, and many sad reverses. His paradgxical existence has at last come 
to a close, and we are now called upon to allude to the incidents of a career 
more than usually eventful. 

The subject of our sketch was born of reputable parentage, about the dose 
of the year 1 769, in the city of Presburg, in Hungary. If he ever had a mother, 
history has failed to record her name. His paternal relative was a Hungarian 
official of good birth and fair character. Although himself a christian, he had 
the singular caprice of clothing his child in oriental attire, and even obliged him 
to wear, during his whole life, the outward emblems of the creed of Mahomet 
From his earliest years the lamented departed wore a look of solemn gravity 
He never smiled, and was rarely heard to speak. Though compelled, by 
circumstances of primitive formation, to remain seated during many long years, 
and though gifted by illiberal Nature with the use of but one arm, he exhibited 
signs of a clear and precocious intellect. 

It is, however, but little to his credit, that he beat his father before he was 
six months old, and not long after, waged successful war against his Intimate 
sovereign, the Empress Maria Theresa. Later in life, he was the friend of 
Franklin, the opponent of George the Third, and Louis the XV. — the slave of 
Eug&ie de Beauhamais — the conqueror of Napoleon — the favorite of Frederick 
the Great, and the Grand Turk — to-day a pet of aristocratic circles— to-morrow 
the denizen of a garret. 

In the course of his long and industrious life, he accumulated a large estate, 
which he survived to see wasted by the extravagance of another. He perished, at 

VOL. IV. — NO. I. B 



2 Tll£ CMSSS WORLD. 

last, like St Laurence, amid devouring flames^ shedding no tears, and meeting hist 
fate with the tranquil resignation of a man in a box, with anchylosis of both 
inferior extremities. As a republican, he must undoubtly claim our respect, 
since, perhaps, no other man has ever checked the march of so many kings as he. 

The writer of this sketch was near the deceased at the time of his death. 
It was in Philadelphia, on the night of the 5th of July, 1854, about half-past 
ten o'clock. The east roof of the National Theatre was a mass of whirling 
flames. The front of the Girard House was on fire, A dozen dwellings were 
blazing fiercely, and the smoke and flame were already curling in eddies about 
the roof, and through the windows of the well known Chinese Museum. At 
the eastern end of this building, nearest to the fire, our friend had dwelt for 
many years. Struggling through the dense crowd, we entered the lower hall, 
and passing to the far end, reached the foot of a small back stair case. The 
landing above us was concealed by a curtain of thick smoke, now and then 
alive, as it were, with quick tongues of writhing flame. Tq ascend was impos- 
sible. Already the fire was about him. Death found him tranquil. He who 
had seen Moscow perish, knew no fear of fire. We listened with painful anxiety. 
It might have been a sound from the crackling wood -work, or the break- 
ing window-panes, but, certain it is, that we thought we heard, through the 
struggling flames, and above tha din of outside thousands, the last words of our 
departed friend, the sternly whispered, oft-repeated syllables^ ecAec! echecH 

If, gentle reader, you are above the age of twenty years, you will remember 
amidst your early reminiscences, the far-famed Maelzel's exhibition. There is a 
long row of wide-awake little faces on the front bench. The wild eyes of child- 
hood are watching the nimble pupypets. A paynim-puppet cuts deftly off a 
puppet's head, and tiny tumblers leap and bound, and Lilliputians ride more 
desperately than bigger men, and fight and quarrel in similar fashion. You 
will remember, also, the little oyster woman, with her sugar bivalves, which 
none but the little girls got ; and how you wished you too were a girl for the 
nonce, all for the love of sugar-oysters. Then Moscow burned for the thou- 
sandth time, and while Napoleon fled and Frank and Cossack rode and ran, 
you avowed to your big brother that it was great fun, and you'd just like to 
come eveiy night, always. And then those babies, whose squeezed stomachs 
gutturally groaned ma ma, papa, you can buy them in any toy-shop now-a-days. 
That stalwart trumpeter, so gifted with unbounded lungs, we shall not soon 
forget him ; and last, and, perhaps, least for you, that joy of your elders — the 
Automaton Chess-player, the Turk— even he, with his oriental silence and 
rolling eyes, would haunt your nightly visions for many an evening after. Since 
then we have known Aim better, and we confess to this day a certain mysterious 
awe of his eternal cross-leggedness, his turbaned front, and left-handed activity. 

It is our intention to record as briefly as possible, the incidents of the 
Tuik's career, touching both lightly and briefly on the better known points. 



TMB CHESS WOKLD. 3 

Perhaps no secret was ever kept as the Turk's has been. Guessed at, in 
part, many times, no one of the several explanations in our possession has ever 
practically solved this amusing puzzle. For several years the Turk was owned 
by a company of Philadelphia gentlemen. All of them were aware of the 
secret. It was, however, a self-guarded trust, since few who had seen the 
interior arrangements of the automaton would have been able to expound his 
puzzling anatomy. Half in jest, then, and partly from the above named con- 
sideration, the secret has keen kept. There are no longer any reasons for 
concealing from the amateurs of chess the solution of this ancient enigma. 
Wolfgang, Baron von Kempelen, the inventor of the automaton chess-player, 
was born in Hungary, about the year 1723. He was an aulic coimcillor of the 
royal chamber of the Hungarian States ; a man of extraordinary mechanical 
ability, a good naturalist, and an excellent artist. A pamphlet by K. G. von 
Windisch, Basle, 1783, gives the best account of him. He seems to have 
devoted a lifetime to mechanics. Among the fruits of his skill were the Cascades 
at the Castle of Schonbrunn, and an automaton which pronounced some thirty 
words and a few phrases. 

In the year 1769, while at Vienna on official business, von Kempelen was 
present at an exhibition of magnetic experiments by one Pclletier, a Frenchman. 
The Empress Maria Theresa was also present. In conversation with her 
majesty, Kempelen intimated that he could make a machine whose results 
would be more surprising than those which they were then witnessing. The 
curiosity of the Empress was excited, and at her request the councillor con- 
structed, some six months after, the well known Automaton Chess-player. 

The automaton made his dibut at Vienna in the Imperial presence. Thence 
von Kempelen removed it to Presburgh, his residence, where for some time he 
amused his leisure with its exhibition to a crpwd of savahs, who flocked from 
far and near to view the wonder. 

At the present day it is difficult to conceive of the eager curiosity with which 
the Turk was everywhere greeted — and it is to be remembered, that at this early 
period his performances were regarded as the bonafde results of mere mechani- 
cal arrangements. The inventor himself by no means encouraged this view of 
his invention, and he very honestly represented it as a bold and fortunate illusion. 

At this time the Turk had no vocal powers, and instead of the fatal ^ckec, 
nodded his head three times. No false moves disconcerted him ; he quietly 
replaced the piece, and shook his head as if displeased. Besides the constant 
good fortime of the automaton in his ordinary chess conflicts, he performed a 
feat at chess which seems to have excited even more wonder than his successful 
play. We allude to the problem of the Knight's move. This must have been 
solved by von Kempelen or his assistant. At present we believe it is well 
understood, and may be found explained in various chess books. In his exhi- 
bitions, as described by contemporaries, von Kempelen opened all the doors of' 

B 2 



4 TUK GUESS WOALD. 

the chest at once, raised the clothes from the mass of clockwork which formed 
the Turk's body, and even exposed the hollow cavity of the Turk's limbs. A 
mysterious casket was invariably placed on a side table. It afterwards 
disappeared, and was probably but a means of misleading the spectator. Von 
Kempelen soon tired of the rdle of showman, and we hear no more of the Turk 
until he reappears at court, by request of Joseph. Again crowds flocked to the 
levee of the inventor, and journals and newspapers spread far and wide the 
fame of the Turk's achievements. 

Von Kempelen's pursuits made sad inroads upon his estate ; accordingly we 
now find the automaton setting out on a tour of exhibition, destined to replenish 
the pockets of his master the councillor. 

In the year 1783, the Android encountered the Chess-king, Philidor, at the 
Cafe de la R^gence, at Paris. Before the Philidors and Legals of this famous 
resort, the crescent of the Turk grew pale, and he met with a number of re- 
verses. Here, also, he played with Franklin, who was then at Passy, and 
whom von Kempelen especially invited, by letter, now in the collection of the 
Philosophical Society, of Philadelphia. In Berlin, the Turk met with better 
fortune. He cpnquered the courtiers of the Great Frederick, and with a sad 
want of complaisance, overcame that monarch himself. The king seems to 
have avenged himself by purchasing the Turk ; we can imagine the ;"esult ; a 
good round sum to von Kempelen. The top of the commode is removed, and 
the secret, complex from its sheer simplicity, is revealed to the resentful king. 
So von Kempelen goes smiling back to Presburg, and the Turk sits sullen in a 
garret at Potsdam. When Napoleon entered Berlin, in 1806, somebody 
thought of the neglected Turk, and Mr. Mselzel, a clever mechanician, was ordered 
to inspiect and repair the dusty old enigma. From cob webbed dreams of 
King Fritz, and the brave Empress, the veteran Chess-player, awakened to 
encounter a greater than they, fresh from the field of recent victories. On this 
remarkable meeting we may dwell for a moment, since its history has been 
faithfully preserved by an eye-witness, and has never before met the public view. 

The Emperor, on this occasion, signified his wish to do battle with the Turk, 
and, accordingly, Mr. Maelzel arranged a second table, near that of the Turk, 
proposing to repeat the moves on both tables. This was Mselzel's usual mode 
of exhibition. Napoleon characteristically overstepping the barrier which 
separated the Turk from the audience, struck his hand on the automaton's 
chess-board, and exclaimed, ** I will not contend at a distance ! We fight face 
to face." A grave nod indicated the Turk's assent, and the game began. 
The Emperor was diastrously vanquished. Shortly afterwards, a second 
exhibition was ordered. On this memorable occasion, the Emperor placed a 
large magnet on the automaton's board. Maelzel smilingly moved the iron, so 
as not to embarrass the game. The Turk played on, with his usual skill ; the 
fatal ^c/ifc was heard again and again, and a second time Napoleon was defeated. 

To be concluded in our next. 



THB CHBSS WORLD. 5 

THE RUY LOPEZ, OR SPANISH OPENING. 

The Leipsic Schachzeitung for January contains the following rather interesting 
paper on the inveterately vexed question of a defence to the Ruy Lopa Knight's 
Opening. 

It is the natural duty of a Chess Magazine, as an organ for the promotion 
of the game, to give attention to every contribution which tends to develope 
the theory of the openings ; more especially to such as concern dibuts, which 
happen at the time to be particularly in vogue. For this reason our Magazine 
has occupied itself considerably during the last few years with the Evans* Gam- 
bit, and with the Spanish Game. In the case of the latter, indeed, we have 
rejected no contributions which seemed to lessen the difficulty of the defence. 
Last year we published, among others, an attempt by a very able amateur of 
the gaftie, at Mecklenburgh, to prove that the line of defence — 

1. P. to K. fourth P. to K. fourth 

2. Kt to K. B. third Kt. to Q. B. third 

3. B. to Q. Kt fifth B, to Q. B. fourth 

4. P. to Q. B. third P, to K, B. fourth 

which all the books decried as unsatisfactory, was a valid one. 

We devoted considerable space to the variations of the amateur in question — 
Dr. A. Schliemann, in Schwerin — and to-day we publish another contribution 
from the same source, although we do not estimate the defence he advocates so 
highly as the ordinary one. 

An objection raised from another quarter upon the point whether, after — 

1. P. to K. fourth P. to K. fourth 

2. Kt. to K. B. third Kt to Q. B. third 

3. B. to Q. Kt fifth B. to Q. B. fourth 

4. P. to Q. B. third P. to K. B. fourth 

5. P. to Q. fourth P. takes K. P. 

6. B. takes Kt Q. P. takes B. 

7. Kt takes K, P.— 

It is advisable for Black to play — 

7. . B. to Q. third 

on account of — 

8. Q. to K. R. fifth (check)— 

is contested with great energy by Mr. Schliemann, who, after the following moves, 
prefers Black's game : — 

8. Q. to K. R. fifth (check) P. to K, Kt third 

9. Kt takes Kt. P. Kt to K. B. third 

10. Q. to K. R sixth R. to K. Kt square 

11. Kt to K. B. fourth B. to K. B. square 

12. Q. to K. third 

[If Q. to K. B. fourth, Black would answer with B. to Q. third.] 

And Black's game is safe. 
Our opinion, in the interests of the defence, is that Black would do better 
if at move 7, instead of B. to Q. third, he played 7. Q. to Q. fourth. 

We reserve to ourselves the right of returning to the subject; but now, as 
we promised, shall allow our Chess friend to speak for himself, as his commu- 
nication contains some highly interesting combinations. 



6 THE CHESS WORLD. 

** In the May number of last year, I endeavoured to prove that the move — 

3. B. to Q. B. fourth 

in the Spanish Opening offers a sufficient defence in connection with— 

4. P. to Q. B. third P. to K. B. fourth 

5. B. takes Kt. Q. P. takes B. 

6. Kt. takes K. P. B. to Q. third 

That variation is not thoroughly correct. It is hardly possible it should be, 
when the Analysis turns on new moves. In my opinion, however, this move is 
not only perfectly safe, but is, besides, the best move at Black's disposal. I 
enclose you five games, part played with Mr. Kade of this town, and part with 
Kliefoth, in which I have adopted this move in the defence, as I have in a great 
many other games at the same opening. These games will perhaps contribute 
towards our obtaining a decisive judgment on the above move, as they exhibit 
some corrections on previous analyses of the defence.

ELUCIDATIONS OF CHESS. 

To THE Editors of the Chess World. 

Sirs, — As the very ancient game of Chess is deservedly gaining popularity, 
permit me to make a few elucidations of it, which have recently occurred to 
my mind, and which, I believe, are not generally known. I wish to show 
that the word cA^k signifies a square, and that the plural chicksy which became 
softened into cfuss, signifies squares, and that, therefore, a game of chess is a 
game of squares ; and that when you check a piece in this game, you square it, 
or throw it into a quadrate rdcUion to a piece of your own, so as to endanger its 
safety. To prove my point, I must make some criticisms on this game, which 
will manifest its extreme antiquity. Shtiach in Hebrew, Chaldee, Syriac, and 
Arabic, signifies equilateral equality, and is therefore used for a square bofiurd or 
bed. In Sanscrit, Hindoo, and Persian, chuok signifies a check or square, fix>m 
ckuo four ; and chuok paurna signifies to make squares. Hence came the 
Italian scacco^ or scacchia^ and scaccerio. The German schack, the Swedish 
skaky the Spanish xague, the Provenyal esccu:, the French echec^ the English check 
and exchequer. *' llius," says Thomson in his Etymons,' "this game may have 
originated in numerical calculations on the abacus, which, according to the 
Lord Chancellor's arms and the brewers' signs, contains similar squares, 
evidently connected with our words exchequer and check in most of their signifi- 
cations." The game of chess, however, originated in more remote times among 
the oriental nations. It was probably known to the Hebrew and Chaldean 
races, and was played from time immemorial by the Persians and Hin- 
doos, under the similar name of shairanj, signifying squares. It was probably 
known to the Greeks under different names, such as tessera^ skiraphiay psephos^ 
and pessia. It was almost certainly known to the Latins. They called the 
chessmen calculi, or latrunadi ; the latter word is generally translated chess- 
men by the best dictionaries, though some scholars suppose it to mean draughts* 
men. I conceive, however, that we can positively prove that latrunculi were 
chessmen, by an ancient Latin poem addressed to Piso, which is to be found at 
the end of some editions of Lucan, and which is quoted and translated in our 
Melmoth's Letters of Pliny. In this poem he not only describes the different 
colours of the pieces, but he speaks of them as having different powers and 
classes — some moving fast and some slow ; some operating at long distances 
and some at short ones. The Latin of this poem begins mus: "TV si forte 
Juvaty studiorum ponder efessum,^'* But I believe most of your readers will like 
it better in Melmoth's English version, a few lines of which I voiture to quote, 
as it may be new and interesting to many: — * 

When to relieve the labours of your mind. 
You turn from deep research in arts refin d 
Not in soft indolence you waste the hour. 
But happier genius stul exerts its power. 
To mimic war the radiant troops are led, 
And martial ranks the Yaried table spresui. 
There sable bands, and here a snow-white train. 
With doubtful fate of war the fight maintain. 
Unnumber'd stratagems thy forces try, 
And artful feign, and only feign to fly. 
Now boldly rushes 'mid the ranks of war. 
The chief who viewed the slaughtering scene from far. 
Thu bravelv daring in the arduous toil, 
Repels the host advaocing to the spoiL 
While cautious that moves dreadful on and slow. 
And fraudful meditates the certain blow ; 
What though in guise he seems a slave in chains, 
Two captives he in durance close detains. 



24 THE OHB8S WOBLD. 

The Latin of this line is " Similisque ligato ohU^ ipse duos,^ which exactly 
represents the condition of a Pawn checking two pieces. 

The poem abounds with many other allusions that apply exclusively to the 
game of Chess; but I must not venture to quote them lest I should weary your 
courteous readers. I remain, yours truly, 

Bath. FRANCIS BARHAM. 

P.S. — ^According to this theory, checkmate does not sigtaiy sheikh iwa/— the 
king is dead, but the sqttare of deaths the fatal square. It would be absurd to 
say check-king, if check were derived from the Persian sheikh^ or seignor. 



M." C. F. DE JAENISCH*S, ESSAY ON THE KING'S KNIGHT'S 

OPENING. 

Dear Sirs, — In playing over the first instalment of Mr. Jaenisch*s interest- 
ing article in your last number, I detected two or three misprints, which it is 
desirable to have corrected before the analyses are reprinted in a collected 
form. 

These little errors, 'no doubt, arose in the translation of the moves from the 
French to the English system of notation. 

The first mistake occurs at the nth line, p. 435, where you give : 

P. takes Q. B. P. 

8. 

P. takes Q. B. P. 

When the context clearly shows that the author wrote : 

P. takes Q. B. P. 

8. 

B. takes Q. B. P. 

The next is at page 441, where White's seventh move in the Double Fianchetto 
is given 
7. B. to Q. B. fourth, 
whereas it ought to be, — 
7. P. toQ. B. fourth. 

Yours truly, 

MATHEMATICIAN. 



«« TALUS." 

Dear Sirs, — A controversy has sprung up in our Club as to the meaning 
of the signature adopted by your trenchant correspondent " Talus.,' Some 
think it has an affinity with Talio ; others, that it is some ancient proper name. 
The only Talus of whom our old friend Lempriere speaks, however, is the 
ingenious nephew of Doedalus, a young gentleman who invented the Saw, the 
Compasses, and other Mechanical contrivances, and got himself turned into a 
Partridge for his pains. It is certainly not from him your correspondent took 
his signature. Then from whom or what did he derive it ? Perhaps, Sirs, as 
YOU are considered omniscient, you can tell us who is — I mean who was — 
«* Talus." 

Faithfully yours, 

A PRESIDENT OF A RUSTIC CHESS CLUB. 



THB CHBSS WOBLD. 26 

[We are not sufficiently all-knowing to resolve this difficulty for the " Rustic 
Chess Club," but as our ** trenchant correspondent " will see their President's 
letter, he will no doubt cut the knot in our next number. Meantime, we may 
be permitted to suggest the possibility of his having in his mind's-eye, when he 
chose the signature in question, that inexorable executioner of rightfuU doom 
in the Faene Qtdeene, whom Astraea willed evermore to wait on ArtegiJl : — 

" But when she parted hence she left her groome, 
An Yron Man, which did on her attend, 
Always to execute her siedfast doome ; 
And willed him with Artegall to wend, 
And doe whatever thing he did intend 
His name was Talus, made of yron mould, 
Immovable, resistlesse, ivithout end ; 
Who in his hand an yron flail did hould, 
JViiM which he thresht out/alshoed and did truth un/ffuld.'[ " 



THE "MORTIMER ATTACK." 

To THB Editors of the " Chbbs "World." 

7th March, 1868. 

Dear Sirs, 

Seeing in this month^s number— amongst the "Answers to Corres- 
pondents " — that one of the readers of the " Chess World " has been 
seeking for information in regard to the opening moves of the " Mortimer," 
it has struck me that an abstract of the leading variations of that brilliant 
attack might perhaps be acceptable to some of the subscribers to this 
Magazine, particularly as that new form of the " Evans " is not in auy of 
the English " Books of the Openings." The table I annex is a synopsis of 
the analyses by Mr. Mortimer which were printed in the April and 
August, 1865, numbers of this magazine, together with some other 
principal yariations which have appeared from time to time in the leading 
Chess Periodicals. 

I belieye that up to this period the scattered yariations haye not 
been published in one collection. I hope that my synopsis will supply 
the desideratum.

THE NORTHUMBERLAND & DURHAM CHESS ASSOCIATION. 



Programme of the Third General Meeting, to be held at the Golden lion 
Hotel, King Street, South Shields, on Easter Monday and Tuesday, April 13th 
and 14th, 1868: — President, the Right Hon. Lord Ravensworth. Vice-Presi- 
dents, J. W. Pease, Esq., M.P., Robert Ingham, Esq., M.P., G. O. Trevelyan, 
Esq., M.P., J. Candlish, Esq., M.P., Edmund Backhouse, Esq., J.P., W. Bourne, 
Esq., M.D., J.P., Rev. C. W. King, M.A., W. Sidney Gibson, Esq., M.A., 
B. White, Esq., M.D., A. M. Scott, Esq., John Williamson, Esq., G. D. Rob- 
son, Esq. Committee, Messrs. W. Harrison, Newcastle, W. Clay, Newcastle, 
Wm. Field, Newcastle, T. Spence, North Shields, C. Edmundson, North 
Shields, Ed. Borring, Esq., North Shields, John Scott, South Shields, James 
Nicholson, South Shields, David Hill, South Shields, Thos. Stokoe, Gateshead, 
Wm. Coulthard, Hexham, Thos. Semple, Stockton, T. B. Special, Darlington, 
W. Park, West Hartlepool, S. Storey, Sunderland, J. M. Hunter, Alnwick, 
E. Elgie, Durham, J. White, Beal, R. Douglas, Berwick, W. Hopkinson, 
Jarrow. Honorary Secretaries, Wm. Mitcheson, Newcastle, Wm. C. Cotton, 
North Shields. Treasurer, Wm. Mitcheson, Newcastle. 

On Monday, play will commence at Noon, and will be continued till Ten 
o'clock p.m. On Tuesday, play will commence at ten o'Clock a.m., and 
terminate at Six p.m., when a Meeting of the Members will be held, to hear 
the report of the Committee read, to appoint the time and place of the next 
Annual Gathering, and to transact the General Business of the Association. 

Problem Tournament. — Open only to Members of the Association. A 
prize of the value of One Pound will be awarded for the best set of two Original 
Problems, which require an unconditional mate to be effected in either three or 
four moves. Competing Problems, when forwarded, should be marked with a 
motto, and a sealed envelope bearing the same motto should be enclose^ with 
them. The name of the composer must not appear on the Problems, but, 
together with his address, should be written on the inside of the accompanying 
envelope. The Problems must be accompanied by the solutions of their authors, 
ai^d are to be deemed as the exclusive property of the Association. 

Game Tournaments, 

General Tourney A. — Open to all amateurs. Persons who wish to 
compete in this Tournament must signify their desire to the Secretaries on or 
before Tuesday, the 31st March, and at the same time pay the entrance fee of 
five shillings. A separate prize will be given on account of every eight players 
who enter the lists. A limitation of the time allowed for the consideration of 
each move will be established, but the Committee hope that the competitors 
will avoid all unnecessary delay in this respect. Two games, exclusive of 
drawn games, which do not help to bring the Tourney to a close, must be 
played by each competitor, on Monday, so that the final round alone shall 
remain for decision on Tuesday. The prize will be increased if the funds 
finally collected permit. Ten shillings will be added to the stakes if there are 
four entries ; and twenty shillings if there are eight entries and fewer than 
sixteen. 

In the event of there being sixteen entries and fewer than twenty-four, the 
players will be divided into two sets, numerically ec^ual, and a prize as described 
above, awarded fo the champion in each set. This principle of division into 
sets of eight will be extended as far as the number of competitors may render it 
necessary. 



THB CHBS8 WOBLD. 38 

In each round of the Touraey, the paired combatants shall play one game 
only, drawn games not being counted. All games must be played in conformity 
with the laws laid down in me ** Praxis." 

Handicap Tourney, B. — Open only to the Members of the Association. 
Competitors will be handicapped according to their recognised skill, by some 
player of known ability and experience, whom the Committee shall hereafter 
appoint This Tourney will be subject to the same r^;ulations as the General 
Tournament, and the entrance-fee and prizes will be of the same amoimt The 
victor in Tourney A will be debarred from carrying off the prize in this 
Tourney. 

Tourney C. — Open only to the Members of the Association, and subject to 
the same regulations as Tourney A. The entrance-fee will be 2s. 6d. No 
added money will be given if Uiere are fewer than four entries ; five shillings 
will be added to the stakes if there are four entries, but fewer than eight ; and 
ten shillings if there are eight entries, but fewer than sixteen. If there are 
sixteen entries, the principle of division into sets of eight will be carried out, as 
in Tourneys A and B. Neither of the victors in the preceding Tourneys will 
be permitted to gain the prize. 

Tourney D. — Open to the younger players of the Association only. For 
every eight players wno mutually ae^ree to form a Tourney, the Committee wUl 
add ten shillings to the amount of the entrance-fees, which shall be 2s. 6d. each. 
The same regulations shall be observed as in Tourney A. 

Tourney E. — Open to all the Members of the Association. No victor in 
any of the preceding Tournaments (except under conditions stated below) shall 
be entitled to carry off the prize in this Tourney. In the event of a victor in 
any oi the other competitions being also champion in this, the prize will devolve 
npon the player who occupies the second place of honour'; and he, if already a 
victor in any joust, must in like manner cede the prize to the player next in 
rank, and so on. No added money will be given if the players holding the 
first and second places cannot receive the prize on accoimt of disqualification. 
Should all the winners in the first round of eight players be prize-holders in 
other Tourneys, then the prize shall be awarded without bar to the ultimate 
victor, just as if no exceptional conditions attached to this Tourney. The 
entrance-fee and prizes will be the same as in Tourney C. 

Other Tourneys will be arranged at the time of the Meeting. The Com- 
mittee will recognize no game as won until a correct copv of it be placed in the 
hands of the S^retaries ; and all games played at the Gathenng are to be 
considered the exclusive property of the Association. The fiiU value of every 
prize will be paid in Chess Boards, Chess Men, Chess Diagrams, Chess Scrap 
Books, Works or Chess Literature, or Money. 

Blindfold Playing. — If there be time, after the termination of the 
principal Tourneys, Mr. D. Hill, who was the victor in Tourney A last year, 
has undertaken to play several games without sight of board or men. 

Any further information respecting the arrangements may be obtained on 
application to 

William Mitcheson, Newcastle, "J 

and \Honorary Secretaries. 

Wm. C. Cotton, North Shields, J

American Chess Congress. — It is now more than ten years since the 
first American Chess Congress was held in this city, and so successful was it in 
its results that it has been matter of surprise to many that no attempt has since 
been made to-organize a similar Chess gathering. We have lately heard, how- 
ever, that there is a possibility of an International Chess Congress taking place 
during the present year, and that Messrs. Staunton, Kolisch, Neiimann, and 
other European masters will endeavour to be present Should such a meeting 
be held, we feel sure that the arrival of such renowned masters will stir up all 
the dormant enthusiasm of our players throughout the country, and create an' 
excitement in the Chess world such as has not been experienced since the ap- 
pearance of Morphy, ten years ago. — ^New York Tutf^ Fidd^ and Farm.

THE LAST OF A VETERAN CHESS PLAYER. 

(Concluded frontpage 4.^ 

The pieces were no sooner re-arranged, than the Emperor quietly removed a 
shawl from the shoulders of a lady near by, and with great care enveloped the 
face, neck, and body of the Turk, completing his arrangements with an excla- 
mation of satisfaction. With a muffled nod* the Moslem agreed to the new 
condition, and this third time also victory declared itself for the Turk. For a 
moment the Emperor regarded his antagonist, then with a gesture of scorn, he 
swept the chess-men from the board, and crying Sagatelle! strode over knight 
and pawn, and so out of the room. Next day, at his own express desire, the 
Emperor beheld a revelation of this amusing secret. Like the great Frederick, 
Napoleon was but little pleased with the result. Who owned the automaton 
just at this time we are unable to say, but Mselzel seems to have been the only 
exhibitor. Yet a third man was fool enough to buy for himself a little dis- 
content and the Turk's secret. Prince Eugene de Beauhamais paid 30,060 frs. 
for this privilege, and seems to have enjoyed it no more than the King or the 
Emperor had done before him. In this dilemma Mr. Mselzel offered to pay 
the Prince a certain sum for permission to travel an4 exhibit the automaton. 
The Prince consented, and thus the Turk and his new master began a second 
career of victory by defeating the Sultan and divers Beys and Muftis in the 
capital of Turkey itself ; and for many years the Turk now wandered, like a 
knight errant through the great cities of the old world. Everywhere he threw 
down the gauntlet, and rarely, very rarely, did he meet with disaster or defeat 
In the year 1826, Maelzel came to America with his wonderful menagerie 
of puppets and animated machinery accompanying the marvellous oriental 
chess-player. What became of Prince Eugene's claim does not appear, but 
Mselzel continued his exhibition, and perhaps the Prince had something else to 
look after at home. During this American tour some clock-making Yankee 
citizen of Northern New York *^ concluded"*^ to make a second Turk, and 
accordingly "ciphered out " an automaton chess-player, which played quite as

THB CHX8S WOBLD. 43 

ment were also seen similar pieces of brass, representing quadrants and other 
philosophical iostruments, designed to give the impression that they conduced, 
as they did not, to the movements of the automaton. The drawer which, 
when drawn out, seemed to be of the entire horizontal dimensions of the chest, 
was also deceptive, as its back end was so constructed as to move upon wheels, 
by means of which it did not press backwards with the sides more than a foot 
and a half; whilst it was caught with these sides and drawn forward as the 
drawer was opened, and therefore gave to the drawer the appearance of being 
two feet six inches in breadth or depth. Behind this movable back of the 
drawer there was therefore left an unoccupied space of the whole length of the 
chest, and rather more than a foot in breadtL In this trough was fixed an 
iron railroad, nearly three feet in length, upon which was placed a low seat, 
resting upon iron runners, adjusted to the rails beneath it Upon this sat the 
operator, who was able by his greased runners to slide backwards and forwards 
upon the rails without quitting his seat, which he occupied throughout the 
exhibition. His l^;s were extended horizontally behind the drawer. 

At the commencement of the show he sat behind the mock machinery of 

the smaller apartment concealed by a door, which divided it into two parts. 

In front, he was concealed by a similar door, which closed on that side the 

larger section of the box, and made it appear complete when op«ti for inspection. 

These two doors were made of half the thickness of the partition,#so that when 

they were both closed or placed in contact, each apartment exhibited a partition 

of a thickness equal to that of the rest of the partition, giving smoothness and 

uniformity to the dividing walL When the apparatus was ready for exhibition, 

the two firont doors were opened at once. A lighted candle was placed in the 

larger, and another was held in front of the smaller apartment, which was 

apparently filled with machinery. After closing these doors, the whole chest is 

turned round, so as to show the back of it to the spectators. While this was 

doing, the concealed operator slid forward into the larger apartment, pulled 

the thin door after him, and pushing before him the similar door in front of 

hioL This movement was sometimes made before the instrument was turned 

round, and before the front door of the smaller apartment was closed, at which 

tune Mr. Mselzel usually held a light at the window in the back of it to show 

that no one was concealed in it As the player slid into the laiger apartment, 

he was compelled to flex his knees, for which action provision was made by 

means of a division in its floor, which was filled up by the knees as they were 

bent, and which was again let down when he slid back to his first position. 

Hie floor thus formed a table for him. The whole apparatus, except the small 

window behind, having been closed, the operator began amidst the clatter of 

noisy machinery to arrange himself for the game. This he did by swinging the 

whole interior furniture, wheels, partitions, and all, against the outer doors and 

£ 2 



44 THE CHBSS WOBLD. 

walls of the box, so as to throw all the subdivisions into one large apartment. 
Having completed this part of the arrangement, the concealed player took 
down the green baize by which the box was lined, and exhibited the under side 
of its top and an apartment in the interior part of the Turk, where burned a 
light carefully concealed until required for use. 

On the underside of the chest appeared a chess board directly beneath that 
upon the surface, upon which the game was played. Each square was 
excavated, so as to make the board between the opposite squares very thin. 
The squares were numbered from one to sixty-four, under each of which hung 
a little lever well balanced, to which was attached a small disc of iron. These 
discs, when attracted by magnets placed on the top of the box, swung up into 
the excavations, and remained there quietly until liberated by the removal 
of the magnets, when they vibrated for some seconds like a well hung bell.* 

In front of the operator was placed, by proper means, another chess board, 
jfirmly fixed. This was also numbered to correspond exactly to that above his 
head, and was perforated by holes like a marine chess board, in order to 
prevent the possibility of his chessmen being disarranged. Each of the latter 
was furnished with a peg suited to the size of the apertures in the board. 
Another hole was seen on each square of the player's board, into which for 
great exactness, the steel point of the lower end of the pentograph might be 
inserted, so as to bring the Turk's hand directly upon the proper place above. 

When entirely prepared for action, the concealed player intimated his wish 
to begin, which was done by signals made at the back window of the box. If 
a whc^e game was to be played — ^which Mselzel agreed to only when sure 
of victory — ^he placed the pieces which contained no magnet next to the Turk, 
and the other set, holding magnets in their interior, next to his adversary. 
The operator within could easily tell when this was done, for all the iron disks 
beneath the magnetised chessmen settled against the top of his department, 
and all the others stood still, in a pendulous posture, ready for action. As the 
Turk always claimed the first move, the operator acting for him first advanced 
a chessman on his own marine board, and then after starting some hoisy 
wheels, made for the sake of sound, seized the pentograph and lifted it up, by 
which the Turk's arm was raised to its proper height He then advanced it to 
the proper square of his board, where, by means of a slight rotation, he opened 
the Turk's fingers, brought them down over the proper pawn, closed the hand 
again, lifted up the arm, and deposited the chessman in the desired spot Of 
course he knew this move, which had already been made on his own chess- 

* Tht means by which the discs of steel were balanced were curiously ingenious : a thin 
piece of copper wire, coiled into a flat spiral, served to counterpoise the disc. Any disturbance 
in the 'equilibrium of these little levers, could then be instantly corrected by the concealed 
operatoi^ who had merely to coil up or uncoil the wire in order to effect the desired adjustment. 



THB CHS8S WOBLD. » 45 

board. When the opponent made his move, one of the disks ah-eady described 
fell as he lifted his magnetic chessman, and began to swing freely, so as to give 
time to the man in the interior to observe it, and make the move over again on 
his own board. As the chessman on the outside of the top of the chest alighted 
upon an unoccupied square, its proper disk ascended, and clung to the inside. 
Thus, without any other trouble than that of repeating each of the adversary's 
moves upon his own board, the operator pursued his game to the end. 

When first exhibited by von Kempelen, the automaton had probably no 
railroad and moveable seat, and the operator — ^necessarily a small man — ^lay at 
length behind the drawer until the chest had been carefiilly closed again. As 
great chess-players are not always diminutive personages, the exhibitor of the 
automaton was finally necessitated to arrange the interior so as to conceal even 
a tall man. Maelzel's best operator, a man named Schlomberger, was upwards 
of six feet in height To secure his Mussulman from all hazard of defeat by an 
abler adversary, ends of games were usually played. These were so contrived 
as to enable the Turk, who always took in such cases the first move, to make 
that a fatal one. These end games — seventeen in number — ^were represented 
in a little book, of which Maelzel had one copy to present to the adversary, and 
another was in possession of the concealed player. 

The interior of the chest bore evidence of having once possessed a telegraph, 
by which Mselzel or von Kempelen might have given advice in a difficult 
emergency of the game, or have directed altogether a less skilful player. The 
mutilated machinery used for this purpose enabled the describer to perceive 
only the object of it, without qualifying him to detail its construction exactly. 
It consisted oi a small circular piece of brass inserted into the back of the box, 
close to the head of the operator. Upon the peripheral face of this on both 
sides were marked numbers from o to 9. Through its centre passed a very 
small rod, which by means of an index enabled the exhibitor without to direct 
the men within, or the man within to consult the exhibitor without. Something 
of this kind must have been used when, at Berlin, Napoleon I. placed a large 
magnet upon the outer chess-board. There were also vestiges of other arrange- 
ments by which, probably at different periods of its history, this curious piece 
of mechanism was managed by other signals. Sometimes, even when Maelzel 
stooped over the box, apparently to wind-up or adjust the machine, and during 
the clatter of its works, he obviously conversed with his assistant within. 
There was not found, upon a careful examination of the automaton, any trace 
of its having been at any time worked without an assistant in its interior.

THOUGHTS ON CHESS. 
By Dr. H. Hirschbach. 

A CONTRIBUTION to the Chess Zeitung ! What is it to be ? An analysis, in 
which you every moment dread that you will be guilty of an oversight ? Or 
something humorous ? Something, for instance, on the objectionable habits of 
Chess-players ? Or shall I put on a serious countenance and launch an article 
on the moral influence of the game ? Chess Analysis ! When I reflect on the 
errors which, despite the greatest care, have crept into the essays I have already 
published in the way of Chess Analysis, my heart sinks within me. 

No more Chess Analysis I No I for it is one thing to-day and another to- 
morrow. To-day you are right, delighted to have finished a task which you 
believe has been well done ; to-morrow you find out that you have overlooked 
an obvious move, which omission is destructive to the whole scheme. Away I 
Leave me, unwelcome remembrances, and be silent htuniliating self-reproach I 

Chess is, indeed, a hard study, and it often cuts me to the heart when I see 
people sit down to so absorbing a pursuit the moment they have swallowed 
dinner, and when their digesting powers are called into fullest action. It is 
well known that a full stomach has a soothing effect upon the mind, and it is 
not surprising, therefore, that in these post-prandial encounters, each player 
seems eager to make such moves as are most pleasant to his opponent. 

It is not an uncommon occurrence that at the outset the whole board is set 
wrongly, which in such games is not of much consequence ; but it is more 
ridiculous when two earnest players sit down to a well-considered game, and 
after setting out the pieces, and even after some moves have been made, become 
aware that the board was wrongly placed. However, we must not think that 
we shall find among inferior players less vanity than among the Great Guns. 
On the contrary, the lower a player stands on the scale the more irritable is he 
at every doubt expressed as to the accuracy of his moves, and though his game 
stand ever so badly, he will not believe it; but considers his play good and even 
better than that of his antagonist. At all events, among Chess-players, one 
meets with very little of that refined and courtly manner which one would 
expect to find exhibited by those who have daily to deal with kings and queens, 
indeed, a great number of Chess-players know not how to deal with these high 
personages, whose every movement is of the utmost importance ; nor even with 
the lower ranks, bishops, knights or pawns. And it is droll to hear from such 
lips that this or that player is strong with his bishops or his pawns. As if any- 
thing could be more important than in every situation to make the most effective 
move by whatever piece one may use. One need not be surprised, if it be 
remarked that this or that move would just now have been the strongest, it be 
answered, **oh ! that may still be done." 



THB 0HB8S WORLD. 47 

Indeed, while with good players every move changes the phjrsiognomy of 
the game, and its development proceeds, often enough with bad players, during 
a greater number of moves, the game remains substantially unchanged. 

A well-known ph3rsician has, in one of the most extensively read periodicals, 
though not in the choicest language, published an article on the bad habits of 
Chess-players. Were we to follow his example, we could write a rich chapter 
concerning their unseemly practices. One of the worst is, perhaps, to suffer a 
doud of tobacco smoke to intercept the gaze of an opponent, especially if, as 
is often the case; the tobacco is not of the finest kind. It is, moreover, 
extremely disagreeable when a player perpetually lets the ashes of his cigar fall 
on the board, and in making his apologies, scatters them on his adversary's 
dress-coat Fearful, certainly, are the movements of the feet of some players, 
whereby the table and the chair of the opponent are made to vibrate, and he 
himself is thrown into a state of nervous agitation. * 

Detestable also is the rapping on the table with the pieces which bad habit 
some players have acquired, and which appears expressly intended to disturb 
the enemy in his combinations. 

In addition, there is the habit of mocking, if the one has at any time made 
a mistake, thereby causing his game to give way ; mocker^, which he who 
indulges in it loves ironically to clothe in the form of praise, though by it the 
opponent is the more exasperated. Or triumphantly leaning back in his chair, 
with a countenance assured of victory, looks round among the bystanders, 
whilst the other, with wounded feelings, sorrowfully supporting his head with 
his hand, vainly endeavours to retrieve his lost fortunes. No ! I have never 
seen a Chess-player shcrw sympathy for a defeated opponent ; at the most 
there is but an expression of regret that by his own carelessness he has allowed 
the enemy to exist a moment longer than he need have done. Therefore, I do 
not believe in the moral influence of Chess. One, who by an artifice, in which 
he persists, endeavours to make another fall, though it be by intellectual 
weapons only, can, in my opinion, lay no claim to moral worth. 

In watching a match one observes only the victor and the vanquished. If 
it be acknowledged that it is only by the trick that one has conquered, it is but 
an excuse to say that the defeated played badly, as the play of the other has in 
reality been much worse. 

The more incorrect and tricky the game, the more the victor considers it 
his highest interest to make it known, considering it even worthy of publication 
as if he had kept strictly within the rules of fair play. And even if a player 
keep within the rules of the game, without seeking to better his chances of 
success by descending to mean artifices, then, — I am afraid to speak out — are 
not often false moves made ? Have I not many a time witnessed how the 
queen's bishop has glided swiftly from c 8 to g 4, notwithstanding that the pawn 



I 



I 



48 



THK CHS88 WOBLD. 



of d 7 sdll stood finn ; and die opponent being full of tlioiight, has, in tlie 
Qprigilitnesi of his own heart, fiuled to mark the false move ? If the moral 
delinqnency of the victor had been earlier discovered, the lesolt of the game 
would have been rendered null and yoid. Sad is it for the loreis of truth that 
we have to reveal these gloomy and dark sides of the practices of Chess-players; 
but has not life its daik and haunted places ? Notwithstanding that, it is said by 
one of the poets, "Life is still beantifnl,'' ? So also say we^ *<Chess is still 
beautiful," that Chess^play in which powerful intellects coming into collision 
produce an emission of bright thoughts, and the sharpest thinkers grasp the 
victor's crown ; while during the hours devoted to the wonhip of Chess the 
most difficult enigmas of life are forgotten.

NORTHUMBERLAND AND DURHAM CHESS ASSOCIATION. 

The members of this Assocla,tion held their annual gathering this year at the 
Golden Lion. Hotel, South Shields, on Monday and Tuesday last The 
weather was much more suitable for out-of-door recreations than for the 
prosecution of a sedentary game like Chess ; but, notwithstanding the strong 
attractions of the country, a goodly assemblage of devoted amateurs mustered 
on the occasion. Several tourneys were organized, and were quickly filled with 
competitors. The following is the score of the two days* play : — 

Tourney A (open to all comers). — First Round, — Thos. Stokoe (Gates- 
head), beat J. Scott (South Shields) ; R. Punshon (Newcastle), beat D. HUl 
(South Shields); W. Park (West Hartlepool), beat G. D. Robson (South 
Shields) ; J. Nicholson (South Shields), beat W. Mitcheson (Newcastle). In 
this round the first game between Messrs. Mitcheson and Nicholson was drawn. 
Second Hound. — R. Punshon beat W. Park, afler playing two games which 
ended in a remise; and T. Stokoe beat J. Nicholson. The last round remains 
to be played between Messrs. Stokoe and Punshon. 

Tourney B (Handicap Tourney, open to members). — First Hound. — 
T. Stokoe beat W. Park ; J. Nicholson beat R. Punshon ; D. Hill beat 
W. Mitcheson ; G. D. Robson beat G. Newton (absent). Second Round.— T>. Hill 
beat G. D. Robson, to whom he gave Q. Kt T. Stokoe and J. Nicholson 
have yet to play. 



64 THE CHBSS WOKLD. 

TousHBY C (Handicap Toume;, open oaly to the members of tbe associa- 
tion). — Firii S<mnd. — f, Kell, South Shields, receiving pawn and move, beat 
G. D. Robson, South Shields; W. C. Cotton, Tynemouth. beat C. Edmundson, 
North Shields, playing even ; J. R. Scott. South Shields, beat W. Stobbs, 
North Shields, playing even. This tourney w.is left unfinished. 

Tourney D (open to members). First Kmnd.—}. K. Scott beat M. Scott,

THE WESTMINSTER CHESS CLUB TOURNAMENT. 

To THE Editors of the Chess World. 

Gentlemen,— -Some two years ago, or thereabouts, the establishment, or 
perhaps, I ought to say, the reconstruction of the Westminster Chess Club was 
announced with a mighty flourish of trumpets. It was destined, we were told 
in the conventional language of prospectus-mongers to "supply a national 
¥rant" — ^it was to become the Head Centre of British Chess ; it was to elevate 
the tone of our noble game ; and, like an infiint Hercules, to stnmgle in its 
cradle the twin serpents of professional play and chess charlatanism. On the 
first blush of the undertaking, everything seemed to promise fiaiV'ourably for the 
future of the young club. A powerful Tnanogtng committee was formed, com- 
prising most of the leading players of the day, amongst whom I may mention 
Messrs. Staunton, Boden,Biid, Wormald, and Burden ; and by way of giving 
an impetus to the game, a Handicap Tournament was set on foot, which 
secured a very numerous and influential entry. Now, it is not my intention to 
here to inquire how it has happened that the managing committee, from being 
a representative body of English players, has dwindled into a narrow clique-— 
how the fundamental rules of the society have been violated — or how the dub 
itself which, it should be remembered, was avowedly established for the cultiva- 
tion of Chess, has been transmuted into a pleasant little whist coterie (and to tell 
the plain truth, but for the support of the card-playing section, the Westminster, 
like the old London Club, or the " British Chess Association," would now be 
little more than nominis umira, both ;socially and financially) ; but as an old 
member, I think I am entitled to ask, through the medium of the Chess 
World, as the recognised organ of British Chess, how long we are to await 
for the termination of the Handicap Tournament ? Personally I do not pro- 
fess to be capable of elucidating this subtle problem, but I can furnish your 
readers with a few simple facts. 

The Tournament, if my memory serves me aright, was commenced 
sometime during the summer of 1866, but I am not in a position to 
speak with any certainty with regard to the precise date. The affair 
was permitted by the Committee to drag its slow length along until 
early in January of tAe present year^ then it was unanimously decided at a 
General Meeting of the Club that another fortnight should be allowed for the 
remaining competitors to play out their games, at the expiration of which period 

r 

the prizes should be awarded according to the then existing state of the score. 
At this time there were practically only five players left in the handicap to compete 
for the three prizes, viz. : — ^Messrs. Wormald, Bayliss, Duffy, McDonnell, and 
Hewett. Of these five Mr. Wormald had scored every game he had played^ 



68 THE CHESS WOBLD. 

and Mr. Bayliss had lost but one ; and as each of these gentlemen had only 
two more games to play, the former was, mider any circomstances, entitled to 
the first prize, which the latter, even supposing he were defeated in both his 
I remaining parties^ must necessarily make a tie for second place. Not a single 

game was played during the prescribed fortnight^ but no further steps were taken 
in the matter by the committee for another month. At the expiration of this 
period the managing committee, represented by a quorum oi six, three 
of whom, I may mention, were actually interested in the result, took upon them- 
selves to re-open the question, and decided (though I believe not unanimously) 
in direct opposition to the expressed opinion of a General meeting of the Club, 
that an additional fortnight should be allowed for play, and that the remaining 
games shoulcPbe played out From this decision Messrs. Wormald and Bayliss, 
in my opinion, very properly dissented, and declined to take any further part in 
the handicap, consequently, two games were scored against each of these 
gentlemen in default. To crown this tissue of absurdities, only tivo games have 
been contested between the three remaining '' disinterested " competitors during 
the last seven weeks, and yet no offidal adjudication of the prizes has been made. 
Under these circumstances, which I have narrated to the best of my humble 
ability, is it too much to ask what has become of the managing committee of 
the Westminster Chess Club. 

' I am, Gentlemen, yours faithfully, 

A Member. 
Westminster Chess Club, April 23rd. 



(|lt£ss (iosst^. 



Chess Literature.*— 'The chess clubs of this country almost universally 
disregard one of their most useful functions in neglecting to provide, for the 
use of members, copies of the periodical and other publications in which 
the great examples of play are recorded. The consequence of this is that many 
players, though connected with otherwise good clubs, have no opportunity of 
practising over thoroughly fine games, and authors, as well as publishers of 
chess works fail to derive any remunerative return for their undertakings. The 
proper remedy— or, at least, one of the most easily applied and most desiiiible 
remedies — is for chess clubs to make it a rule to take a certain number of copies 
of any journal, specially devoted to the game, which meets the approbation of 
the members. By this means they will, at a trifiiug cost to the members, give 



THE CHESS WOBLD. 69 

substantial help to the publication, and obtain, in return, for themselves what 
is positively necessary if they are ever to reach a high mark in chess practice. 
The tendency of late years has been for regular chess serials to decline, and for 
their places to be taken by chess colunms in newspapers or weekly magazines. 
These substitutes by no means compensate for the loss of the original, for it b 
not possible, in the space of a weekly column or half-column (the latter being 
the more frequent of the two) to give either a full account of important chess 
matches, or opportunity for such literary discussion as the game would 
naturally excite. We strongly recommend this matter to the consideration 
of the members of chess clubs, being persuaded that they cannot do anything 
that will advance the pleasure and interest of the game so much as they would 
by promoting among themselves and their competitors the study of chess 
literature. — Birmingham Journal, 

Mr. C. F. de Jaenisch's Analysis of the French Opening. — The 
Philadelphia Evening Bulletin contains the following observation on the above : 
** In the March number of the Chess World the famous analyist, Major Jaenisch, 
commences one of his admirable analytical articles. The first opening treated 
of is the French, and the Major treats it in his usual able manner, throwing 
fresh light on several doubtful points ; but we desire to call attention to the 
following line of play : 

1. P. to K. fourth P. to K. third 

2. P. to Q. fourth P. to Q. fourth 

3. Kt. to Q. B, third. 

This move, first introduced by Mr. Paulsen, is better, we believe, than the 
orthodox 3. P takes P; and, play as Black will, his Pawn at K. third confines 
his Q. Bishop. Suppose — 

3. B. to Q. Kt. fifth 

4. B. to Q. third Kt. to K. B. third 

5. B. to K. Kt. fifth . P. to K. R. third 

6. B. takes Kt Q. takes B. 

7. Kt to B. third Kt to B. third. 

8. Castles B. takes Kt 

9. P. takes B. P. takes P. 
10. B. takes P., &c. 

We are well aware that Black may vary his moves indefinitely, but the 
advantage in every case seems to remain with the first player. Black being 
unable to escape entirely from his confinement. "

requesting the reader to be good enough to correct them on the margin of his 

copy before proceeding farther with the analyses.

The close game, under its least objectionable form, and such as the second 
player usually empl(3ys, is distinguished from the open game only by the cir- 
cumstance that the Black Queen's Pawn is advanced but one square, and the 
Black King's Bishop can move only to the K.'s second, where his own action 
is very circumscribed, and where he restricts also the action of his Queen. As 
to the K.'s Pawn, it is there advanced two steps, and the Q.'s Bishop has the 
range of his diagonal, which sometimes leads to danger; because in being 
removed, this Bishop leaves undefended a very vulnerable point, the square of 
the Q. Kt.'s Pawn. On the other hand, this form of the close game has the 
great advantage of paralysing the attack of the K. Kt's d^btUy without 
depriving the second player of the chance of developing his game subsequently 
VOL. V. — NO. III. G 



i4 THB CHKm WORLD. 

hy the advance of the Q.'s Pawn, in the event of the adversary affording him, 
by some oversight, an opportunity. 

This form constitates, therefore^ if not the sorest, at least Che most con- 
venient defence to the opening in question. Here follows the model of it: —

At this moment it would seem that all embarassing consequences of the close 
opening for Black had disappeared, since he will be enabled to advance his 
Pawns to the attack of the side on which your King is Castled. Meanwhile, 
the anal(^ous attack which you will direct against his King's wing, will have 
more chance of success, owing to the ill disposition of his pieces on that side. 
Your game, taken altogether, deserves the preference, although to demonstrate 
its superiority, the game should be continued by correspondence by players of 
the first force.

JAPANESE CHESS. 

The following interesting article appeared lately in the PkUaddpkia Daily 
Bulletin:— 

All of our readers, of course, remember the visit of the Japanese Embassy 
to our shores eight years ago. On their arrival in this city we determined to 
convert them into a grand means of communication between Oriental and 
Occidental Chess, and we entertained strong hopes of embellishing our column 
with a Japanese Gambit, between two leading players of the Sho-hO'yey or 
some other Japanese Club. On being introduced into the quarters of the 
Embassy, we learned that the game was almost exclusively confined to the 
middle and lower ranks — a striking illustration of the semi-barbarism of these 
islanders. 



86 THB CHESS WOBLD. 

A set of Japanese (*hessmen, which we borrowed from a friend for the pur- 
pose, served as an interpreter, and very effectually too, for our strange guests 
immediately turned them out on the table and explained their use. In the 
absence of a board, they asked, in very intelligible English, for a piece of paper, 
and with marvellous rapidity laid out a ''board," writing the names of the 
pieces with great neatness in their appropriate squares. We prize this "auto- 
graph" Chess Board very highly as an interesting memento of the Embassy. 

The del^[ation that visited the Philadelphia Chess Club consisted of eight 
of the soldiers, each carrying his long, heavy sword in one hand, and some of 
them a light fan in the other. After a few minutes spent in salutations, two of 
them took their seats at the table which had been prepared for them, and the 
first game of Japanese Chess ever played in a Christian land (except such as 
may have been played within the seclusion of the Japanese quarters since the 
Embassy reached America) was b^im. 

The players, one of whom, Yamada Woomagen, was a fine looking man, 
drew for the first move, by tossing a Ho-hei^ or pawn, into the middle of the 
board {h la Mercantile Library fashion), the move being governed according to 
the side falling uppermost, and the game proceeded by the advance of the 
pawns, followed by the different pieces. 

The board and pieces being carefully marked and numbered, we had hoped 
to make some record of the game, but owing to the game's intricacies and 
peculiarities, and to the extraordinary rapidity of the play, we were soon hope- 
lessly bewildered. 

As the game progressed it was soon apparent, from the expression on the 
faces of their companions that one of the players was speedily gaining ground, 
and they all laughed heartily, but most good-naturedly, when the loser arose 
from the table, with the simple remark, ** He beat me " 

This game was not played out to a mate, and they accordingly agreed to 
play another game, the loser in the first game yielding his place to one of his 
companions, Sano Kanaye, who, from his greater proficiency in English, acted as 
interpreter to the party. He seemed to play a bold game, and in fifteen 
minutes he announced **Ote!" which signifies killed^ suggesting a significant 
analogy with the mat of the Persians, which bears the same meaning. 

Our visitors stated that there were in Jeddo seven "Chess masters," 
appointed by the Government, to instruct the people in the game, and that 
there were many Japanese, books on the game, some of which they promised to 
send to us when the Niagara returned from Japan. The Orientals, however, 
have poor memories. 

They described to us another Japanese game, somewhat similar to draughts, 
played on a board "nineteen squares each way," making altogether three 
hundred and sixty-one squares. 



TUX CHBSS WOBLD. 



81 



Some of them expressed much curiosity to learn our game, and took in 
some elementaiy lessons, which were given them, with remarkable aptness. 
They were much gratified by a present of a set of Chessmen and a copy of the 
** Handbook," which will probably become a. text book of the Japanese Chess 
College at some future day. 

After partaking of an ilnpromptu collation and recording their names on the 
register, our visitors took leave of us in high spirits and with many expressions 
of pleasure at their visit, leaving with us a most agreeable impression of their 
gentle, cheerful politeness, and their aptness and intelligence in acquiring and 
communicating knowledge. 

After this somewhat lengthy preface, we give a description of this remark- 
able game, partly from our own recollection and partly from the very interesting 
account of the game given in the report of the Japanese Expedition* The 
following diagram gives an accurate portraiture of the position of the pieces at 
the commencement of the game : —

This game is played by two persons, with forty pieces (twenty on either 
side), and upon a checquered board of eighty-one squares, nine every way. 
The board is of uniform color, though the alternate squares might be colored, 
as with us, for the sake of convenience. The pieces are of a uniform color, 
as they are used (at pleasure) by either party as his own, after being captured 



S3 THS CHS88 WORLD* 

from the adversaxy. They are of various sizes, are long and wedge-shaped, 
bong at the same time sharpened from side to side, in fronts and the name of 
each piece is inscribed upon it — ^both the original and the one assumed upon 
being reversed — (as below). Each player distinguishes his men by alwa3rs 
having the pointed and thin end forward. But they would be more readily 
known if the back parts of all were painted with some decided and striking 
color, as that part of his own men is seen by each player. The pieces are laid 
flat upon the board, and they capture by occupjring the places of the captured 
pieces. 

Names and powers of the pieces : — 

OhO'Shio (king)-- centre square, first row, has the same power as our 
king, and when ot^d ot checkmated, the game is lost. 

Kin-shio (gold, or chief counsellor) — ^upon the first row, and on either side of 
the Ohfhshio, Has the same power as the King, except that he cannot move 
diagonally backwards. A Kin-shio standing at K. a would be able to move to 
Q. 2, Q. 3, K. 3, K. B. 3, K. B. 2, and E. square ; but not to Q. square and 
K. B. square. It is the only piece, the- Oho-shio^ of course, excepted, that can 
neither be reversed or acquire different power. 

All the following pieces and Pawns may be reversed (at the option of the 
player) when they move to or from any square in any of the adversary's first 
three rows, and they do hereby acquire different powers, as well as different 
names. 

Gin-shio (silver or sub-counsellor) — upon the first row, and on each square 
next outside the Kin-shio^ has the same power as the King, except that he 
cannot move directly to either side or directly backward. A Gin^hio standing 
at K. 2, would be able to move to Q. 3, E. 3, K. B. 3, Q. square, and K. B. 
square ; but not to Q. 2, K. B. 2, or K. square. When reversed or turned 
over, he becomes the Gin-narikin^ and acquires all the powers (and those only) 
of the Kin-shio. 

Kiema (flying-horse) — ^upon the first row, and on each square next outside 
the Gin-shiOf has the same move as our Knight, except that it is strictly 
confined to two squares forward and one laterally. A Kiema standing at E. 3, 
would be able to move to E. B. 5 and Q. 5 ; but not to E. Et. 4, E. Kt. 2, 
E. B. square,, Q. square, Q. B. 2, and Q. B. 4. From this it will be easily 
seen that the Kiema can have but four moves during the game, as the fourth 
move would carry him to the extremity of the board. When reversed, it 
becomes a Kiema-tutri-kin^ with all powers (and those only) of the Kin-shio, 

Kioshia (fragrant chariot) — apon each comer square, first row, has the same 
move as our Rook, except that it is strictly limited to a forward movement, bat 
that may be any number of steps. Thus a Kioshia^ by moving but one step at 
a time, may make eight moves in a game, but by leaping over a square its 



THX CHS88 WOBLD. 83' 

nnmber of mores becomes considerably reduced ; and, indeed, it may leap its 
whole file at once, providing the passage is clear. When it is reversed, it 
becomes a KtO'shia-nari-kin^ with all the powers (and those only) of the 
Kin-shio, 

His-hia (flying chariot) — on the second square, second row, right side of 
the board, analogous to our E. Et. 2, has the entire power of our Rook, and 
when reversed it assumes the name of Rioko (the dragon), and acquires in 
addition to its former power, the movements of the King. 

Kakuko (the horn) — on the second square, second row, left side of the 
board, analogous to our Q. Kt. 2, has the entire power of our Bishop^ and 
when reversed assumes the title of Riome (the dragpness), and acquires, in 
addition to its former moves, all those of the King. 

Ho-hd (the soldiery) — on all the nine squares of the third row. The 
Ho'hei (called Ho for short) moves like our Pawn, but never more than one 
step at a time ; but, unlike our Pawn, it captures in the same direction that it 
moves. Thus a /T^^ at E. 3 would be able to capture an inimical Ho at K. 4 ; 
when reversed it becomes a Ho-nari-kin^ and acquires all the powers of the 
Kin'skio. 

Gin-nari'kin — ^The name assumed by the Gin-shio when reversed, has the 
same power as a Kin-shio. 

Kiema-nari-kin — ^The name assumed by the Kiema when reversed, has the 
same power as a JCin-shio. 

JCioshia-nari-kin — The assumed name of the Kioshia when reversed, has 
the same power as the Kin-shio, 

Rioho (the dragon) — ^The assumed title of the Hishia when reversed, has 
the powers of the Eing and Rook combined. A Rioho^ standing at E. 4,. 
would, in addition to the legitimate Rook moves, be able to go to Q. 5, E. B. 
5. Q. 3. and K. B. 3. 

Riome (the dragoness) — ^The title assumed by the Kahuko when reversed, 
has the combined moves of the King and Bishop. A Riome standing at K. 4, 
would, in addition to the legitimate Bishop's moves, be able to go to K. 5, 
K. B. 4, K. 3, and Q. 4. 

Ho-nari'hin — ^The name assumed by any Ho or Fawn, on its being 
reversed, has the same powers as a Kin-shio, 

Besides the preceding moves and powers, any Piece or Pawn which has 
been captured may be replaced upon the board at the discretion of the player, 
as follows, viz. :-^When it is his move, instead of moving one of his own men, 
he can replace any one of the captured pieces of his adversary upon any vacant 
square whatever, observing to keep that side up to which it was originally 
entitled, ue,^ a^ptured Gin-shio he uses as a Gin-shio^ a captured Kiema as a 
Kiema^ ftc, &c., but he puts it on the board as his own piece, and being, of 



84 THB CHESS WOBLD. 

course, subject to reversal if it be caught moving to ox from any square in the 
enemy's country. And the player must furthermore observe, that he cannot 
replace a Ho on any column on which there is one of his own ; in other words, 
he cannot double a Ho, We may state, in conclusion, that no piece can pass 
over the head of any other piece, the Kiema alone excepted. 

Since writing the above, we learn that it is to the indefatigable research and 
persevering industry of Dr. Daniel S. Green, Fleet Suigeon of the Japanese 
Elxpedition, that we are indebted for the very clear and minutely descriptive 
account of the game in the report of the expedition. 



FRENCH CHESS LITERATURE. 

The late Congress in Paris appears to have had the good effect of stimulating 
attention there to Chess ; and as a result, France promises to be singularly 
fruitful this season in the production of works upon the game. In addition to 
the Book of the Congress, and a second volume of the second edition of the 
Stratigie Raisonie des Otwerturesy we have a new monthly magazine, the. 
PhUidarien^ of which we shall find occasion to speak hereafter, and an* 
announced edition of the Analyse du Jeu des Echecs of Philidor, by Mr. 
C. Sanson. As this work is one which appears to have been conceived 
in a right spirit, and if conscienciously executed will be a valuable contribution 
to the literature of Chess, we must allow its projector space to say what he has 
to say concerning it 

The following is an abstract of his Atns aux Amateurs d^ Echecs on the 
subject : — 

*' The edition of Philidor which i^re are about to offer to the public, is 
intended to fill a gap in Chess biblic^japhy. The only French edition of this 
master's works is incomplete, mutilated, often inexact, and not nearly so good 
as the editions published in England. 

" We have successively studied the different editions in all languages, and 
have collected the facts and documents which appertain as much to the life of 
Pliilidor as his theoretical analyses and games. In these respects we can affirm 
that nothing so complete will have appeared on the subject in any countxy. 
The typographical labour, confided to an editor skilful and intelligent, will be 
faithfully performed. The name of Mr. Lebigre-Duquesne is a guarantee for 
us with the public for the perfection of this portion of the labour. 

*' Whatever the celebrity which is attached to the name of Philidor, it must 
be admitted that since his time the science has made immense progress. The 



TfiLB CHESS WORLD. B5* 

openings have been methodically classified, and some of them reduced 
to an accurate demonstration. The defence known as the Philidor Defence^ 
vigorously attacked by two contemporary celebrities — ^Messrs. Morphy and 
De Riviere — although it resisted the profound examination made of it by these 
masters, is now rejected in those great International Tourneys which are 
periodically occurring in the Chess world. 

•* Many openings now in use were unknown to him — ^the EvanS'Gamhit^ 
the Scotch' Gambit^ and a host of othors upon which volumes might be written. 
His most glorious title to consideration, independently of having been the 
strongest player of his time, consists in his enunciation of a unique principle, 
and makings it the basis of his analysis; — 

* ** *The Pawns are the Soul of Chess^ • • • • im^ qjj ^Jj^j^ good or 
bad arrangement depends entirely the gain or loss of the game.' 

" We may criticize this doctrine as too absolute, but we must acknowledge 
that Chess is indebted to it, in a great measure, fbr the progress it has made. 
It is an indisputable axiom that the consolidation of the Pawns in the centre of 
the Chess board leads to a manifest superiority of position. In the Evans- 
Gambit, for example, they give an irresistible attack. 

'* As to end-games, in those of the Bishop and Rook against Rook : of the 
Queen against Rook and one Pawn ; the analyses of Philidor are law to 
.this day. 

" To complete his works by works which were worthy to be his is the 
task we have undertaken. The variations which we shall give as complement, 
or as rectification of those by Philidor, will be derived from the most celebrated 
contemporary authorities. All countries have been put under contribution, so 
that giving the work of the Master, our work may be considered, thanks to 
the co-operation of his disciples, as a treatise ' in extenso ' on the matter. 

•* The book is ta be divided into two parts. The first will contain the 
introduction or mechanism of the game, its principles, its maxims, and its 
counsels ; the biography of Philidor, augmented by hitherto unpublished par- 
ticulars ; his analyses of Chess, followed by variations, acknowledged to be the 
best, printed in different type from that of the text to distinguish one from the 
other ; and his games, 6.6 in nimiber, with those of contemporaries, all illus- 
trated by notes and diagrams. 

'* The second will present an analysis of the openings unknown to Fhilidor,- 
accompanied by the most remarkable games of La Bourdonnais and Morphy ; 
all the analyses of end-games which have struck us as most noticeable in 
modem treatises, and a collection of fine games at odds.'*
