THE WEST GERMAN CHESS LEAGUE. 

Thb Rhenish Chess Congress, with the usual festiYities» games and problem 
tournaments, &c., will be held this year in Aiz-la-ChapeUe, on the 2nd, 3rd, 
and 4th of August 

The West German Chess Association, established in the year 186 1, by the 
union of other Chess Clubs from the Rhenish provinces and Westphalia, aims 
at promoting the spread of the knowledge and practice of Chess ; and considers 
the most appropriate means for so doing to be the social meetings of the 
members, combined with tournaments. There will, therefore, be held yearly 
a chief assembly under the name of the ** Rhenish Chess Congress," of which 
persons can become members by the small annual contribution of a thaler. 

The names of those proposing to join the Club may be sent in to the 
undersigned members of the committee. 

The venerable and ancient, but friendly town of Aix-la-Chapelle — ^femous 
for its coronations and its congresses— will have the honour and pleasure of 
seeing this Chess Congress held within its walls. 

Among the numerous visitors who flock hither from all countries, there will 
be found many friends of Chess ; and we may express a well founded hope 
that the princes of Chess, Paul Morphy, A. Anderssen, and Louis Paulsen, 
beade many others, will honour the Congress by their presence. Moreover, 
we cherish the expectation that, at this time, there wiU be a larger ntmiber of 
persons in Aix-la-Chapelle, who though not engaged in the contests, will enrol 
themselves as members of the West German Chess Club, and thereby enable 
the committee to celebrate this festival, now held for the first time in this town, 
in a worthy manner. 

The preliminary programme for the seventh chief assembly is as follows : —

of ' ' The Erholung:" Report of former proceedings : A lottery for the Rhenish 
High Tournament : Meeting of Members in Committee : Election of new 
Members : Concert and re-union in the adjacent ' ' Elisengarten, " and afterwards 
a public supper.

THB CHESS WOBLD. 105 

Book of the Chbss Conorbbs at Paris in 1867. — Subscribers and 
others interested in the French Tournament of last year will be gratified to 
learn that the volume conmiemoratiye of that great Chess event is nearly 
ready for publication. The whole of the games played in the contest for 
the Emperor's Prize, as well as those of the lesser matches in the Tourney, 
are ^eady printed. The remainder of the work, consisting of the Chess 
Problems — a very fine collection, we are told — and the Memoir of the 
Congress, fi*om the pen of M. Fery d'Esclands, is passing through the 
press ; and the complete book may be expected to be issued by the end 
of the present month. 

Photographic Portraits of Chess Celebrities. — ^Among the 
novelties in photography may be mentioned a card representing a chess 
board, every square of which has the " picture in little" of a Chess nota- 
bility famous for his play or his problems. The idea is Mr. S. Lloyd's, 
but in the specimen sent to us from New York the conception is better 
than the execution. We believe, however, it is intended to produce tha 
" Chess Champions" on a larger scale, and then the arrangement of the 
heads will probably be better, and by the judicious omission of some 
portraits, room will be. found for those of players more generally known. 



(Sbituarg. 



The last number of CTieas StraUgie announces the death of the Duke 
de Caraman, a zealous promoter of the late Chess Congress in Paris, and 
one of the most enlightened and liberal patrons of the game which France 
possessed. From the American Chess organs we have the ill-tidings, too, 
of the demise of Mr. Denis Julien, a gentleman well known and respected 
in the Chess society of Kew York ; and of Mr. J. C. Warner, formerly 
Chess editor of Wilkes' SpirU of the Times, The Leader , The Sunday Press, 
Sfc. Mr. Warner Jiad gone but lately to Panama as correspondent of the 
New York Tribune, and fell a victim, it is said, to the insalubrity of t^e 
climate.

ON CHESS. 
By Von Herrn Gustav ,Ullricht. 



The CAess Zeitungy a short time ago, contained a philippic against various 
bad habits of Chess-players, from the sharp sarcastic pen of Herrn Herm. 
Ilirschbach. We sincerely rejoiced when we read those censures, regretting, 
nevertheless, the omission of the mention of many improprieties, which, with 
even more reason, might have been exposed, and which we could have con- 
demned yet more strongly. We refer, in the first place, to the intrusive remarks 
of bystanders, and to the fact of their sometimes, in the eagerness of their 
observations, even touching a piece. Such practices, if seen only among 
inferior players or those who frequent coffee-houses, are, perhaps, unimportant, 
and may only excite a smile ; but in those localities Chess is not played — ^but 
played with. In the rules of Chess, nothing is said of such habits, because it 
is certainly taken as a matter of course that those not sharing in the game, 
whatever it may be called, and no matter whether it be played for money or 
for honour, will avoid interrupting its course by thoughtless talking. The 
custom of observing silence was not merely appropriate to the olden time, and 
in these more cultivated days surely there can be no necessity to change it. 
What would happen to such self-appointed counsellors if they offered their 
advice in like manner to Card-players ? Possibly some one may exclaim, 
•* Yes, friend, but that is another thing." Why? In the first place. Chess is 
a game of skill, and if any one not participating in the game makes inconsiderate 
remarks, his observations will have more or less influence on the course of the 
game, for the advantage or disadvantage of one or other of the players. In 
Card playing the same thing takes place. The greatest vexation is caused, 
however, when perhaps only in consequence of a thoughtless remark* a game 
VOL. V. — ^NO. IV. K 



lii THB CHSSS WOELD. 

is lost : not only on account of the money at stake, but because the player 
considers it a point of honour to win the game ; the fact of holding the prize 
impUes genius, talent, or at the least, a good memory on the part of the winner: 
while of the loser the contrary is presupposed. Many a Chess-player will 
remark that counsels applied at the right time will have a salutary influence. 
Quite right ; but then it makes one at best only Uke a whist-player. At the 
end of the game, they interchange their ideas about this or that different course, 
and how it might have turned out if Jones or Brown had taken such or such a 
lead, or followed up so or so. Then the opinion of any spectator who understands 
the game would be accepted with pleasure. On the contrary, to speak during 
the progress of a game would involve its hasty conclusion, — as the player 
would put t(^ther his cards, and fling them on the table, if not in the face of 
the unfortunate adviser. Again, who speaks, for instance, during a clattering 
game of dominoes ? Here also talking would certainly be taken amiss ; for 
though a game of dominoes is in itself noisy, it yet requires some amount of 
thoughtful attention. One excuse frequently urged by talkers is, that the game 
is not being played for money; and again, they say, ">\hat does it matter if 
for once a game is lost through a little talking?" It is true that mostly they 
do not play for money ; the greater number of players seek only satisfaction, 
or, as they say, honour, in winning. a game — or even may declare that they 
esteem Chess far too noble to be necessarily played for money, and that it is 
chiefly an entertainment, and so forth. We had when we resolved to write 
this article, nothing in view but the promotion of the progress of Chess-play. 
Whoever has anything beyond entertainment in view, must think deeper; as 
with a game of Six and Sixty, or Dominoes, to him who wishes merely to 
show he understands the moves, it will certainly be of no great consequence if 
there is interference by talk or the men are touched ; generally, at all events 
in such play, it is a mere accident who wins the game, and it becomes more 
lively and entertaining for such players. But to him who joins a Club for the 
purpose of studying Chess, the interference of b3rstanders must be a horror, as 
it hinders him from making progress. A Chess player who wishes by-and-bye 
to perform something creditable, must in the first place give free play to his 
men: secondly, he must after his antagonist's move, above all things consider 
what the other may have intended, if he has made up his mind, and how best 
he can defeat that intention. For this, he has to calculate various moves ; at 
the second or third next move it will be seen that his antagonist followed up a 
well-concealed intention, very different from what he had expected ; he has 
now to resort to other dispositions to make up for lost time, and again he has 
to contemplate other variations, and so on. Now it happens that our kind 
neighbour on the right does not only look on complacently and smilingly, but, 
not able to keep his secret he declares he would have done so and so. A 



THS CHBSS WOSLD. 115 

second neighbour, however, says, ** Much better for Black if he had played 
quite differently, for then it would have happened — " so, or so. Another, 
** But bless me I what a splendid move White has now I Black has hopelessly 
lost:" — and so on. If a player is not totally deaf, how is it possible for him to 
make a calculation ? Wherefore it is evident that remarks made during the 
course of a game, and while the game goes on — especially if there be money 
at stake, are always disturbing; on the one side they betray ideas to the 
antagonist : on the other hand, such pieces of advice from bystanders are of no 
value, for though it often happens that lookers on can see this or that as the 
game proceeds, because they have their own ideas which they persistently 
cherish, so on the other hand the players themselves have generally gone much 
deeper into the science of the game, and have a more profound understanding 
of their own moves than the bystanders can possibly attain. Very often the 
spectators overlook a Pawn covered by a larger piece, and on this error they 
will found an ingenious combination, and treat the others to it. — '* They cannot 
see the forest, for trees." Most Chess-players who are not themselves quite 
clear as to what would be a correct and appropriate opening, or how they can 
best carry on the game, may be indifferent to the interference of the bystanders, 
for they also combine and calculate — they do so more superficially. A player, 
for instance, has made what he calls a place: consequently makes a fUghty 
move, 'and only proceeds to make a real calculation when his antagonist has 
taken an important step; he is not much disturbed, but he will never be equal 
to a correct and well-considered game. It is necessary before a player moves, 
that he should have looked over the possible moves of his opponent, and 
calculated against the most likely. Whoever is made uncomfortable by close 
attention to a game, let him not say he is playing Chess ; he only moves the 
men — ^and let him move ever so ingeniously, he makes no progress, although 
he may admit that he has the pleasure he sought, and the entertainment he 
aimed at There are players who have played with each other for years, and 
one may be blessed with more talent than the other, but we shall' find that he 
will not win many more games than his antagonist. As it is said, ''Chance 
will win. " Even those who are endowed with real talent for Chess-playing, if 
they always choose weaker players than themselves, will not make much 
prc^ess in spite of their talent. They who choose worse players than themselves 
are always hunting afler brilliant moves, and endeavouring to gain over their 
opponent to make ingenious combinations, and to lead him on till he altogether 
loses the thread of his own thought. To sum up, let us remark that all 
Chess-players who belong to a Club should endeavour to abstain from such 
practices, or the use of their membership would be lost. One should remember 
that besides the entertaining moments it grants, it is in principle an educating 
institution for our noble game, and amusement is secondary. Berathung's 

K 2 



116 THB CHESS WORLD. 

partien, Wett Kampfe, general enquiries after good openings, and interesting 
conclusions; now and then, after a serious game, one calls for Eosaken or 
Husaren partie, for the sake of practice, and in order to quicken the sight: 
All these things should, and can be done. 

In conclusion, we refer once more to the subject of playing for money. 
Many Chess friends, in mooting this question will at once put their veto on it; 
considering the stand-point which Chess takes among all other games, they 
will say, ''Such a noble, generous game, and money 1 how can they agree?" 
We ourselves, we may ren^ark in passing, never play for money, and would 
explicitly state that we do not defend the -custom on ordinary occasions; but it 
cannot be contested that this game would on the average have more and better 
players if this practice had been recommended, even if only a very small stake 
were played for. Many years ago, Algaier was a person of importance in 
Vienna. At the same time he constituted himself a teacher of Chess-play ; and 
we believe from the written reports which we have read, that his pupils played 
almost exclusively for money: for that master himself, whether on principle or 
not, we cannot say, never played otherwise than for money. It was customary 
in Vienna, in those games for every one to put down Zwanzig Kreuzer, but 
M. Algaier played for nothing less than Dukaten or Louis d'orchen. He may 
have done a good business, but the consequence was that his pupils took more 
pains to enter into the finesses of the game, and so many became really good 
players. We do not defend the practice, but if it were accepted the con- 
sequences would be two-fold. First, those counsellors of whom we have been 
writing, if they were sitting at the board themselves, would as little allow 
talking as the Card-players alluded to: and secondly, the number of true 
Chess-players would be considerably increased.

THE KING'S BISHOPS GAMBIT. 

The modern classical* defence to the King's Bishop's Gambit, represented 
jy the moves — 

1. P. to K. fourth P. to K. fourth 

2. P. to K. B. fourth P. takes P. 

8. B. to Q. B. fourth j^ to K. R. fifth (check) 

4. K. to B square P. to K. Kt. fourth 

5. Kt. to Q. B. thh-d B. to K Kt. second 

has for many years received so much attention analytically, and enjoyed 

such an acknowledged preference over the various other modes of defence 

afforded by this fine opening, that it now seems somewhat out of date 

to challenge its validity. The ingenious variation of 6. P. to K. Kt third, 

introduced at this juncture by the celebrated McDonnell, as the initial 

move in the attack which bears his name, threatened by its success, 

for a time, to shake the hitherto recognised solidity of the classical 

defence, but subsequent analysis has clearly demonstrated that, cu origi- 

naJdy conducted^ it can be satis&ctorily opposed in more ways than one. 

This attack, however, it appears to me, is susceptible of being greatly 

Improved and strengthened ; to such a degree indeed, as, in my opinion, 

to overturn any defence that may be adopted against it. And this notion 

of the unsatisfactory character of the opening moves in the regular 

defence to the King's Bishop's Gambit, seemks to be implied by the recent 

P. to K. fourth 

general abandonment of it in favour of that arising from 1. 

P. to K. fourth 
P. to K. B. fourth B. to Q. B. fourth B. takes P. 

2. 3. 4. &C. , a mode 

P. takes P. P. to Q. fourth K. Kt to B. thmi 

of play now much in vogue, which seems to leave Black with fully the 

better game. 

In the subjoined attempt to support the soundness of the McDonnell 

attack when modified as I propose, and, as a consequence, to shew that 

the principle of the old defence is unsound, the reader is requested to note 

that many variations leading to unfavorable results for the second player, 

have, after assurance of their worthlessness, been dismissed or pui*posely 

withheld, in order to take full advantage of the space allotted to me. 

G. B. PRASER. 
Dundee, Jfay, 1868.

With the superior game.)

GAMES. 



The five immediately following games were played in the Great 
Toumay for the Emperor's Prize, and, with the notes by Messrs. 
Neumann and De Riviere, are taken from the shortly to be published 
Book of the Paris Cheas Congress. 

Game ZXXI. 

Between Messrs. 8. Loyd and Winaw6^ 
(Paroff^8 Defence.) 
WTiite, (Mr. Lloyd.) Blach. (Mr. Winawere.) 



1. P. to K. fourth 

2. Kt to K. B. third 

3. Et takes P.

[There are few better openings than this fine old classical Qiuooo Piano, It does 
not allow of a vigorous attack at the outset, but it enables each player to bring his 
pieces into the field in battle order, and, when the opponents are well matched, it 
generally leads to an interesting contest. We should be pleased to see it more fre- 
quently adopted.]

Gbbss Toubnament at Adelaide, South Australia. — The City 
of Adelaide Chess Club, under the Presidency of W. Blyth, M.P., wo are 
glad to learn, is very flourishing, and is at present actively engaged in 
the completion of a Handicap Tourney began a few months back. Of 
this contest we And the following particulars in the Adelaide Observer^ a 
weekly paper which, much to its credit, devotes a column to the promo- 
tion of the game: — ^^For some time past active exertions have been 
made by a few of the members of the City of Adelaide Club to hold a 
tournament upon the same principle as that of the Melbourne tournament 
of 1866 and 1887. The Secretary of the old Chess Club (Mr. J. Mann), 
having obtained the sanction of the members, consented to devote the 
balance of funds, two prizes to be fought for in the tournament, on con- 
dition that all the members of the Old Adelaide Chess Club should be 
allowed to compete, and that one of those prizes, which should rank as 
the second, should only be given to a member of the Old Club. A con- 
siderable number of players having signified their assent to take part in 
the tournament by signing a document to that effect, a general meeting 
was convened for Monday evening, February 24th, at the Club Room, 
King William Street, for the purpose of making the final arrangements 
for the tourney. It was decided on that occasion that, in accordance 
with the plan adopted in a recent tournament, both in the old hemisphere 
nd the new, each player should play one game, exclusive of draws, with 
eveiy other player, until the circle is completed. A Committee was 
appointed, consisting of Messrs A. L. FuUarton, J. Mann, and H. Char- 
lick, for the purpose of handicapping the players and conducting the 



Tlllfi CIIU/^S WORLD. 145 

general business of the tournament. Mr. H. Gharlick was also appointed 
TreasvLTeT, pro tern, &nd empowered to collect the entrance-fees. "With 
regard to the regulations for play, it was decided that the rules laid down 
in Staunton^s Handbook should be strictly adhered to, and that all 
disputes should be referred to the Committee; that the players should 
strictly play in accordance with the terms imposed on the several classes 
by the Handicapping Committee; that in case of a breach of tliis stipula- 
tion another game would have to be played between the two competitors ; 
that in all cases where practicable the games shoull be played at the 
Club Room ; that the players should be left to arrange amongst them- 
selves as to the time of playing, though, with reference to this point, a 
hope was expressed that they would make every effort to play as fre- 
quently as possible, so as not to protract the tournament; that in the 
event of ties in the highest aggrep:ate scores, both players should play a 
match together of three games ; that the winner of each game should as 
quickly as possible communicate the result to one of the members of the 
Committee, in writing, and mention the terms upon which it was played, 
so that the Committee could ascertain if it was conducted in accordance 
with the classified divisions. It was understood that the prizes would not 
be given in money, but in suitable works, &c. As the Handicapping 
Committee had classified the competitors, it was decided that they 
could commence forthwith. The Handicapping Committee completed 
their somewhat invidious task, and classified the players as follows: — 
First class to give K. B. P. and move to the second class; Knight to 
the third class, and Rook to the fourth class ; the second class to give 
K. B. P. and move to the third class, and Knight to the fourth class; 
the third class to give K. B. P. and move to the fourth class. First 
Class— Messrs. R. B. Hale, J. Mann, W. J. Fullarton, H. Charlick, 
T. D. Sison, W. D. Scott, R. M. Steele, and H L. Thwaites. Second 
Class— Messrs. A. McLeod, A L. Fullarton, W. D. Webb, D. Mahncke, 
and — Haussen . Third Class— Messrs. J. Hodgkiss, M. L.C. , H. Noltenius, 
M M. Finnis, C. Young, J. Ey, and W. B. Dawes. Fourth Class— 
W. A. Cawthorne, A Gaedechens, A Sommers, J. MacQeorge, and 
(). Zeigler." In our next, we shall endeavour to give one or two of the 
games, a number of which have reached us late in the month. 

North German Chess Association. — The first meeting of a Chess 
Congress under this title took place at Hamburg during the Whitsuntide 
Holidays It is reported to have been very numerously attended. One 
interesting feature in connexion with it was an exhibition of blindfold 
play by Mr. C. Shallopp, who conducted eight games simultaneously and 
without a Chess board ; another was the dispatch of a complimentary 



146 THE CHESS WORLD. 

greeting by Telegram from the whole assembly to Professor Anderasen 
at Breslaw. 

The West German Caess Association. — The following notice 
regarding this Meeting has been received :— " The Committee of the 
Aachener Schach- Verbin have invited a few eminent Chess Masters 
and many Amateurs to countenance by their presence the Congress of 
the West German Chess League, to be held at Aix-la-Chapelle, on the 1st 
of August next, and the three following days ; and of these, Messrs. 
Anderssen, Paulsen, Max-Lange, and Kolisch, are cx)nfidently expected." 
The Committee venture to recommend to the patronage of visitors, 
Frank's Hotel, Belle Vue, Hayer's Hotel, and Qiesen's Tavera, both for 
the goodness of the accommodation and for the moderation of their tariff. 

West Yorkshire Chess Association. — The Thirteenth Annual 
Meeting of the West Yorkshire Chess Association was held at the 
AthenaBum, Sheffield, a few days since. In the first-class tournament, 
the Rev. A. B. Skipworth and Mr. Young, having defeated their respec- 
. live opponents in the first games, competed together for the prize, and the 
deciding game was won by the former gentleman. For the second-class 
prize there were eight competitors, and the first rounds resulted as 
follows: — Mr. Ammelburg beat Mr. Hague, Mr. Shuttleworth beat Mr. 
Ellis, Mr. Fieldsend beat Mr. Winterbottom, and Mr. Finlinson beat 
Mr. Jeffrey. Then Mr. Shuttleworth played Mr. Fieldsend, and on the 
former proving victorious, he was awarded half the prize, the other half 
being divided between Mr. Ammelburg and Mr. Finlinson, who did not 
finish their decidin&c game. In the third-class tournament, Mr. Allan beat 
Mr. Edwards, as Mr. Heselton did Mi*. Thompson; then, on«Mr. Allan and 
Mr. Heselton being matched agiiinst each other, Mr. Allan proved vic- 
torious, and consequently took the prize for that class. The President, in 
the name of the Sheffield players, cordially welcomed all present, and in 
the course of his address, mentioned that it was intended to offer a prize 
to be competed for during the ensuing season for amateurs. The Rev. 
A. B. Skipworth tendered the thanks of the visitors for the hearty and 
kind reception which they had met with at the hands of the Sheffield Club. 
It was then decided to hold the next annual meeting of the Association 
at Huddersfield. Mr. Skipworth afterwards brought imder the notice 
of the meeting the fact that a new Association had been started at York, 
and he wished to ascertain the feeling of the members of the West 
Yorkshire Association with regard to the name that had been chosen, as 
it had been intimated that some dissatisfaction had been caused by the 
similarity of title. The general opinion of those present seemed to be 
against the new Association having a name so similar to that of the old 



THE CIIKSS WOBT.D. 



147 



one given to it ; and Mr. Skipworth promised to bring the matter before 
the Association with a view of suggesting an alteration. A vote of thanks 
was heartily accorded to the Sheffield Committee who had made the 
arrangements for the present meeting. Play w;is tlicn resumed, and 
contiuued till late in the evening.

A. 

5. Kt. takes P.* 

This move has hitherto received very little attention, and it was therefore 
necessary to commence the analysis of it almost ab origine. We have en- 
deavoured, as much as possible, to reduce to system the variations springing 
from it, and to present its multifarious combinations in as simple a form as its 
importance would permit. It will be seen that 5. Kt. takes P. possesses the 
advantage of subjecting Black to a very irregular and complicated line of 
defence, entirely different to the combinations which arise from 5. Q. takes P. 
The attack springing from 5, Kt. takes P. has not, perhaps, been proved to 
acquire a decisive advantage ; but the course of the second player is so involved 
that the slightest error would give White the superiority.

7. Castles 

Messrs. Morphy and De Riviere give 7. P. to K. B. fourth at this point, 
and decide in favour of White. We believe that if they had continued their 
analysis they would have agreed with us that 7. P. to K. B. fourth is, in reality, 
bad play. (See a.)

7. P. to Q. B. fourth 

Although irr^;ular, and contrary to the principle that the Q. B. Pawn should 

not be played two steps, leaving the Q. Pawn unsupported in the rear, this 

advance is generally considered indispensable at this point. The commanding 

position occupied by the King's Knight greatly restrains the movements of the 

Black pieces, whilst by driving him away, Black at least gains a move.

Bilguer's Handbook, p. 90., note 2.), that Black should have played his 

Queen's Bishop's Pawn earlier. As this must signify that the Q. B. Pawn 

ought to have been advanced at the sixth or even the fifth move in the opening, 

we have thought it necessary to test the suggestion of the celebrated German 

writer under the sub-variations, already cited, of b and e. But their result

K. B. third, or lastly, F. to K. B. fourth, as in the present ^ame. This move gives 
in our opinion a slight advantage to the second player."]

Rapid Bay, South Australia, 

Mm, 1868. 

To THE Editors op " The Chess World." 

Dear Sirs, — I Dotice that Mr. Pavitt has referred to my last letter to 
you in his communication to you (pa^e 421, Vol. Ill), and promises a few 
remarks in your next issue. They do not appear, however, in your 
March number, and as I do not think it possible to assail the proofs I 
gave in my letter in your publication of January, 1868, that Mr. Pavitt's 
suggestion (in answer to my defence in the Mortimer attack) is unsound, 
I conclude that he will not revert to the subject, and will therefore simply 
give the revvM, which of course is all that Chess-players care for. I have 
much respect for Mr. Pavitt, both for his excellent problems, and his 
suggestions on my defence ; for, as the proposer of a new defence in a 
difficult attack, I certainly have felt obliged to those who have " argued 
the point," and I trust, in conclusion, that Mr. Pavitt feels no annoyance 
because I have maintained my own views and answered his objection, as 
'I believe, fully. 

The result, theQ, of my examination of the Mortimer attack is, that I 
still hold to the views I have proposed in your issue of April, 1867, page 
53, and that I think the move I suggest, of 22. B. to Q. B. sixth is calcu- 
lated to give the second player as good, if not a better game than the 
attacking player. I believe the best method of continuing the attack to 
be 23. Q. Kt takes B., and certainly not 23. B. to K. R. sixth (Mr. Pavitt's 
move), which, as proved in my communication to you of January last, is 
pretty sure to lose the game for the first player. 

I remain, dear Sirs, 

Yours truly, 

EDWARD J. CATLOW.

THE BRITISH CHESS ASSOCIATION. 
To THE Editors of ** The Chess World." 

Gentlemen, — Some months ago I invited the attention of Chessplayers, 
through the medium of your excdilent magazine, to a series of scandalous 
blunders in the adjudication of certain Problem prizes. Unfortunately the men 
who had the charge of that tourney are invested, or have invested themselves, 
with the management of the only Chess organization we have that pretends to 
be national, the British Chess Association. What then, it may and should be 
asked, are the aims of that Association, and by what means and with what 
success does it prosecute them ? Occupying the ground, and by its existence 
excluding others, does it discharge the functions of an English institution? 
Were the disgraceful proceedings in the Problem Tourney characteristic or 
exceptional? Does it deserve the support or the reprobation of British 
players? These are questions of paramount importance. The Committee's 
Annual Report has just been issued, the Annual General Meeting has been 
held ; and the present seems, therefore, a fitting occasion for submitting infor- 
mation which may aid your readers in determining how the questions should be 
answered. 

From the time of the London Congress in 1862 (which was not so very 
great a success as it was held out to be) down to 1866, the Committee of the 



184 THE GUESS WORLD. 

Association did next to nothing. They published a f>ook, it is true, a chronicle 
of their doughty deeds, and (subordinately) an exposition of the policy which 
such an institution should pursue ; but which, for no better reason than that 
** it has of late become a matter of great difficulty to put the machinery in 
motion," they for four long years ignored. Two years ago the Association was 
"placed on a permanent basis, with its constitution defined, and a line oi 
action clearly marked out," and now we have the Committee's own account of 
what they have accomplished during their term of office. In 1866 a meeting 
was held in London, and last year there was a meeting at Dundee. The 
principal feature of the London Meeting was ** the institution of a Grand 
Challenge Cup as the guerdon of British championship in Chess." The 
Managing Committee would seem to have been not so much shamed as 
frightened into this concession (such as it is) to the reasonable demand that the 
British Chess Association should do something for British Chess. A "Grand 
Tournament" could not be got up, but there was a "Handicap Tourney," a 
kind of contest that can never be very satisfactory ; for, beyond the limits of 
a coterie, where every one knows the play of every one else, how is fair 
handicapping possible ? There was also a Problem Tourney, conducted I have 
told how, and there was a banquet. The Meeting at Dundee was a good one, 
but would certainly have been better if the Association had had nothing to do 
with it, or, I should rather say, if it had had nothing to do with the Associa- 
tion. The success of the meeting is to be credited solely to the local club, 
acting under the energetic leadership of Mr. G. B. Fraser. 

Financially, the Association is in a very languishing state. The annual 
subscriptions amounted last year to £^(^ i8s. This was increased by special 
donations to ;£^ii4 8s., the Dundee Chess Club contributing and collecting 
nearly the same sum. The Manager, for services, the precise nature of which 
kis unknown to me, draws about one third of the amount of the annual sub- 
scriptions as salary. To the advancement of Chess is devoted the sum of 
;f 40, and to management, jf 45 5s. 6d. A Book of Transactions was promised, 
also a Chess Directory ; but the first has been senselessly planned on a scale 
beyond the Committee's means, and neither is forthcoming. One obvious 
method of increasing their funds does not seem to suggest irself to the Com- 
mittee : I mean the exacting of subscriptions from their fellows in office, the 
members of the General Committee of the Association. I fear that Com- 
mittee is a sham, an array of names published for the purpose of obtaining 
subscriptions. About one-half (24 out of 50) do not subscribe, and are there- 
fore not even members of the Association, Is it with the consent of Messrs. 
Barnes, Boden, Owen, Staunton, &c., that the Managing Committee make use 
of their names ? One reason of the decrease of subscriptions is, there can be 
no doubt, the little countenance given to British Chess. Six years ago the 



THE CHESS WORLD. 185 

income exceeded ;£'8oo. Of this upwards of £400 was paid to foreigners in a 
single year, at least twice the sum paid to native amateurs. The decline from 
£Soo to one-seventh of that sum indicates a healthy state of public feeling ; 
for an institution that makes " How not to do it, "so persistently its study, does 
not deserve public support. 

The information I have been able to obtain regarding the Annual General 
Meeting is extremely meagre. By the Resolutions of 1866, a ** Congress " has 
to be held in London this year, and at the General Meeting the Congress was 
appointed to commence on the 23rd November. Beyond this nothing appears 
to have been done, except that another person, besides Mr. G. W. Medley, has 
been authorised to write "Hon. Sec." after his signature. Nothing else has 
been reported by the new Hon. Sec, and the necessity or-oatpcdiency of what 
has been done is not apparent. It is little that the Managing Committee have 
been doing, and it appears they mean to do less than ever. They hav* a 
Manager at £2$ a year ; what can be the use, then, of a couple of Hon. Sees. ? 
The newly-appointed Secretary, we are told, is **F. H. Deacon, Esq." 
Any advantage that may result from the appointment will be Mr. Deacon's, 
rather than the Association's. I have been looking over a number of circulars 
issued in the name of the British Chess Association during the last six years, 
and I do not find the name " Deacon " in any Committee List, or even in any List 
of Subscribers. He has, however, had a sneaking connection with the Asso- 
ciation before. When the Problem Committee of 1862 had made a mess of it, 
he was called in as a fit and proper person to assist in rectif)ring (? justifying) . 
the Committee's awards. Neither he nor they were deterred by the considera- 
tion that he was a competitor ; and he may have brought his problems under 
the favorable notice of his associates ; for, though his set was unnoticed* in the 
original award, he, after his and the Committee's joint deliberations, is described 
as deserving commendation. I remember, too, that when the Chess Code of 
1862 was criticised unfavorably, Mr. Deacon stepped forthwith great confidence 
as its defender. But the' authors of the code disowned their self-elected cham- 
pion; and, though Mr. Deacon nailed the flag "No surrender" to the mast, 
they acted on the recommendation of their censors. 

He is memorable also on another account. In 1859 he caused two games 
to be published, which he alleged he had played with Mr. Morphy. The 
American emphatically denied that he had ever played with Mr. Deacon at all, 
and asserted that the games were "simply forgeries." I understand that nobody 
that has played with Mr. Deacon is likely to forget him, and Morphy had an 
extraordinary power of recollecting trains of play ; so, if he was beaten by 
Mr. Deacon, it is impossible that he could have forgotten either the fact 
or the game. What could be his object, then, in making such a charge 
against Mr. Deacon ? Either one or other of these gentlemen must have been 

p 



186 THJB CHB8S WOBLD. 

guilty of offensive and aggravated untruth. If Mr. Deacon is innocent 
(as it is to be hoped he is, were it only because the charge brought 
against him is the graver of the two), the defence he adopted was ill- 
advised. I know of nothing beyond his ipse dixit (see Illustrated London 
News, March 30th, 1861), save the assertion of a friend that he would 
have borne evidence had not the chaige been so flagrant. Why did not 
Mr. Deacon hand about among his friends the game he won of Morphy at the 
time it was played T or, if he did, why did he not adduce their testimony ? 
For the honor of British Chess, Mr. Morphy's charge, if untrue, ought to have 
been rebutted by indisputable evidence, and until it is, and Mr. Deacon stands 
clearly exonerated in the matter, I doubt the wisdom of his friends in allow- 
ing him to accept the Secretaryship of the British Chess Association. 

It is only just to add, that Mr. Deacon has composed some creditable 
problems, and played many good games. Those of the latter which have been 
published are generally very copiously annotated, and the friend that' writes the 
notes for him often praises him lavishly. I have dwelt long enough on him. 
His accession is less likely to strengthen the moribund British Chess Associa- 
tion than to accelerate its fidl, and the ill-wishers to this institution may there- 
fore not be sorry for the appointment. I remain, gentlemen, your obedient 
servant, TALUS.

this competition will be given in our next, and the names of the

competitors can learn any particulars by appl^'ing to Mr. W. S. Pavitt,

Great Chess Msbtino at York. — ^A gathering of the Yorkshire 
Chess Amateurs and their friends is appointed for the week beginning 
Monday, August 3rd, to be held at the De Grey Rooms, York. On this 
occasion, in addition to a niunber of prizes to be competed for by players 
of the first, second, and third class of strength, there will be a challenge 
cup or prize, probable value £50, guaranteed value £40, to become the 
property of the person who first twice wins the society^s £10 prize. Com- 
petitors must pay an entrance fee of £1 Is. In addition to their subscrifKion 
of 10s. 6d. as members. There will also be offered a challenge cup or 
prize, pi-obable value from £25 to £30, to become the property of the 
person who first twice wins the Society's £5 prize. Finally, a problem 
prize, value £10, but subject to diminution in case of there being less than 
twenty entries, the entrance fee being 5s. A programme with full par- 
ticulars of the proposed assembly may be had on application to A. Ball, 
Esq , the Hon. Secretary, St. Leonard's York. 

Chess Tournament at Warsaw. — In another part of our present 
number will be found a couple of games played in a Handicap Tourney, 
recently finished at Warsaw. Through the politeness of Mr. L. Roguski 
and other members of the Committee, we are in possession of several 
more games contested in this very interesting competition, the best of 
which will be given in our next The Tournament appears to have been 
organised by Mr. S. Winawere, who, it will be remembered, gained the 
second prize in the Great Paris Congress last year. Nineteen combatants 
entered the lists, and in all, about three hundred games were played. 
At the end, the first prize was awarded to Mr .Simon Winawere, the 
second to Mr. Klerynski, the third to Mr. Roxanski, and the fourth to 
Mr. Landau.

THE CHESS PLAYER.* 

The reader's imagination will have no difficulty in picturing two individuals 
seated at a small table. Let him suppose these persons to be two of the lights of 
the age ; one, perhaps, is a dignitary of the Church, renowned for his extensive 
erudition: the name of the other stands high in the ranks of science. Their 
powerful minds — which daily pour forth important suggestions respecting the 
spiritual and material welfare of mankind — are absorbed in silent and intense 
reflection. Their eyes are fixed. ** On what?" you will ask. ** Are they 
watching the results of a chemical experiment, or endeavouring to decipher a 
Greek Manuscript of St. Augustine ? " By no means ; the table to which all 
their faculties seem rivited, is curiously inlaid with alternate squares of white 
and black wood, upon several of which stand upright toys, bearing some 
resemblance.to children's skittles, only they have a greater variety of shapes, and 
are more elaborately carved. " Surely," you continue, ** these intellectual 
magnates cannot be solely occupied by such frivolous objects ? " We will see. 
The Philosopher has, you perceive, with a trembling hand shifted the tallest of 
the pieces of ivory from one square to the next — a simple act, which you or I 
would perform without experiencing the slightest emotion, or expecting it to 
cause any excitement in the breast of another. But mark its consequences upon 
the Archdeacon I With lips compressed, and brows closely knitted, he leans 
both arms upon the table edge, and, bending his head, increases the intensity 
of his gaze upon the playthings. His eyes — heavy with their load of thought — 
move with slow deliberation from square to square, from the white pieces of 
ivory to those so prettily dyed red. Suddenly his visage is lighted up with a 
fine idea ; he moves his hand as if to clutch one of the toys ; yet, ere his fingers 
close upon it, a second, and, perhaps, a better thought arrests them. His arm 
now remains in mid-air, and lo ! another ray of genius flashes into his mind ; 
he seizes a figure ; he moves it, and utters a strange syllable which sounds some- 
thing like " Check ! " Upon this the Philosopher completely forfeits his charac- 
ter as such, by assuming a fierce look of surprise and disappointment ; and, under 
the influence of those feelings, he unwisely, and without due consideration, 
changes the situation of his tall image. His grasp is scarcely relaxed from it, 
VOL. IV. — NO. VI. Q 



194 THS CHESS WORLD. 

before the Archdeacon makes another transposition of one of his own playthings, 
and exclaims; in a tone of extreme exultation, "Mate!" The over-exclt6d 
individuals immediately rise from the table ; and, as stage-duellists wipe their 
rapiers after a fatal thrust, so does the Archdeacon remove, with his handkerchief) 
the copious exudations which are escaping from under his wig. The absorbing 
question, discussed upon the chequered table by the aid of those elegant speci- 
mens of carving, is evidently solved ; in a manner, too, not at all pleasing to the 
Philosopher, who is trying all his might not to lose his temper. The Church<* 
man, on the contmfy, is so delighted, that he hums a tune of much too lively a 
measure to be found in any collection of psalmody. These outbreaks of temper 
and merriment somewhat puzzle you. Aware of their surpassing intellectual 
powers, you would have thought that no greater amount of wisdom and acumen 
than they had just appeared to exhibit, would be requisite for discovering the 
longitude or the Philosopher's stone. Nor would you, perhaps, have thought 
altogether erroneously. Still, all that deep consideration — that concentration 
of the brightest faculties — ^those complicated processes of reasoning — ^were 
equally necessary for the Archdeacon to win, and for the Philosopher to lose — 
a game of Chess ! 

As a mere looker-on, and knowing nothing whatever of the game, the only 

rational conjecture by which yon can account for the excitement and mental 

exertion displayed by the divine and his antagonist, is, that, toy-like as are its 

implements. Chess must be a very complicated affair. This, in point of fact, is 

the truth. The table or board, you may perceive, is divided into sixty -four 

sections, upon which thirty-two pieces or "men" are to be moved ; multiply 

sixty-four by each of the progressive numbers contained in thirty-two, add up 

the results, and even that product, large as it is, will give you but the faintest 

notion of the number of combinations the game is susceptible of : moreover, 

each sort of man has a move peculiar to itself. Then, as the game advances, 

the pieces diminish in number: all which tends to make the combinations actually 

numberless ; insomuch that no two games were ever ascertained to be alike. 

This I mention, that you may entertain no unfavourable impression regarding 

my two excellent friends ; for, peurile as their motions may have appeared to 

your ignorance of Chess, let the cloth of the one and the scientific eminence 

of the other guarantee that they are none of those children of larger growth 

who are — 

" Pleji^d with a rattle, tickled with a straw." 

The difficulties of the game, let me tell you, make it a science as well as ati 
amusement 

Besides all this, the game of Chess is rendered respectable by the great men 
who have been its votaries from the earliest periods of antiquity. There now 
lies before me a print copied from one of the tombs of Thebes, in which King 



TUK CUKSS WORLD. 195 

Rameses III; is in the very pith and marrow of a game of Chess.* His 
Majesty is evidently getting the best of it ; and, although that particular game 
must have been played somewhere about three thousand five hundred years ago, 
jret so cleverly has the sculptor wrought, that I can detect th'e same sort of 
exulting smile upon the Egyptian monarch's face while lifting his "man" to 
give checkmate, as that we have just noticed upon the countenance of the 
Archdeacon under similar circumstances. To enumerate all the sovereigns, 
warriors, sages, and heroes, who have played at, and consequently ennobled 
Chess, from the times of the Pharaohs down to the present, would not be 
amusing ; so, patient reader, I shall let you off with Rameses as a specimen of 
the ancient, and (oh, heavy declension !) with 'some notices of an esteemed 
living friend as illustrative of the modem Chess-player. 

So completely identified is Mr. Mortimer Mason with the game, and so 
intense has been his application to it, that a close observer may detect "Chess- 
player" written in capital letters upon his countenance. He has a broad forehead 
made slightly bald at the crest from a habit of thrusting his hand into his front 
hair while in the throes of a difficult position. His eyes, though penetrating, 
have a thoughtful expression ; his mouth is usually closed ; the tight com- 
pression of the under lip indicating great firmness and energy of purpose when 
once his mind is made up as to the move he will make. I shall never forget 
my introduction to him. It took place at the house of a country friend, 
whither we had gone under pretence of shooting. After dinner, he chal- 
lenged me to the Chess-board, and we sat down at seven o'clock. As Hector 
and Achilles are described by old Homer to have commenced their fight 
with more caution than energy, so did we — dreading, though unconscious 
of, each other's power — ^make our first moves with slow and painful delibera- 
tion. By half-past eight my adversary forced an exchange of Bishops — a 
desperate measure, indicating that his courage was rising. At nine, affairs 
looked more serious, and vain was our hostess's interference to effect a 
truce by sending to announce tea. The fight was continued with equal 
determination on both sides till ten ; and by half-past eleven — ^when a servant 
brought us sandwiches — only two Pawns and a Knight, from each sdde, besides 
the Bishops, had been dragged from the field. Up to this time our host — 
himself an enthusiast — ^looked on ; but at midnight, a summons not to be 
slighted withdrew him from the scene of action ; though not till he ordered his 
servant to inform him of the progress of the game during the night, and of its 
final fate. About. two o'clock the next morning Mason saw an opportunity of 
gaining a strong position, by throwing away a Rook ; and the servant retired 
to knock up his master, and inform him that ** the White Castle was taken." 

* See Wilkinson's " Manners and Customs of the Ancient Egyptians^" plate 395. 

Q » 



196 THE CHESS WORLD. 

My counter-move struck terror into the enemy's ranks j it was an unexpcicted 
manoeuvre, one of those happy flashes of genius that sometimes dart unbidden 
into the mind. /, too, sacrificed my Rook, so as to obtain those advantages 
of position my antagonist coveted. But, in the end, Mason was too much for 
me. We fought on till four ; exhaustion was stealing over us^ and seeing that 
my opponent's skill was in reality greater than mine, I seized an opportunity of 
exchanging the last two fighting pieces left upon the board. Convinced th^t I 
could never win the game, I took care not to lose it ; and at a quarter to fivei 
John had the pleasure of announcing to his sleeping master that ** it was 
a drawn game ! " From that memorable morning Mason and I have been fast 
friends, notwithstanding that when we encounter he seldom fails to beat me. 
During these frequent meetings I have managed to draw from him spme 
particulars concerning his history, which, as he is a type of a large class, will 
perhaps be found interesting. 

One wet afternoon, whilst on a visit to a college friend in the mountains of 
Wales, Mason learnt the moves ; and, from that period. Chess may be 
designated the motive power of his existence. He was in a state of perplexing 
doubt as to the choice of a profession. Having a competency for the necessities 
of a respectable subsistence, he first thought of the army, then of the bar, but 
finally made up his mind in favour of the church, and actually went down t,o 
Wales to observe horw his friend lived, so as to judge how he would like the 
kind of life himself. A dozen games at Chess, however, decided him against 
the learned or the military professions ; from that time he became simply a 
CHESS-PLAYER. His habits and motions are governed by his favourite 
game : were he a Knight or a Bishop, he could not more completely belong to 
it ; for whatever may be the point in discussion in any company, the name 
of Mason, incidentally mentioned, is certain to change the subject to Chess- 
playing. Whenever he visits, not the friend himself, but his Chess apparatus 
is the attraction ; whenever he travels. Chess is the cause of his journey. In 
London he is well known at every coffee-house where the game is respectably 
played, from his frequent patronage and presence. He visits them in r^jular 
rotation every season, and when he has used them quite up, commences his 
annual provincial tour. After Oxford and Cambridge he proceeds to , the 
north ; and so great is his skill, that his journey may be said to be one triumph 
till he gets to Edinburgh. There he is but too delighted to find his match — 
an advocate, belonging to the Chess Club of the Modem Athens, being strong 
enough to give him first move. From Edinburgh, Mason starts for Paris, to 
make a tour of the Chess-playing caf^s. He then returns to open the London 
campaign ; and thus has he passed year after year during the last quarter of a 
century. If you drop in at any of the cigar divans, literary institutions, or 
coffee-houses, during the London season, you will — provided you select the 



THE DHESS -WORLD. 197 

right evenings— meet Mr. Mason. He has his nights also for the various 
literary and mechanics' institutions, of all which he has been elected an 
honorary member from his eminence in the Chess world, of which their 
subscribers form no inconsiderable proportion. At his entrance many of the 
games in progress will be stopped, and he will most likely be pointed out to 
the country cousins present as the "lion" of the room. Should he condescend 
to play, many a table will be^deserted by ambitious imitators of his style. His 
favourite rendezvous is Kllpack's In Covent Garden — famed amongst artists, 
actors, and authors, as the cosiest of Chess rooms. Call there on the proper 
evening, and you will see at the end of the room a group of lookers-on, 
shrouded in anxious silence, whilcTitanding round a Chess-table. In the midst 
of them you will perceive our friend Mason seated opposite to his favourite 
antagonist, a gentleman whose grave deportment receives additional solemnity 
from a pair of silver spectacles. On Mason's countenance sits the calmness of 
an assured victor, whilst his opponent occasionally breaks out into a state 
of perspiring perplexity. So complete is the absorption of the players, that 
nothing short of an earthquake would be likely to disturb them. Indeed, Mason 
once withstood a test which would with other men be hardly less effective. 
One night his lawyer, who always knew where to find him, hastened into 
the divan to acquaint him with some good news. The spectacled antagonist 
had fortified his King so strongly, that our friend coiJd by no stratagem weaken 
its position ; but at the moment of the attorney's entrance, a chance of sur- 
prising the enemy's camp by a grand coup de main offered itself. The lawyer 
rushed into the midst of the group at this ticklish point, saying, **Mr. Mason, 
1 have news from India!" "Indeed!" he answered, moving his Bishop. 
•*Yes, sir. Your uncle, the major, is no more !" **Ha, ha!" he exclaimed 
to his opponent, shutting out each faculty from everything but the game ; 
** you must take it, you perceive !" The antagonist made the capture. 
** Good !" he rejoined ; and while moving his Knight, the lawyer informed 
him that seven hundred a-year was, by the death of his uncle, added to 
his income. He was deaf to the charming intelligence, for, at the moment 
it was uttered, he had earned the delightful privilege of exclaiming ** Check- 
mate ! " Then turning to the attorney, he said in a careless tone, ** I 

beg your pardon, Nibbs ; but you were remarking ? " The information was 

repeated, and Masdti, afler paying a short but affectionate tribute to the major's 
memory, coolly asked his adversary if he would have another game ? This 
sang-froid upon an occasion which would have caused in other breasts the most 
lively emotions, is only to be equalled by Dr. Robertson's story of an ancestor 
of his Royal Highness Prince Albert. John Frederic, Elector of Saxony, 
having been taken prisoner by the Emperor Charles V., was one day amusing 
himself at Chess with Ernest of Brunswick, when a messenger came to inform 



198 THE CHKSS WORLD. 

him that his enemy had condemned him to death. In spite of the sad intelli- 
gence, the elector played on with such spirit and ingenuity that he won the 
game. 

Though now possessed of a good income, Mason is still a bachelor. Not 
having found a fitting match at Chess, he has never made much progress in 
courtship. He had however, one affair of the heart with a well-known lady- 
player in the North. Matters went on for some time with great likelihood of 
a matrimonial termination; but Mason's love of Chess proved too strong for his 
love of the lady ; he beat her nearly every game, and she naturally jilted him. 
There is a French proverb which says, **If you would win a damsel's heart, 
always lose to her at Chess." This is probably founded on an anecdote con- 
cerning Count Ferrand of Flanders, whose wife conceived so mortal a hatred to 
him from their misunderstandings over the Chess-board, that when he was 
taken prisoner at the battle of Bovines, she suffered him to remain in durance a 
long time, though she might have easily procured his release. It would seem 
to be the fate of Chess-players, in all ages, to be ill-requited lovers. Mason, 
like many more of his class, revenges himself by a touch of depreciation of 
the whole sex : he is ungallant enough to say that the reason why so few women 
play well, is the unbroken silence which Chess imposes. 

My enthusiastic friend's character would not have been complete had he 
failed to write on the subject of Chess This, however, he has done^ and in a 
manner quite worthy of himself. His work is historical, its main object being 
to reform the nomenclature of the pieces. The origin of the game is decidedly / 

oriental, and the power of the fair sex being extremely limited in the East, it is, 
he argues, manifestly absurd to call the most powerful actor in the whole 
collection a ** Queen." The truth is, that the orientals call this piece the 
general or grand vizier, Fc^rsana. " And why," inquires our author, "may we 
not unsex the Queen, and cai?>ter, with more propriety, the premier?" Again, 
what have Bishops to do with §^t?arIik?a^'H^^ Evidently nothing ; for tliey 
have been barbarously substituted for the ^IppwlMfcwhich the French have 
corrupted to>/, and ourselves to Bishop. Against tl^ltf^^' ^^^°"'^ P*^^"" 
warfare is not so strongly directed, because the Persian wm^K^ ^^'^"^ "^^""^ 
we get rook) means a war-chariot, in which a miniature CaJ^^^^ '^^""l* 
With Knights he is contented; and states the Pawns to be co^I^"^ 
from^..,. (Persian A.^4 "footmen," or infantr>^ which they^fc/^a 
considered His concluding remarks consist of what I cannot ZlT^ 
judicious lamentation over the exclusiveness of the game to the mTddie m 
higher classes of society. - The humble and intelligent artisan "h^" ^ 
-would find much more amusement in Chess than in the expensiCe reveMe. 
the tap-room, or in the beer-accompanying pleasures of skittle. It is . 1 

mistake to suppose that the intricacy of Chess is a bar to the humblest crp^^'^t 



THE CHESS WORLD- 



199 



acquiring its theory. The moves may be learnt in one or at most two lessons, 
and when once learnt, are never forgotten. This is all which is required; 
because the tyro — provided he play with a person as unskilled as himself — feels 
as deep an interest in his games as the best imaginable players. The expense 
of a board and men is trifling ; to ingenious persons nothing, for they are easily 
made." Mr. Mason concludes his benevolent remarks with proposals for 
establishing a sort of Chess mission for the furtherance and dissemination of the 
* * most philosophical of all games." He has also mentioned to me, in confi- 
deijce, that he intends to leave behind him endowments for all the Universities 
of Great Britain, sufficient to found chairs (and of course tables too) for Chess 
professorships. Long, however, may it be ere his last will and testament 
becomes effective I Long may the grim winner of that most chequered of all 
games — the game of life — withhold his final move from Mr. Mortimer Mason I

One evening, my uncle Gregory unrolled a bundle of Chess manuscripts which 
had been given him by a Latin monk of Jerusalem. They were on strips of 
parchment, of various sizes and colors, and were written, he said, in a sort of 
barbarous Latin interlarded with Grsedsms and often disfigured with Armenian 
and Hebrew idioms. From their appearance they might have been three or 
four hundred years old. Many were evidently translated from the Arabic and 
Persian and all of them related to Asiatic Chess. I cannot repeat here the 
learned disquisition of which my Uucle made them the text. But I will give a 
{€sff of them as he rendered them into English for our gratification, premising 
that I have somewhat altered the positions in order to make them conform to 
our method of playing the game. 

L 

Arrange your white pieces and pawns, O lovSs of the gentle art of chess, 
in battle-array, as at the commencement of a game. Then tell me on what 
square the black monarch shall be placed in order to enable the player of the 
white forces to mate in three moves.

The hidden meaning of these cabalistic lines was first found out by me, 
El'Zuli, ihe Arabian ; they were carved, by a cunning hand, on the back of an 
ancient Chess-board, which had been preserved far many generations in a

IV. 

Baldwin, King of Jerasalem, was expelled from his dominions by means of 
a rebellion fomented by the arch enemy of mankind and the chmch. In these 
straits he took refuge in a hermit's cave where he beguiled the time by playing 
Chess ; here the evil one appeared on a certain day and tempted the fugitive 
monarch to stake his soul against the restoration of his throne on the chances 
of a sm^le game. In this trial of skill the King was soon worsted and the 
awful mate was approaching when the hermit entered. Divining at a glance his 
critical ^tuation the worthy man of God counselled him to play his King to its 
fifth square ; he followed the advice of his ghostly monitor, although without 
comprehending how he should avoid the consequent mate in four moves. The 
tempter immediately vanished and, with wonder, Baldwin observed that the 
figures on the chess-board had assumed the form of a cross. At that moment 
intelligence arrived that the rebellion was at an end, and that the deposed lord 
of the Holy City was invited to reascend his throne in peace. This position 
has ever since been known as the Cross and the Crown or 



Crux et Corona.

THE KING'S BISHOP'S GAMBIT. 

To THE Editors of **The Chess World." 

Gentlemen, — Mr. Eraser is deserving of the best thanks of all Chess- 
players who take an interest in the "bookish theorie" of the game, for his 
admirable article on the ** McDonnell Attack " in the King's Bishop's Gambit, 
which appeared in the last number of the Chess World. The analysis in 
question, is, in my humble opinion, one of the most valuable modem contri- 
butions to the literature of Chess, and, should it stand the test of practical play, 
will necessarily effect a complete revolution in the theory of the Bishop's 
Gambit, an opening that has for years baffled the ingenuity of all the leading 
authorities on the game. There is one point, however, on which I should like 
to have Mr. Eraser's opinion. I am well aware of the risk I run in venturing 
to criticise the labours of so pains-taking and accurate an analyst, but it appears 
to me that he has omitted in "variation V." to consider the effect of a line of 
play, which so far as my examination has gone, seems to strengthen the defence 
considerably. I repeat the opening moves : — 

White, Black, 

1. P. to K. fourth P. to K. fourth

Of course the foregoing variations are merely tentative and experimental, 
and must be accepted for what they are worth. Possibly they may be altogether 
erroneous, but I venture to submit them, as they appear to me to touch upon 
the only part of Mr. Eraser's analysis which falls short of absolute demonstra- 
tion. Should Mr. Fraser, however, on further examination, succeed in 
establishing the validity of his attack against the line of defence above indicated, 
there can, I think, be no doubt that the check of the Queen at the third move 
of King's Bishop's Gambit will have to be abandoned as theoretically defective 

and untenable ; for unless it can be safely followed up with 4. — — — • 

P. to K. Kt. fourth 

or the purpose of maintaining the Gambit Pawn, the check at Rook's fifth is 

not only so much "lost time," but involves the defence in an unnecessarily 

embarrassed position. It is true that instead of attempting to support the 

Gambit Pawn, the second player may, afcer checking with the Queen at Rook's 

fifth, move 4. ' ■ ' or 4. • ^ but neither of 

Kt. to Q. B. third P. to K. B. fourth 

these defences can be considered perfectly satisfactory. The latter, indeed, 

seemj to leave him with an inferior position from the outset, while the 

former, though I believe the best move the board affords, leads to some 

extremely difficult and complicated variations which involve the immediate 

surrender of the Gambit Pawn, and demand the greatest exactitude on the part 

of the defence. If, therefore, the "Modern Classical Defence" to the King's 

Bishop's Gambit is to be definitely abandoned as insufficient (and practically it 

Aas been abandoned, for we rarely see it adopted now-a-days in actual play), 

the question naturally arises, which of the so-called " irregular " defences, such 

as 3. , 3. , 3. 



P. to Q. Kt. fourth F. to Q. B. third P. to Q. fourth 

3. -: , 3. f must be substituted in its 

P. to K. B. fourth Kt. to K. B. third 

place ? Personally, I am of opinion that the three first-nam.ed defences are 

demonstrably inefficient, and that, if the King's Bishop's Gambit problem is 

capable of any satisfactory solution, we shall have to fall back upon either the 

"old classical defence" of 3.—^ or the modem move of 

P. to K. B. fourth 



Kt. to K. B. third 

Should you consider the subject sufficiently interesting, I shall hope to be 
allowed to offer a few remarks on the so-called "irregular defences " in a future 
number of the Chess World. 

I am, Gentlemen, 

Yours faithfully, 

ROBT. B. WORMALD.

West German Chess League.— The seventh annual meeting of 
this society was held at Aix-la-Chapelle, on the Ist, 2nd, 3rd, and 4th 
inst., and, whether in the quality or number of the competitors, appears 
to have been fully equal to any previous assemblage of the leagua As a 
proof of this it is enough to say that Messrs. Anderssen, Paulsen, Max. 
Lange, and Zukertort were in the lists, and that eighteen chess-boards 
were in constant requisition when play was going on. 

The chief players in the grand tourney were Anderssen, Max. Lange, 
W. Paulsen, Zukertort, and Schallopp, who contested their games, for the 
most part, in the gardens of the Erholung. Some of the games in this 
tournament were remarkably good, and excited the intensest interest; 
more particular the one between Mr» Anderssen and Dr. Lange, which, in 
parts, was admirable; that between Messrs. W. Paulsen and Zukertort, 
which occupied eleven hours; and the game between Br. Lange and 
W. Paulsen, which lasted above fifteen hours, and served, eventually, to 
place Dr. Lange at the head of the competitors. The following is the 
score of the four leading combatants in the chief tourney: — 

Won. Lost. Drawn. 

Max. Lange 1 of Anderssen 1 to Anderssen 

„ 1 of W. Paulsen ... 

,, ... ..* 1 of Zukertort ... 

Anderssen ... ,.. 1 of Lange 1 to Max. Lange 

„ 1 of Zukertort 1 to W. Paulsen ... 1 

Zukertort 1 of W. Paulsen 1 to Max. Lange 

„ 1 of Schallopp 1 to Anderssen 

W. Paulsen 1 of Anderssen 1 to Zukertort 

fi ... >.. ... ... .. ... 1 to Max. Lange 

In the Tourney by players belonging to the Khenish provinces, the 
first prize was gained by Mr. Tendering, of Bonn; the second by 
Dr. Schuster, of Aachen. The proceedings were enlivened on the 2nd 
inst. by a dinner at •' Bernart's Locale," at which above sixty guests sat 
down, and interchanged toasts, songs, and sentiments for many hours. 

The Yorkshire Chess Meeting. — This meetifig was held in the 
De Grey Rooms during the week beginning August 3rd, and, though not 
so numerously attended as the promoters of it expected, attracted several 
good players, and passed off pleasantly enough. Ten competitors entered 
the lists for the chief tourney, the first prize of which was gallantly won 
by Mr. E. Thorold, of Bath, one of the finest players of the day. The 
Rev. W. Wayte, of Eton, carried off the second prize. In the second 
class tourney Mr. B. W. Fisher gained the first prize, and Mr. Finlinson, 
of Huddersfield, the second prize. The combatants in the third class 
tournament were manjr of them ladies, and one of these. Miss Felgate, 
bore off the second prize. These three prize tourneys were, of course, the 
main objects of attention ; but there were many other contests which were 
regarded with great interest, more particularly five games played simul- 
taneously by Mr. Wayte without sight of a chess-board, the whole of 
which he won. All the arrangements of the meeting were made and 
carried out by Mr. Ball and Mr. Skipworth, two of the most enthusiastic 
chess amateurs in Yorkshire, and appeared to give very general satis- 
faction.
