A LEAF FROM MY CHESS LIFE. 

I AM an old man now, but not so old that I do not still cling with the weakness 
of age and the folly of our kind to the hope of an additional decade or two of 
this earth's life. As my hair has been gradually whitening from winter to 
winter, and the stoop in my shoulders becoming more and more marked, I have 
outgrown, in great measure, my familiarity with the game of cheSs. Only 
occasionally now, when I happen to be watching a contest between some of my 
youthful friends does the old enthusaism come back again, and a crowd of 
chess reminiscences rush with glowing warmth to my mind. One of these, a 
trivial but well remembered incident, I have written out here for the pages of 
the Chess Monthly. 

My first master in chess, singularly enough, was an old nogro belonging to 
my father's plantation, and the only chess-playing person of his race with whom 
I have ever chanced to meet. He had followed an uncle of mine through the 
stirring events of our Revolutionary struggle and had managed to learn the 
elements of the game from the servant of a French officer in the American 
army. He knew little more than the moves and some of the simpler variations 
of the Queen's Gambit, which opening he habitually played; so that fur some 
time I firmly believed, upon his authority, that one of the fundamental and 
inviolable laws of chess required each combatant to begin with Pawn to Queen's 
Fourth, I still possess a souvenir both of his chess wisdom and mechanical 
skill in the shape of a unique little pocket board and set of men which 
have accompanied me in all my travels; for, in an amateur way, I have seen a 
large number of the kingdoms and republics, the monarchs and peoples, of 
the curious world we live in. 

While I was still some years under age, my father, who had an overweening 
confidence in my steadiness of character, determined, instead of putting me at 
the university, to send me at once to Europe, for he had an educational theory 
of his own, in which travel and a lafge acquaintance with the world usurped 
altogether the place of colleges. Accordingly I found myself, just before the 
meteoric return of the Emperor from Elba, snugly at home in the Latin 
quarter in Paris, buiily charging myself with the French which I was after- 

VOL. IV. — NO. VII. T 



234 THB CHESS WORLD. 

wards to fire off at the landlords and post-boys of all Europe. During the 
Hundred Days I watched with eager interest the transcending political events 
passing around me and felt all the hero-worshiping sympathy of youth for the 
great monarch of the chess boards of war and politics. His ultimate and final 
defeat at Waterloo was, to me, a sore disappointment, but I soon dulled the 
edge of my grief in witnessing the gorgeous displays and brilliant festivities of 
the Allies, during their second occupation of Paris. Curiosity led me to visit 
Vienna at the time the famous Congress of 1815 was sitting there. Some 
casual readings in Italian history, however, induced me to start thence, in hot 
haste, for that land whose name and whose language roll so softly from the 
tongue, and whose grand share in the world's events is graven in the pages of 
history and on the memories of scholars for ever. But the excitement of a new 
life, the wonderful and interesting incidents of the times, and the thousand 
intoxicating details of old-world experience had half turned my brain and 
wholly exhausted my physical endurance, and I fell sick at Modena. The kind 
care of some students in the newly re-established university at that place, who, 
even in that reactionary period, felt a lively enthusiasm for America and her 
liberal principles, soon restored me to strength. But long after my convalescence 
I Hngered in the ducal city, clinging to the several delightful acquaintances I 
had formed and enchanted by my first measured view of Italian life. My time 
was spent in frequent intimacy with the students and in viewing the scattered 
curiosities of the town. I used to ascend the old Ghirlandina and muse on the 
story of the feuds between the Modenesi and the Bolognesi, a witness of which, 
the Secchia RapitOy made famous by the poet Tassoni, was still preserved in 
the lofty and ancient tower; I roamed through the Gallery of Art in the 
Palace and through the glorious Biblioteca Estense, over whose multitude of 
books the erudite Muratori and the critical Tiraboschi once so worthily presided; 
and in the broad ducal gardens, full of laughing fountains and shady trees, I 
loved to smoke my sunset cigar, while I practised my new fledged Italian 
speech upon the groups of gay and careless burghers who thronged, in the 
afternoon, the long promenades. 

Travel and its consequent excitement had driven all chess ideas from my 
mind. From the day I left my quiet Virginian home I had not played a solitary 
game. But in rummaging the bookstores on the Strada Maestra, I chanced to 
find a copy of Ercole del Rio, and my passion for the game, which had been 
kindled in America, burst straightway into a flame. The mementos of my 
African teacher — ^the little board and men — ^were immediately unpacked from 
the idleness of my trunk, and I betook myself to a favourite haimt of mine, a 
garden-like place, outside of the city, on the margin of a tiny streamlet, just 
big enough to ripple. It was a dreamy spot, where I could lay and gaze out 
upon the Via Emilia with its strange alternation of noblemen's carriages and 






THE CHESS WORLD. 235 

peasants' carts, of officers on horseback and market women on foot, and think 
of the gray past, when stately Roman armies womid along its course, going 
home to create emperors, or marching abroad to conquer empires. There, 
protected from the too loving rays of the sun by a screen of vines and olives, 
beneath the smiling sky, in the mild air, and amid the spicy odors of 
the South, I passed long hours in stud3ring the ingenious variations of the 
Anonymous Modenese. 

One morning, I remember it well, a gem of a day set like a diamond in the 
years of my youth, I was aroused from my absorbing study of the book and the 
board by a pleasant salutation from an old gentleman who had come into my 
secluded nook unheard and unseen. As I looked up he said simply, but with 
the grace of a courtier, that he had walked early into the town and met a 
student acquaintance who had told him of me and of my Chess zeal, and 
informed him of my usual resort ; that being a lover of the sport himself he 
had sought me out; "nor ought we," he pleasantly added, **to be altogether 
strangers, for I still recollect how in my younger days, our countrymen fought 
together for a common cause against a common foe." After my response he sat 
down beside nie and narrated, incidentally, something of his story. He was a 
French Count and, like the majority of his class, had fled his country upon the 
eruption of the terrible political volcano of 1789. He had travelled much 
since then, but had been now for some years quietly settled an hour or two*s 
walk west of Modena. The few members of his family had either died in 
exile, or suffered in their native land the sad fate of their monarch and 
his j^eautiful consort. After the Restoration, he had become so weaned to 
foreign customs and life abroad, that he felt little desire to return to his desolate 
home, and lived now on a small pension from the French King. I subsequently 
learned from the students, by whom he was respected, although they gave him 
humourously the soubriquet of // Conte dello Scacchiere, that though far from 
leading the life of a hermit, he still rarely or never attended the gay assemblies 
of the city, and went to court only once in a twelve-month to pay his respects 
to the reigning Duke, Francesco IV., through whom the Bourbon bounty 
reached him. To the peasants in his neighborhood he stood in the kindest 
relations, both as a friend and counsellor; to the poor his charity was only 
measured by his means. 

A thousand years of life could not drive from my remembrance the intensely 
pleasant hours we passed, morning after morning, in chess converse beside the 
sparkling Italian rivulet. The Count had been a frequenter of the Parisian 
Cafg de la R%ence in the time of its ancient glory. He had known and 
encountered L%al, the teacher of Philidor, and had played with Franklin and 
Rousseau. To the French philosopher he was accustomed to give, I believe, 
the odds of a piece; with the American sage he played upon even terms, but 

T % 



236 THE CHESS WORLD. 

won a majority of games. Our own countryman, he told me, was the complete 
embodiment of the principles which his Morals of Chess inculcate. His spirits 
were never raised by victory nor depressed by defeat ; slow and careful in his 
moves, he paid the closest attention to the progress of the game, in which he 
allowed himself to become wholly absorbed, but bore the issue with the calmest 
and most courteous indifference. After one game, played at Franklin's house 
in Passy, in which the old patriot philosopher had shown far less than his usual 
strength, he remarked to the Count, *'I have had to play to-day against both 
you and the gout." With Bernard, Carlier, Verdoni, Leger and the rest of the 
* Amateurs * our chess Count stood upon the footing of intimacy. Of Bernard's 
brilliant style of play he spoke with unbounded enthusiasm. But he chiefly 
loved to dwell upon the gentle character and Caissian skill of the light and 
lustre of modem chess — the immortal Philidor. A common passion for music 
had drawn them somewhat together. He sat by the side of the composer when 
Philidor's last opera, Themistocle, was performed at Fontainebleau in 1785, 
and frequently accompanied him to the organ of a church in Paris where 
Philidor occasionally whiled away an hour or two in either playing or listening 
to some favorite melodies from his own motets. He had playefl many games 
with the great master and witnessed innumerably more ; as he often referred, 
in the course of our conversations, to those remarkable contests, we mourned 
in unison his sad neglect in not recording them for the profit and delight of 
posterity. There was, he asserted, as much difference between the mere 
externals of Philidor's play — the motions of his arm, the gaze of his eye, and 
the shades of his countenance — and other men's, as between the grac^ul 
majesty of Apollo and the uncouth awkwardness of a boor. In his combats 
with the monarch of chess, the count received the knight at first and afterwards 
the Pawn and two moves ; but finally in the last battle they ever had, just 
prior to Philidor's return to London in 1 788, the game was drawn at the odds 
of Pawn and move. Upon the story of this last encounter the old chessman 
was wont to linger with a delightful prolixity. It took place at the Count's 
own lodgings one Sunday evening, and its every incident and circumstance 
were treasured facts in the storehouse of his memory. ^ 

As our acquaintance rapidly ripened into intimacy, I used often to ask my 
grey-headed friend to meet me face to face over the chess-board ; but either 
averse to displaying the weakness which accumulated years might have substi- 
tuted for the skill of youth and manhood, or afraid of the mental effort 
required, he at first steadily refused. At length, perhaps, to put an end to my 
repeated solicitations, he quaintly proposed that we should play a game, in 
which we should have a day to reflect on our every move, reporting our decision 
to each other as we met in the morning at. our quiet trysting-place under the 
vines. With the ardor of an enthusiast I immediately acceeded to his whimsical 



THE CHBSS WORLD. 237 

project, and we drew for the move. I gained it and promised to communicate 
my opening move to him the following day. Determined that my native skill 
should not lack the material assistance which books afford, I bought, as I went 
home to my lodgings in the dty, a copy of Ponziani's excellent work, and 
placed it with Del Rio and my chess-board upon my table and straightway 
began my preparatory studies. After a good deal of consideration I could find 
no sounder opening than that of the KLing's Bishop, which I consequently 
resolved to play. 

Our game, which lasted of course during several weeks, I insert here, as 
recorded from day to day in my journal at that time. I am led to do so, not 
because it is a specimen of good play, but from the peculiar oddity of the 
manner in which it was conducted. The sombre reflections of mature age have 
in no wise diminished my chess sensitiveness, and I therefore beseech the 
critical student to remember that my practice had been at that period very 
limited : — 

Young America, Old France.

The day upon which the Count announced his second move I went home at 
an early hour and sat down to consider my reply. What was my dismay to find 
that neither of my chess authors made any mention of his bold counter- 
gambit ! Thrown, consequently, upon my own unaided resources I spent some 
hours in brain-muddying analysis. At length I determined to prevent his 
castling with the King's Rook by capturing his Knight.

This was certainly perplexing. The Count threatened an immediate check- 
mate. If I moved my Eing, he mated me at once ; if I Castled, he checked 
with his Enightand won my Queen; if I played Pawn to Eing's Bishop's thirds 
he simply took it and I did not dare to retake. There was evidently no other 
move than to play my Enight home again — ^bad as this was.

As I was passing out of the city the morning after I had ingeniously planned 
this twentieth blunder, I met Giovanni Battista Borsari the friend and pupil of 
Del Rio— the inheritor of much of that great master's chess skill and of all his 
chess manuscripts. I had been introduced to him a week before, and he 
courteously begged leave to accompany me in my walk. During our conver- 
sation he asked me how my odd game with the Count was progressing. With 
all the modesty usual to young players, I replied that although my opponent 
had at first taken advantage of my want of practice and played a puzzling 
counter gambit unknown to chess writers, still I had succeeded in parrying the 
attack he had thereby obtained. Now, I continued, my pieces will soon be as 
well developed as his, and then, with the advantage of a Pawn which I have 
contrived to win, I see no reason why the victory should not be mine. But at 
length we arrived at the rendezvous, where we found my old chess-loving 
Gaul already seated. After the customary exchange of courtesies I announced 
my last move. **Ah, my friend," said the Count pleasantly, and apparently 
without noticing my remark, " are you fond of problems ?" " Yes, " I answered, 
"but I have solved so few, that if they are in more than three moves they 
puzzle me." ** I have one for your scrutiny, but as it is neither by Stamma, 
nor by either of our Modenesi, I will not answer for its beauty." 

He took my little board and b^^an to arrange the position. After a 
moment he drew my attention to it. 

** Here you have it; W^i^e to play and mate in three moves P 

Imagine my horror I He had set up the position of our game and the 
white pieces were his ! I looked at it awhile and saw that severe as was the 
shock given to my chess ambition, I had nothing to do but to acknowledge the 
soundness of the Count's problem and join the amused Borsari in laughing at 
my own discomforture. 



THE CHBBS WOBLD. 239 

A heavy sorrow which hung a cloud of grief over many long days of my 
existence — the death of the^entlest of fathers — forced me to return to America 
early in the autumn. My parting from the Count was sad and affectionate, for 
we had grown into each other's daily life until the ties that boimd us were almost 
as warm as those of parent and child. After my arrival home we exchanged 
two or three letters, and then our correspondence ceased. Some time afterwards 
I commissioned a countryman, who was about to visit Italy, to enquire into his 
fate, and bring me, if possible, some memorial of him. This American friend 
wrote me from Bologna that the venerable nobleman died in May, 1 8 19, and 
sent me a copy of Stamma, which had found its way from his library into one 
of the book-shops of Modena. As I look now at his neatly written autograph 
on its first page, I recall, with the distinctness of yesterday, the form of the 
courtly, genial, and kind old chess-lover, and vividly retrace in my memory the 
living delights of the golden summer months I spent with him in the honored 
home of Ponziani and of Ercole del Rio. — American Chess Monthly,

To THB EDTTOBa OF " The Chess Wobld." 

Mt Dkar Sibs, — ^The non-appearance of my promised remarks, to 
which Mr. Oatlow allndes in his letter giren in yonr last issue, would 
almost warrant his belief that I had allowed " judgment to go by de&ult," 
neyerthdess, although I haye been yery dilatoiy about it, I haye not 
altogether oyerlooked the point we are amicMy contesting. (I want that 
last adyerb considoubly emphasised, as a short but eflPectiye answer to 
some of the kindly remarks in which my unknown--to me — ^brother 
Chess-player has indulged ) 

Mr. Oatlow has put his finger on the weak places of my attack, this I 
must fairly admit, but I do not yet yield ; what I may haye to do in the 
future is another thing, for I belieye against both the fifth and sixth 
defences I gaye for Black, I can strengthen the attack so considerably, 
that I shall, in nearly all the variations, win ; and at any rate draw the 
game in those in which I fail to secure the victory. 



THE CHESS WORLD. 285 

Before giving tbe analyses, I must in juGticc to myself remark, that 
the "weaknesses" were owing more to a desire to estftblish a demonBtrat«d 
runcluslon, than to a failure in the initiatory more of the attack. TTiat, 
I still maint^n, is the best at White's disposal, and the variations I now 
offer would haye been given before, only that I was adverse to an indeter- 
minate end-game if conclusiveness could be — and, aa I rather rashly 
deemed had been— atuUned. 

Those that are interested in Mr. Catlow's defence to the Mortimer 
Gambit are referred to Vol IIL pp. 63, 138, 361 and 431, but more 
particularly to pp. 138, 139 and 140. 

The attacks assailed are those I have mentioned, the " Fifth " and 
" Sixth," on the assumption, then, that the first four are unassailable, 
which I do not assert. I proceed to show that my move of 93. B. to 
K.R. sixth is the best White has, by varying his afterplay. In the"PifUi 
Defence," instead of the move 24, B. takes KL P. <cbeck) I propose for 
White 
24. R. to Q. B. square

dangerous advanced Pawns for the piece he is in arrear. He may indeed

THE (mESS WORLD. 267 

In the sixth defence, after Black's twenty-sixth move of Q. to K. Kt. 
third, I remark that " forcing the exchange of Queens is his best line of 
play," and proceed by making White capture the Queen at once ; this I 
think is not his strongest move, I fancy 27. Kt. to K. R. fourth will prove 
better ; if then : — 

27. Q. takes Q. 

28. Kt takes Q. (check; K. to K. square. 

[His best move, I believe.] 
30. R. takes B. 

And the Pawns must be equal, whilst for his piece minus. White has 
compensation enough in position to draw, I think. 

In conclusion, I would hazard an inference. It appears to me that 
Mr. Catlow, from what he says in his last letter, does not estimate his 
move 80 highly as he did. The words, " as good," in the last paragraph, 
don't exactly correspond with the tenor of his letter at page 52, Vol. III. 

I am, Deab Sibs, 

Very truly yours, 

WILLIAM a PAVITT. 



To THE Editors op " The Chess World." 

Gentlemen, — In the last July number of the Chess World there is an 
article, by G. B. Fraser, on the King's Bishop's Gambit, in which he 
states — " White's sixth move, as is well known, is the commencement of 
the ^* McDonnell attack." At this stage McDonnell continued the game 
by 7. K. to Kt. second, instead of which I propose to do so by 7. Q. to 
K. B. third." From the wording of the above, your correspondent could 
hardly have been aware of the following note at page 818 Qf Mr. 
Staunton's "Handbook," — ** You may now adopt the very ingenious 
mode of carrying on the attack, which we owe to McDonnell, viz : — 

6. P. to K. Kt. third P. takes P. 

7. K. to Kt. second Q. to K. R thu-d 

[Jaenisch recommends 7. Q. to K. B. third, instead of 7. K. to Et. second.] 

8. P. takes P. Q. to K. Kt third 

[Black has a alight advuntage.]" 

Your correspondent will also observe, by the above note, that Black 

has another move besides the five moves which he gives as the only ones 

possible. 

Yours truly, 

Demerara,.Aug. 1st, 1868. E. G. Y. .

(Lhtss (SossiD. 

The Late Pbofbsboe Duncah FoRBBa— The following tribute to 
the memoiy of Dr. D. Forbes, with the accompanying game, we extract 
from the Bath Chnmide : — '* The recent lamented death of the dis- 
tingnished Oriental scholar and author, Professor Duncan Forbes, creates 
a Toid in the ranks of English Chess which it will be difficult, if not 
impossible, adequately to supply. In addition to his varied and extensive 
acquirements in Eastern lore, Dr. Forbes had an encyclopedical acquaint- 
ance and familiarity with the history and antiquities of Chess which has 
been possessed by no writer on the subject, either living or dead. An 
appointment he formerly held in the British Museum gave him access to 
the ample store of rare MMS. relating to Oriental Chess owned by that 
establishment, of which he skilfully availed himself in the composition of 
his learned work, *' The History of Chess.* To him alone is due the merit 
of having set at rest the long-vexed question r^;arding the birthplac^ 
of the noble game, by proving, as he has indisputably done, that it 
originated in India Professor Forbes was an enthusiastic amateur of the 
time-honoured pastime whose archaeology he understood so well, and a 
player of no ordinary calibre. The following porfw was contested by him

Death of Professor Duncan Forbes. — We are grieved to have to 
announce the death of this distinguished Oriental scholar, who expired at 
his residence in Burton Crescent a few weeks since. For above thirty 
years, the name of Dr. Forbes has been a household word among the 
Amateurs of Chess in England. At one time he was a player of consider- 
able skill, and the composer of many highly meritorious Chess Problems. 
It is not, however, as a Chess-player or Chess-problematist that Dr. Forbes 
has earned a right to be remembered reverentially by the lovers of the 
game ; his claims to their regard are based on surer ground : they rest 
upon the enduring foundation of his admirable "History of Chess," the 
most profound and interesting account of the " Royal pastime " which we 
possess. This eminently valuable treatise comprises a description ot 
the earliest Chess of which there is any record, — the CTvaturangay or 
PrimcBval Hindu Chess; its gradual change into the Shatranj^ or 
Mediseval game; the introduction of the game from India into Persia, and 
subsequently into Arabia. It then traces the progress of Chess towards 
the West, and shows how it was carried into the Lower Empire by the 
Persians, and into Western Europe by the Arabs. It treats, most 
entertainingly and learnedly, of early Chess in France and Germany ; of 
Chess in Scandinavia, England, Italy, and Russia. Gives animated 
pictures of Modem Oriental Chess, Chess in Abyssinia, Syria, Egypt, 
Persia, Hindustan, Chess in Burmha, Sumatra, Java, Malacca, Borneo, 
and China. A great charm and merit of the book is that it deals with the 
practical quite as much as with the theoretical nature of the game. We 
are not merely told that the Primaeval Chess was called Chaturangi^ but 
in that case, as in every other of importance, we are taught the moves and 
powers of the pieces, have explained to us the technical expressions of the 
game, and are presented with illustrative diagrams of curious games and 
solutions. Besides the " History of Chess," Dr. Forbes was the writer of 
innumerable articles, in the various Chess organs, on the subject of the 
game, his passion for which continued unabated to the end. To the 
world at large he was best known as the author of some of the best 
educational works in the Oriental characters, now used in the colleges and 
schools of India, including a Hindustani-English and English-Hindustani 
Dictionary, a Grammar of the Hindustani Language, and a Grammar of 
the Persian Language.

THE PROFESSOR'S DAUGHTER, 

You ask me to contribute something to the CAets Playet^s Annual^ It is so 
many years since I took an active interest in the game, that I find some difficulty 
in complymg with the request. The claims of my class, and the duties of the 
laboratoty, absorb most of my time ; and when I do play a game of chess, I feel 
that I have lost much of the old trick, and have acquired none of the new. 
Some of my pupils are as deeply read in Staunton's Handbook as I could wish 
them to be in my Manual of Chemistry^ and it is not always pleasant to be 
beaten by men who cram their chess for play as they cram their science for an 
examination. In fact, my game is like the book which the poet Voss chaiac- 
terizes in that teirible distich: — 

"Dein redseliges Buch lehrt mancherld Neues and Wahres; 

WHre das Wahre nur neu ; wSre das Neue nur Wahr !" 
" New diings and true things are taught, my fziend, in tHy gossiping voliune : 

" Oh that the true things were new ! Oh tiiat the new things were true I 

Hence I sddom play at chess, and have neither game nor problem to offer you. 

I( however, the relation of a youthful adventure {important, indeed to me, but 

of sn^U value to your readers) be worth a place in your Annual^ you are 

welc^R to insert- it. Of course I do not wish my name to appear; but you 

may allow me the pseudonyme of Albert. 

It was in the memorable chess year when De la Bourdonnais and McDonnell 

played their matches, that I left England to take up my abode at a celebrated 

German University, in order to improve my knowledge of anal3rtical chemistry^ 

in the laboratory of Professor Steilmann. My highest ambition was to be a 

good chemisty and to this end I determined to sacrifice all other pursuits ; or, 

at any rate, so to subordinate them that they should not interfere with my main 

object. The advice of my favourite Voss was ever before me : ' 

" Nicht hab' Uebervertraun, noch Misstraun ; strebe besdieiden 
Etwas, nicht Alles zu sein, aber noch weniger nichts." 

Despairing of being at once a good chemist and a fine chess-player, I deter- 
mined to sacrifice chess, and avoided the University Club, and was shy of the 
company of chess-players. It was, however, difficult to carry out the resolution; 

* This little story first appeared in Tomlinson's Ckesa Player's Annual. 
VOL. IV. — NO. Vm. Y 



274 THE CHESS WOBLD. 

the Professor being himself a votary of the game, and a hero-worshipper withal. 
His heroes were two : Berzelius the chemist, and the Chess-writer Koch. He 
was alwa3rs quoting the Lehrbuch der Chemie and the Codex der Schachsptdkunst; 
two works so perfect in his estimation, that he looked with suspicion on any- 
thing in chemistry not expressed in the unwieldy nomenclature of the learned 
Swede, and positively shrank from any discovery that cast a doubt on his 
chemical views ; so also would he reject as heretical any new move or new 
opening if it had not the sanction of the Codex, He justified his choice in this 
way I ** Is not Berzelius the greatest chemist in the world ? Has he not written 
the most elaborate and logical treatise on Chemistry — ^that * wise daughter of a 
foolish mother * — that the world ever saw ? And did not Berzelius quote my 
arrangement of pseudomorphous groups as the best ? Why, then, shall I not 
love Berzelius ? And as for the honoured Koch, is he not a brother professor 
at Kloster Berge and preacher in the Cathedral at Magdebiirgh [Koniglich 
Westphmicher Superintendent und Domprediger in Magdeburgh]. He has 
enlightened chess by his genius, and made it respectable by his profession. 
Shall I not, therefore, love Koch ? 

Such was the Professor*s mode of reasoning. For my own part, I stuck 
to the Lehrbuch, and generally succeeded in turning the Professor's thoughts 
from the Codex by asking how I could express some simple atomic formula in 
the more complex orthodox symbols. 

My diligence so won upon the Professor, that he invited me to his little 
evening parties, where I met the other professors, their wives and daughters, 
and a few favoured students like myself; but soon observed that love of chess 
was quite as good a passport to these soirees as knowledge of chemistry. The 
Professor was a widower ; but his only child, a lovely girl of eighteen, prided 
at these parties. She played, chess with sufficient skill to beat most^r the 
students, and her father was not a little proud of her accomplishment. It was 
confidentially whispered that the Professor would allow her to marry none but 
a good chess-player. He argued, that knowledge of the game indicated 
steadiness of character {ballast^ as we call it), prudence, forethought, analytical 
skill, so essential to a chemist, and patience, equally essential to a disciple of 
Berzelius ; and believing his daughter to possess all these virtues, he naturally 
wished her future husband to be equally accomplished, just as a man whose 
daughter is an heiress, in seeking a partner for her, looks to his estate. How- 
ever, she was a sweet girl — a little inclined to pedantry; and he was a kind 
old man — a good deal so inclined. He was short, with a large head and silver 
grey hair, streaming in abundance down his neck as if to make up for its 
absence in front His features were small — nose very small (not an indication 
of a large mind, certainly); the face of a healthy pink, which appeared all the 
brighter from the contrast with his grey hair and his black velvet cap. He had 
a stoop, which was apparently increased by his head projecting considerably 



THE OHiCSS WOBLD. 2*75 

forward. His hands were very small and narrow, .and the fingers long — a 
conformation to which I often thought that he owed much of his manipulatory 
skill. When he did not wear his professor's gown, he delighted in a black 
velvet robe, which assisted by his stoop, hung considerably forward. What 
was remarkable for a chemist, his hands were always clean, his linen spotless, 
and I never saw an acid stain upon his dress. Although much of this precision 
was natural to the good old man, it must have failed occasionally but for the 
constant attention of Miss Steilmann, who was devotedly attached to her father, 
and proud of his personal appearance. And certainly the affection was mutual. 
It showed itself in various ways at these little soiries. When she played the 
piano or sang, the Professor could not play at chess or discuss Berzelius, though 
ready for both when any one else was performing. When she brought out 
some piece of fancy-work or other specimen of her skill, he watched with 
nervous anxiety the looks and words of the professors* wives and daughters, 
and was as grateful for a word of encouragement from them as Hedwig herself. 
** Poor thing ! *' I heard him say, partly to himself; **she has no mother." 
But I am wandering from my text. I was speaking of chess. 

I have seen him watch a game all through ; and as his daughter was 
approaching the inevitable mate (I don't mean a pun), the benevolent old man 
would regard the student with a sort of compassionate shake of the head, as 
much as to say, ** You won't do." 

On these occasions, caring little for the music, I used generally to make up 
to the geological professor, and engage him in conversation on his favourite 
science. He was really a profound man, and the short sentences which he 
puffed out with the smoke of his long pipe were worth attending to. But how 
to es(jM| the ever-recurring question ** Don't you play chess ? All educated 
Englishmen are fond of the game." It makes me blush to think of the many 
shifts and evasions to which I was driven : without actually plunging into a lie, 
I have performed many a circmt round one ; declaring that I had played, and 
knew the moves — that it was too scientific, and so on. When asked if I had 
ever seen McDonnell play, ** Who is McDonnell ?" was my answer. (The 
fact, however, was, that I had played many games with that great man, who 

gave me a knight and . Ah, well ! never mind.) This excited still more 

surprise in the good Germans. ** What ! an Englander fresh from his native 
land, and not know McDonnell 1 " 

After going to one or two of these parties, I found that they were becoming 
more and more attractive to me ; and the cause of the attraction was neither the 
Professor of Geology nor the game of chess. What, then, was it ? If any one 
suggest the Professor's daughter, it is not in me to deny the soft impeachment ; 
for certainly there was enough in her of beauty, both of mind and person, 
sweetness of temper and engaging expression, and the low musical voice, the 
exponent of all three, to justify my preferring her before geology, chemistry, 

Y 2 



2Y6 THK CHBSS WORLD. 

and chess. Suffice it, therefore, that on one particular evening (it was the last 
sotri4 oi the Easter term, my third term in the University), Hedwig came 
smiling towards me, and, with a slight blush on her fair open &ce, invited me 
to be her partner in a consultation game against her father and a student who 
was reckoned a strong player. I don't know whether any incautious word 
of mine had led Hedwig or her father to penetrate my secret ; but whether or 
no, the invitation placed me in a dilemma. If I declined, I should have con- 
veyed an expression of indifference to one whom I was anxious to please ; if I 
accepted, I should probably betray my skill, and thus be drawn into the magic 
circle of chess-play. However, I accepted the invitation, and sat opposite to 
my fair partner at a small table, with our opponents some way off, although 
this was scarcely necessary, for the music and the conversation would have 
prevented our overhearing their deliberations had they been nearer. On casting 
lots, the move feU to our opponents, and they opened a gambit, which we 
accepted. They resolved it into the old form of AUgaier, which is so well 
known.* All the book moves were made, up to the eighth move of the first 
players. Hedwig suggested every move, and I did not venture to differ ; but 
now was a grand opportunity of playing a new move in the defence (suggested, 
I believe, by Homy, but not much known at that time), viz., K. B. to Q. third. 
I could not resist the opportunity of proposing this move to Hedwig. 

II, — ^But that confines our Q. P. and Q. B. ; besides, they can play their 
K. P. upon our two pieces. 

A, — I need scarcely point out to you, Mademoiselle, that if they do so, we 
capture the pawn for nothing ; for if they retake with the Queen, we win h«r 
by K. R. to K. 

** That is very true," said Hedwig, musing. ** I wonder what papa will 
say to that ; I am afraid he will not think it a legitimate move. But how is 
this, Mr. Albert ? I thought you knew nothing of chess beyond the moves, 
and in your first game you play like a master 1 " 

A, — Indeed, Mademoiselle, I think very lightly of my poor skilL But this 
move is not my own ; it is by a countryman of yours — Herr Homy. 

H, — ^Then I do not think papa ought to object to it. Shall we try it T 

A, — If you have no decided objection. 

I fancied that she had an objection to play anything that seemed to be 
heretical, lest her father should not be pleased; but I could not resist the

If, — I am only a consenting party, papa ; it is Mr. Albert's move.

I immediately called out ** 9. B. takes P. ;" and if the old man was surprised

then came to us in some agitation and said, ** Yes, if we take, we lose the 
Queen; and if we do not take, we lose the game. But how is this, sit^ — I 
thought you were not a chess-player ? " 

I made the same reply to the Professor as I had already done to his daughter. 
All the Company now assembled round the board, and the coup was discussed 
and admired. But one result, which I regarded at the time as a misfortune, 
was that many, in one unanimous voice, declared that I must be a chess-player 
by book, or by nature, as was proved by this crucial experiment of playing the 
right move at the right time. 

" What shall his pimishment be? He shall sit down this instant, and play 
a game with Miss Steilmann, in the presence of this goodly Company." 

Thus was I getting deeper into difficulties every moment. I sat down, 
determined to play my very worst. I knew that my fair companion had a 
tolerably good book knowledge of the openings, and could take advantage of 
any ordinary blunders of mine. I therefore hoped still to be able to establish 
my reputation as a very bad player. Every one present being interested in the 
tourney, there were plenty of spectators ; and the Professor sat by his daughter*s 
side, and, resting his elbows on the table and his face on his hands, looked on 
with as much grimness as his mild face was capable of. 

" What shall I play, papa? " asked Hedwig, after we had each moved K. 
P. two steps, 

** A regular Bang's gambit," answered the Professor. " I want to see how 
he manages the defence."

If, — 4. K. B. to Q. B. fourth. ^.—4. Q. to K. second.

as if to say, " That's right, my dear ! If he takes the pawn, there is danger of

However, I did take the pawn, checking, and Hedwig played 6. K. to 
B. second. 

Every one looked with some amusement to see if I moved my Queen out of 
the jaws of the lion; it was my object to lose her,«nd I played 6. K. Kt. to 
B. third, a move which no^one wondered at after such a specimen of play as 
was exhibited by my third move. Of course, Hedwig played R. to K. Most 
of the spectators tittered ; some of them turned away to seek other amusement, 
thoroughly convinced of the truth of my repeated assertions that I was a weak 
player: the Professor leaned back in his chair, and regarded me with his 
** You won't do " expression. 

Now, I don't think I am a vain man; but on this occasion I was stung by 
that titter — ^hurt by the Professor's contempt, which I was afraid Hedwig might 
share. I was, therefore, no longer anxious to lose the game. On the contrary, 
I determined to do my best to win it. I had not very accurately measured 
Hedwig*s strength, but thought it likely that, even after losing my Queen, a 
little combination might give me such an advantage in position as would enable 
me to retrieve the game. The spirit of the old Westminster Club revived 
within me, and my fancy pictured McDonnell commanding the sable host, with 
his admiring Boswell, in the person of Greenwood Walker, by his side. I no 
longer saw before me the fair young thing that had wound herself about my 
heart, but a chess foe that could only be beaten in stem fight. I played 7. P. 
to Q. fourth, and Hedwig captured the Queen, of course. My eighth move, 
Kt. takes R., checking, on which K. retired to Kt. square; and my ninth, P. 
takes B., caused the Professor to lean forward again ; and I felt instinctively 
that his interest in me was beginning to revive. Some of the lookers-on 
advanced closer to the table, and Hedwig was evidently alarmed at the price 
she had paid for my Queen. She played 10. the obvious move, Q. to K. second, 
and my equally obvious reply was 10. P. to K. B. fourth. Her Q. then cap- 
tured the Pawn at my Q. B. fifth. I played ii. Q. Kt. to B. third, and she took 
the Pawn at E. B. fifth with her Q. B. This enabled me to get the move into 
my own hands. I played 12. P. to K. Kt. fourth, and she captured my Q.B.P. 

The Professor was somewhat restless in his seat during these moves, and the 
company was silent When I played 13. K. R. to R. second, some one whis- 
pered, •* He knows what he is about." 

The Bishop retired — 13. B. to K. Kt third, and I advanced my K. B. P. 
upon him, and he retired to K. B. second. I now brought my heavy artillery 
forward — 15. K. R. to K. second, and Hedwig took the opportunity of playing 
16. Q. Kt. to Q. second. I next played 16. Q. B. to K. third, which of course 
was met by 17. P. to Q. fifth. 

" He docs indeed know what he's about," said one, in a tolerably loud

whisper. " All these moves are compelling ones, you see." I now castled, 
and the move was accompanied with a buzz of surprise. My fair opponent had 
evidently only two things to do, either to capture Kt. with Kt., or to take B. 
with Pawn. She naturally preferred the former, inasmuch as it still left a piece 
en prise. Whereupon I played 19. B. takes P., threatening the Queen and 
winning back the Knight. The Q. retired to her B. third, and at move twenty 
I captured the Kt. with my B. 

The poor Professor was much excited; drops of perspiration stood on his 
brow; he was anxious for his daughter's chess reputation, which he thought 
would be permanently damaged in losing such a game, after winning the Queen. 
He was too honest a player to give a hint, or even to make the most distant 
allusion to what ought to be the move; he was, however, evidently relieved 
when he saw his daughter play 21. Kt. to Q. fourth. His face^ brightened with 
the thought that Hedwig's three pieces bearing on my castled King's side, and 
with my Kt immoveable, must turn the game in her favour; but a cloud again 
came over his face when I pinned the Kt. by playing 21. B. to K. Kt. second. 
But she brought her R. to the rescue: 22. R. to Q., and hope again dawned. 
But I also had a Rook to oppose to her Rook, and I mercilessly played 22. K 
K. to Q. second, doubling my Rooks. In order to liberate her Kt. and still pin 
mine, her next move was 23. Q. to Q. B. fourth; whereupon I took Kt. with

as effectually as if it were free." 24. She took my B. with her B., and I retook 
with R. 25. R. takes R. on both sides, thus leaving me "with three pieces 
against the Queen. « 

The Professor was in despair, aud moved uneasily in his seat. Hedwig had 
evidently given up sdl hope of retrieving the game. She might have checked 
at K. sixth, but with no effect, as she probably saw; for she played 26. Q. to 
K. second; and now to complete the cruel work on which I was engaged, I 
played 26. Kt. to K. fourth, having a move in store, which I thought would 
immediately finish off the game. Her natural move was 27. P. to Q. B. third; 
to which I replied 27. P. to K. B. sixth. ** A good move," remarked some one 
behind me. The Professor directed a piteous look to the speaker. Hedwig of 
course played 28. P. takes P., upon which the Kt. retook, checking. If the 
K. went to B. second or to Kt second, the Q. was lost by R. to Q. seventh. 
If the K. went to K. B., the Q. was equally lost by B. to Q. sixth. The only 
move therefore was 29. K. to R. I then played R. to Q. seventh; the Q. went 
to B. square, and I mated with the R.

To THE Editors of the Chess World. 

Gentlemen, — ^In the May number of yoor valnable Periodical, at page 
69, you have inserted an observation on the snbject of my essay on the King's 
Knight's opening; extracted from the I^iladdphia Evming BnUdtn, The 
author of this observation seems to reproach me that in speaking (in the said 
essay) of the normal opening, — 

I. P. to K. fourth P. to K. thixd 

a. P. to Q. fourth P. to Q. fooith 

I have not mentioned the attack introduced by Mr. L. Baulsen, — 

3. Kt to Q. B. third 
which the writer in question prefers to the rcQaSax move of 3. P. takes P., 
because, after — 

3. B. to Q. Kt 6fth 

4. B. to Q. third Kt to K B. third 

5. B. to K. Kt. fifth P. to K R. third 

6. B. takes Kt Q. takes B. 

7. Kt. to K. B. third Kt to Q. B. third 

8. Castles B. takes Kt 

9. P. takes BL P takes P. * 
10. B. takes P. 

the Black's Q.'s Bishop will be confined in his movements by the K.'s Pawn. 
It is true that the writer does not give the forq;oing moves as forced, but he 
thinks that as a consequence of Mr. Paulsen's attack. Black cannot in any way 
avoid the constraint of his Q.'s Bishop. The extreme courtesy with which the 
author of the observation expresses himself on the subject of my article, induces 
me to hope that he will show the same indulgence with respect to my present 
reply, which I have long hesitated to render public. It would, indeed, have 
been impossible to defend myself without saying frankly what I think of Mr. 
L. Paulsen's innovation. Now, being unable to approve of that innovation, I 
should have preferred, as in my essay, to have said nothing about it The 
ability of Mr. L. Paulsen as a Chess-player, is, in my opinion, not merely 
remariuCble^ it is phenomenal; and I have certainly no intention to depreciate 
his skill. But what can be more natural than that a player of such strength 
should adopt irr^ular and abnormal attacks for the better disconcerting his 
opponent ? There is, in Chess, a frmdamental law arising from the nature of 
the game, and which ought never to be lost sight of in judging of match games, 
or others actually played: it is that the advantage of the first move permits to 

* We must remaiie diat Black has no need to take this Pawn, and should have Castled 
at this mo^.

him who has it, the liberty of taking; yarious routes, while his adversary has 

the choice only of a few moves consecrated by long experience. The same 

deviations are not on tb^s account more commendable ; they sometimes lose 

the advantage of the move ; they sometimes give to the first player an inferiority in 

p^i&ftion, bat very seldom compromise him irretrievably. To cite only examples

of the fifft species^ let ua.say that, in the eoyal opening, (i. ,)

In the same way, if the game began i. , the r^;ttlar attack of 

P. Jo Q. fourth 

White would consist in advancing his Queen's B.'s Pawn two squares, or 
playing his KL's Pawn or Q.'s Kt.'s Pawn one square. But he would not by 
any means compromise the future of his game if, instead of the above, he were 
to play Kt. to Q. B. third, or Q. B. to K. B. fourth ; although those moves are in 
a very bad style, and opposed to proper principles. In the first place, the said Kt 
retards the advance of the Q.*s B.'s Pawn, the advancement of which is absolutely 
essential for the preservation of the move to the first player in all openings on 
the Q.'s side. As to the Q.'s Bishop, it is generally useless at the K. B.'s fourth, 
his true place being at the second square of the Q.'s Kt., where it can operate 
upon the adverse royal wing. Again, the freedom the first player has to 
deviate, augments considerably at the third and fourth moves of these openings ; 
and we shall see presently that the new attack of Mr. L. Paulsen, which we 
are reproached for having neglected, comes within this last category of irregu- 
larities. 

We have a few words to say on the fear which the author of the observation 

in the Philadelphia Evening Bulletin expresses, to the effect that the legitimate 

P. to K. fourth 

result of the French game, ( , ) with the best play on both sides, 

P. to K. third 

must leave the second player with his Queen's Bishop confined. This 'appre- 
hension could be justified only in case the Bishop were obliged, as in the Royal 
Opening, to be played on the King's side. Now, the writer seems to forget 
that this Bishop is mainly serviceable close at home, in the openings on the 
Q.'s side, of which the French game is the principal. His best place, and that

at the 5th move.

* Philidor has taught us not to bring out, without necessity, the Knights before the Pawns.

preference, for it was not until forty years after the death of the Master, that players began to 
see the difficulty of defending satisfactorily the Black K.'s Pawn in this latter opening. But 
to attack the adverse Q.'s Pawn with the Q.'s Kt. in the two situations of the text, with no 
other effect than to have the K#. pinned, hardly looks tike a master's move.^ And we must be 
permitted, on this point, to follow submissively the teaching of Philidor.

THE KING'S BISHOFS GAMBIT.— "THE IRREGULAR 

DEFENCES." 



To THE Editors of ths Chess World. 

Gentlemen. — ^In a foot-note to a game between Messrs. Rosenthal and 
De Riviere, pubBshed in the August number of the Chess World, the able 
Elditors of the Book of the Paris Chess Congress, append the following pertinent 
remarks apropos of the various defences to the King's Bishop's Gambit: — 

''The defence called ''classic," wMch consists in now giving check with 
the Queen at King's Rook's fifth, is abandoned, as also is that of F. to K. 
Kt fourth, which is found in anci^it authors. 

The Counter-Gambit of Kieseritzki, P. to Q. Kt fourth, is &llen into 
disuse. There remains, then, the defence of 5. P. to Q. fourth, or 3. Kt to 
K. B, third; or, lastly, 3. P. to K. B. fourth, as in the present game. This 
move gives, in our opinion, a slight advantage to the second player." 

As the move of 3. P. to K. B. fourth, which has been appropriately designated 
the " old classical defence," in contradistinction to the *' modem classical 
defence " of 3. Q. to K. R. fifth (check), has received but comparatively little 
attention in this country, albdt some of the leading German authorities, pro- 
nounce it to be the best reply to the sortie of the King's Bishop, that Black has 
at his command, I shall venture to offer a few remarks on some of the most 
noteworthy features of the variation, in the hope that such of your readers as 
take an interest in Chess theory may be induced to test, by actual play, the 
efficiency of this '' modem revival." Let me remark in limine^ that the following 
analysis promises little or no claims to originality, but is derived mainly from 
German sources ; such as the Handbuch^ the Theorie^ and Praxis^ and Schach* 
zeitung\ and the few modem games, illustrative of the defence, with which I 
happen to be acquainted. 

l^hite. Black. 

1. P. to K. fourth P. to K. fourth

To THE Editors of the Chess World. 

Gentlemen, — In reference to M. Eraser's proposed move of Q. to K. B. 
third for White in the McDonnel Gambit, in place of the ordinary move of 
K. to Kt second (at the seventh move), I may state, that this move is given 
in the first edition of Von Bilguer and Der Lasa's Handbtich^ published in 
1843, and now lying before me ; this move will be found at page 344, at the 
sixth line from the top. The novelty of M. Eraser's move consists in this, that 
he first of all, as far as I know, appears to have succeeded in proving that this 
move of Q. to K. B. third, could be made safely and successfiiUy. At the time 
of the publication of the Ifandbtichy as well as before and also since that time, the 
strong players of Germany, thought the proposed move of Q. to K. B. third 
(as the seventh), decidedly inferior to the ordinary orthodox move of K. to 
Kt second. 

In 1843, when I came to reside here, after my long sojourn in continental 
Europe, I played seven experimental games, at this very opening, with a fine 
Polish player, Michalouski, formerly president of the Dumfries Chess Club, 
now long dead, and we both thought the move Q. to K B. third, inferior to 
the move K. to Kt. second. When " Gamma " was here in July last, and 
also when I visited him at his own home last week, we played over together, the 
very interesting variations given in M. Eraser's able anal)rsis, and we agreed 
that in all the variations. White, the opening player, had the advantage, or at 
least appeared to us to have the better game. However, humanum esterraret 

Much credit is due to my landsman for thus reviving an interesting line of 

play, which had been abandoned as untenable by the strong players of 

Germany. 

Yours, &c., 

Kirkconnel Manse, DELTA. 

I2th Octobery 1868.

Book of the Paris CoNORssa — This long expected work is on the 
point of completion. Four hundred pages are now ready. These contain 
the games contested for the Emperor's prize ; the matches of Mr. Neumann 
with Messrs. Winaw^re and Rosenthal ; the tourney which took place at 
the Cafi^ de la R^ence, and the Problems contributed by 18 competitors

The Late Joseph Greekiev^o, Esq^ of Sheffield.— At a meeting 
of the council of the Athenaeum Chess Club, held on Tuesday eyening — 
the President, ytr. G. S. Taylor, in the chair— it was unanimously resolved, 
on the proposition of Mr. G. B. Cocking, that the following record of the 
valuable services rendered by the late Mr. Greening to the club be entered 
in the minute-book, viz: — ^'Mr. Greening^s connection with the club 
commenced with its establishment, Nov. 22, 1847, his name being the first 
that appears in the minute-book. He was elected a member of the first 
council, and was re-elected to each succeeding one, without intermission, 
up to the time of his death. On several occasions he was elected 
president or vice-president, which offices he sustained creditably to 
himself and advantageously to the club, until failing health necessitated 
bis absence. He was one of the most r^ular attendants at the meetings 
of the club, and on all such occasions his amiability and geniality of 
disposition were manifest, iustif]png the admiration of his associates. The 
strongest player ever found in him a worthy antagonist, and the novice a 
courteous instructor. His absence will be noted by casual visitors, and 
lamented by those who knew him best; he will be missed firom his accus- 
tomed seat, and long will it be ere his loss is forgotten." 

Huddbrbfield Chess Clur. — ^The opening meeting of the season of 
the Uuddersfield Chess Club was held on Saturday last, at the G^rge 
Hotel Chess-play commenced at four o^clock in the afternoon. Amongst 
the company present were Dr. Scott, Messrs. J. Watkinson, J. H. 
Finlinson, J. R. Robinson, D. Boscovitz, T. Mellor, W. Allan. E. Dyson, 
W. C. North, H. Wigney, A. Robmson, J. E. Wheatlee, T. Holliday. 
• T. Fleldsend, (Bradford). T. Arkwright, (Hohnfirth), &c. The annual 
meeting took place in tne evening— Mr. Watkinson in the chair. In 
opening the proceedings, Mr. Watkinson alluded in feeling terms to the 
loss the Club had recently sustained by the removal from amongst them 
of their lamented President, Mr. David Marsden. Mr. J. R Robinson 
proposed that Mr. John Watkinson be elected President of the Club. Mr. 
Watkinson, he said, had been the life of the Club since its commence- 
ment, sixteen years ago, and had devoted much time and labour to the 
promotion of its success, and it was chiefly owing to him that the 
Uuddersfield Chess Club had risen to be the leading Club in the county. 



THE CHKS8 WOELD. 



305 



Mr. J. H. Finlinson having seconded this proposition, it was carried 
unanimously. Mr. Watkinson, in reply, thanked the members of the Club 
for the position they had placed him in. It was an honour somewhat un- 
expected on his part ; but he should be glad to do all in his power in the 
future, as he had done in the past, to promote the prosperity of the Club. 
The town had been recently enlarging its boundaries, and he thought it 
was a fitting time for then: Chess Club to throw more life into its 
proceedings. He took the present large gathering as an augury of a spirited 
session. The other officers appointed were :— Vice-President, Dr. Scott; 
Hon. Secretary, Mr. J. H. Fmlinson; and Committee-men, Messrs. G. 
Brook, jun., J. Eastwood, and J. R. Robinson. Chess-play was then 
resumed, and continued till a late hour. A handicap tournament was 
held, Mr. E. Dyson coming off the winner. In the course of the eveninff 
Mr. Fieldsend, of Bradford, proposed that a match betweien the Bradford 
and Huddersfield Clubs should be played during the winter; and in all 
probability it will be contested in January next.

CHESS EDUCATION. 

It is much to be regretted that we have no national Chess oiiganication deserving 
of public confidence. How long the present state of things — ^so very unsatisfactory 
as it is — ^is to continue, it were rash to conjecture. But it does not follow that 
because a so-called British Association stands in the way, there can, therefore, 
be nothing done for British Chess. Ought there not, for that very reason, to 
be the more diligent enquiry as to what can be done to alleviate existing evils, 
and to prepare all who wish Chess well, to act in its interests when the opportune 
moment comes? Especially is there need for earnest, unflagging, individual 
effort, when, as now, associated effort is hampered, — when Chess suffers injury- 
through a compelled quiescence in its domains. Perhaps the Editors of the 
Chess World will allow one who has neither the leisure for Chess, nor the 
opportunities he once had, to throw out a hint or two appropriate to these 
do-nothing times. So far as my suggestions are of value, others may manipulate 
them to better purpose. And even should any of my notions be found worthless 
or impracticable, it can do little harm to propound them; for discussion is better 
than stagnation. For my own part, I like to see evident error stand boldly out, 
since truth can then be set side by side with it Your readers must not think, 
however, that I mean, at this time, to set before them anything particularly 
questionable. 

Chess gets, in many respects, but sorry justice at the hands of its friends. 
We may not be ashamed to acknowledge a casual acquaintance with it ; we 
own we know the moves and reckon it not at all a bad game, but as for any 
lively interest in it, that may be known but to a few who are possessed with a 
like admiration. We listen to the reproaches heaped on it; we hear, without 
protest, its merits set down as defects; we even assent to murmurs muttered 
against it by men that know nothing of it Because it is not a frivolous parlour 
amusement, because it is not like this or that or the other pastime, it is decried, 
and the special place that belongs to itself is not recognized. I would have 
Chess-players resist, more manfully, this tabooing of their noble game. But 
more than this is required; their action must be not defensive merely, but 
aggressive. Not only must care be shown to counteract current errors regarding 

VOL. IV. — NO. IX. 2 B 



314 TUB CHESS WOBLD. 

the game, and prevalent prejudices against it, — ^mnre must be attempted in the 
way of educating young men in Chess. I propose nothing so Utopian as the 
introduction of Chess chairs in the Universities; nor would I bring the game 
into contempt by encouraging " professionals " of the sort we know, to go the 
round of the Public Schools. What is wanted is a more informal education. 
It wotdd cost many of us little time and trouble to give valuable aid and great 
encouragement to youths that show promise of aptitude for Chess. If it is my 
&vorite pastime, my love for it should lead me to do what little I can to imbue 
those I can influence with something of my liking. It may be no treat to me 
to give the Queen to a big boy, and beat him, but it may render him, and 
through him may render Chess, very important service. If I continue to give 
the Queen and a hint or two, I may come to have more enjoyment in the play; 
and should my pupil prove apt, I shaU soon have to play at considerably less 
odds. By and by I shall possibly begin to think I am losing my power of 
combination, my skill in strategy. Perhaps so. At any rate, the distance 
between myself and my opponent is rapidly lessening. At length he beats me, 
playing even ; but my high estimate of my own abilities induces me to attribute 
this not to the weakness of my play, but to the value of my teaching. Some of 
my pupils may boggle at receiving odds; these are not so hopeful. In a few 
cases it may be prudent to humour this fancy of self-conceit, though as a rule it 
should be resisted. One of the best schemes our thends can adopt, if they 
would dedicate their efforts to "Prince Posterity," is to give loving lessons to 
any who, though they are but tyros in scientific knowledge of the game, evince 
a hearty interest in it, and an anxiety to practise it. 

The clubs, too, ought to prosecute according to their opportunities, th« 
good work of Chess education. The committees and members of clubs too 
frequently content themselves with providing for their own entertainment. 
Naturally enough, thjey first of all look to convenience for their own play, and 
also, it may be, to securing for themselves the means and facilities for studying 
the game. But societies, as well as individuals, should be aggressive. What 
hinders clubs to entertain, encourage, and instruct others in addition to their 
own members ? They would add greatly to their strength by the institution of 
subsidiary agencies. In some of our large towns flourishing clubs would, I 
think, flourish all the more were they to fit up comfortably, but at little expense, 
a room or two for the accommodation of men that cannot afford to join an 
exclusive club. Clerks, artizans, &c, would there have opportunities of stud3ring 
and practising the game. Admission to such rooms might be granted at a 
nominal charge, and should occasionally be free. Two or three would under- 
take the charge of the property in return for the privilege of having admission 
gratis; and the only restriction requisite seems to be the prohibition of all work 
and amusement excepting Chess-play. Some such method should at least have 
a trial. Committees would do well to consider it ; and i( as is extremely 



THB CHIfiSS WORLD. 315 

probable, some of them can improve on this plan, let them favor other com- 
mittees, through the Chbss World, with the result of their deliberations, or 
(better still) of their experience. 

I am ashamed, on looking back, to find that I have employed so many 
words to say so little. There is, I daresay, little that is novel in what I have 
said. Let it not be forgotten, however, that there are times when common- 
place truths need to be spoken, and spoken again and again, and when com- 
monplace duties urgently demand attention. The present is such a time. We 
regret the pr<esent; let us turn our regrets to practical account by making- pre- 
paration for the future. Better days are surely to dawn on us, .and in view of 
them we ought to be labouring individually so as to hasten them on, and so 
that, when the good time does come, we may be prepared to enjoy its good.

THE PROFESSOR^S DAUGHTER. 

( ConHnued from page 279.^ 

A murmur of applause ran through the room; but the Professor reproved 
Hedwig for an oversight "How could you be so blind? You might have 
drawn the game,*' said he, ^*by a perpetual check." A tear trembled in 
Hedwig*s eye, and she left the room. 

My friend the geologist came forward as the champion of the young lady. 
** A thousand pardons, Herr Professor, there is no perpetual check^-the game 
is forced any way." 

* Surely not," returned the Professor; "if instead of the useless move of 
Q. to B., she had played Q. to Q. B. fourth (check), she might at least have 
won the Bishop." 

*• Again pardon me, Herr Professor," replied the polite geologist " Yott 
could not capture the B., because there is a mate on the board — R. takes P., 
mate. Besides, if Q. checks, the B. interposes. Now just let us go through 
the moves : 

Q. to B. fourth (check) B. to Q. B. third 

Q. to Kt eighth (check) K. to B. second 

Q. to B. seventh (check) K. to Et. third 

Q. takes Kt B. takes Q. (check)— 

the only move to prolong the game. 

K. to Et B. to Et serenth

and the pawns must win."

316 THE CHB8S WORLD. 

•* But why should the Q. go all the way to Kt. eighth to check ? '* asked 
the Professor ; ** why not check at K, sixth, then at K. seventh ; again at Kt 
fourth, and so on.'' 

During this discussion, in which all present were interested, I quietly left the 
room, took my hat, and departed. I was in no humour for sleep, and wandered 
out of the town to a height overlooking the Rhine. The moon was near the 
full, and the sky was cloudless ; the cool night air was refreshing to my heated 
brow, and the loveliness and tranquillity of the scene restored calm to my 
agitated mind. Instead of thoughts rushing through my mind with the rapidity 
of the bright stream below, I was able to look steadily at the events of the 
evening, and their probable consequences. That the Professor was mortified 
at his daughter's defeat was evident; but as I knew that he respected chess 
talent in others, I hoped he would forgive it in me, although his own kin were 
the victim. That I had previously avoided chess encounters was, I thought, a 
favourable circumstance, and I had been so evidently driven into that contest ; 
I had not sought to show off my skill, and had played this with so much reluc- 
tance, that I felt pretty confident of the Professor's love of fair play for setting 
me right with him. But how about Hedwig — how did she feel ? I knew that 
her love for her father was superior to all other considerations ; and if he were 
reconciled, she too would be friendly. I also trusted to her woman's nature^ 
which admires intellectual strength in man, and despises weakness (for, in spite 
of modem Bloomerism, that is woman's nature, as instinctively true as the ivy 
clinging to the oak, or seeking support from the castle wall). Had she won the 
game, as I first intended she should, she would not have been content with her 
triumph at the expense of my weakness. It is true that this might be supposing 
too much — ^namely, that she regarded me with interest — I fondly hoped that 
such was the case ; at any rate, my course seemed clearly to be to make friends 
with her father. But how ? A lucky thought occurred to me. Koch, in the 
introduction to his CocUx^ has a poem on the Origin of Chess. It is the old 
story of that intolerable Brahmin and his impossible grains of wheat The 
professor had once pointed this out to me as one of the finest specimens of 
poetry in the German language ;. and expressed a wish to see it translated into 
English verse. I determined to gratify him that very night. I hastened to the 
porter's lodge, and asked for Qui^ the laboratory assistant ; he was not at hand, 
but his son (whom the students named Cujus^ the genitive of the paternal 
patron3rmic) was there, and I told him to get me the key of the laboratory, 
because I wanted to fetch a book. He soon procured the key and a lantern, 
and we found the Codex by the side of the last edition of Berzelius's huge 
work ; and I carried it home with me. I asked the landlady for an extra 
candle, and was soon deeply occupied in transferring Koch's lines into sounding 
English heroics, The poem contains two hundred and sixty-six lines, besides 
notes ; but as it is in blank verse, my task was easy. 



THE CHKS8 WORLD. HlV 

I began in this style: — 

'Twas at the time when Attila the Hun 
Wasted the plains of fruitful Lombardy, 
. There dwelt In distant Inde a youthful king, 
Tyrant no less than Attila the Hun. 
Unskill'd to rule his people or himself, 
Ignoring other empires save his own. 
He deem'd the empire worshipped him as god, 
And that his words and acts were wise and good, 
Himself the source whence wisdom, goodness, flow'd. 

I finished my translation, and made a fair copy by about seven in the morn- 
ing — folded it in the form of a letter directed to the Professor, with my respects 
— ^took it to the porter, and told him to place it on the Professor's breakfast- 
table. I returned home and prepared for the laboratory, whither I went at my 
usual hour of 9.30. I was busily engaged in weighing some precipitates, when 
the Professor cam* in. He had a fresh rose in his black velvet coat, and his 
face was so radiant with smiles, that he looked as if he had been drinking "sun- 
shine and water."* He took both my hands in the most cordial manner, kissed 
me on both cheeks, after the custom of his country, and thanked me for my 
magnificent Uebersetzung. He said that his daughter was particularly charmed 
with it. I enquired how Mademoiselle was, but made no allusion to the events 
of the previous evening; I thought it best to ignore them, unless the Professor 
himself should introduce the subject. **She is quite well," said he, "and is 
now hard at work in the garden.' 

** Would you kindly allow me to pay my respects to her? " 
** Certainly," replied he. "Take my key and go in by the stone wall gate." 
I was only too glad to avail myself of the permission. Papa was k nd, and 
I hoped Mademoiselle would be equally so. I entered the garden with a pal- 
pitating heart, and found Hedwig trimming the bed of flowers before the little 
arbolfr. She wore a round straw hat and a jacket, which showed off her beau- 
tiful figure to perfection. She looked very lovely, and I felt that the agitation 
and blushes which we usually assign to maidens on occasions of this kind, were 
rather on my side. 

As soon as we began to talk, I was relieved. She gave me her hand, and 
thanked me warmly for the gratification I had afforded her father. "It was 
very kind of you, Mr. Albert, to take so much trouble; the lines are very nice, 
and I shall make them the subject .of a good many lessons in English." 

* I should explain that this was Hedwig's expression; and it arose from her father's habit 
of taking his morning's pipe in a little shady arbour in the garden, into which the morning sun 
just peeped, and lighted up the shelf on which the Professor placed the glass of water which 
he drank with his pipe. The water shone out resplendently, and when the Professor was in a 
very bright humour, Hedwig was accustomed to say, that " Papa had been drinking sunshin* 
and water." 



B\S THB CHBSS WORLD. 

*' If they meet with your approbation, I shall indeed think highly of them, 
and especially if you and the Herr Professor will accept them as a peace- 
offering." 

" Oh, you are thinking of my foolish behaviour last nighty' she remarked, 
with a blush. "Indeed it is I that should make a peace-offering; " and she 
gracefully presented me with a rose. 

"You will at any rate forget and forgive the deceit I have practised so 
many months, in concealing my chess-play," I remarked with* some embarass- 
ment. " Anything like deception is so intolerable to me in your presence that 
I grieve over this, although my motive was pure." 

She blushed deeper, but recovering herself, said, " Ah I that does deserve 
some punishment ; but you are punished already. Everybody is talking about 
you. They all declare you are the finest chess-player in the University ; and 
they talk of getting up matches between yon and some magnates. But why 
evade chess as you have done ? Why play with such evident reluctance as you 
did with me last night ?" 

" Because," said I, leading her to walk up and down the garden, "1 prefer 
being a good chemist to being a good chess-player." 

** *Mais Pun tCemptche pas Vautrk.^ as Cuvier said to a philosopher, who 
boasted that he knew nothing of Law, because he knew a good deal of Natural 
History." 

** That may be true of some minds, but it does not appy to mine," I 
answered." ** Indeed, I doubt whether any man is capable of excelling in 
more than one pursuit. It is easy to get a little knowledge of many things; but 
the seeker after excellence is bound by stem necessity to his own pursuit. Even 
in so trivial a thing as dancing, the proficient must go through the wearying 
rounds of practice, eight or ten hours a day for years together; and when 
excellence is attained, must still practise hours per day to retain it It is so in 
all pursuits, mental and physical, and especially so in chemistry." 

" I am sure you will succeed," said Hedwig. "The Divine blessing always 
accompanies singleness of purpose and purity of mind." 

" I have no friend or relation in the world, Hedwig," said I, "at least, no 
one who cares for me. Science must be my friend and companion; and she, 
like the higher Being to whom you refer, is a rewarder of them who diligently 
seek her. At least, until I get an appointment, I cannot hope for such a friend 
as my nature yearns for — unless you, Hedwig — pray forgive me — will allow me 
to hope when I have left this place that you will think of me." 

"If it is not wrong to say so," replied Hedwig, turning pale, but looking 
full in my face, " I will think of you as my best friend, next to my father." 

"Thanks, dear Hedwig," I said, "and should your father not object, may 
I continue to call you dear Hedwig — ^my own — for ever.?" 

We were now "unter den Linden" in the Professor's garden. The lark 



TUB GHKSft WOBLD. ol9 

was carolling in mid-air, the flowers were the only eyes that watched ug, — 

and here on this bright and beautiful morning Hedwig sank into my arms and 

whispered ** Dear Albert." 

I have little more to say. Five years after this eventful mom, I was 

appointed to the Chair of Chemistry at ', which I still retain. Hedwig 

immediately became die Frau Ptofessorinn — an appointment which, I am 

happy to say, she still retains. 

Wishing you success in your new work, I remain, &c., &c., 

ALBERT. 

P.S. — My wife, who has just been reading the above, requests me to say 
that our respected father still retains the title of der Herr Professor^ although 
he retired from the professorship some years ago. Before his retirement, he was 
so fortunate as to meet with two new minerals, which he named respectively 
Berzdiusite and Kochite. His analyses of them are published in PoggendorTs 
Annalen^ and also in Silliman's American Journal of Science, The Professor 
is still in good health, though advanced in years. During our long vacation we 
visit him at his small house on the Banks of the Rhine, and he returns with us 
to pass the winter in England on account of the milder climate. He has taught 
our eldest boy chess, and endeavoured to inspire him with respect for the Codex 
but I am sorry to say that the young rebel prefers Staunton's Handbook^ and 
justifies the choice on the ground that Koch does not give the Evans' gambit. 
The Professor says th^t Mr. Staunton would have immortalised himself if he 
had brought out a new edition of the Codex instead of the Handbook and

15. Q. to Q. fourth 

Black has a Pawn more, but, in return, two of his Pawns are doubled and 
isolated. On the whole, the absence of other minor Pieces, than two Bishops 
running on different colored diagonals, and the facility with which Black may 
force an exchange of Queens and Rooks by the sacrifice of one or two of his 
Pawns, ensures him, in my opinion, a drawn game.

BRITISH CHESS ASSOCIATION. 

The genend meeting of this Aflsodation commenced its proceedings on 
Tuesday the 24th inst, at the rooms of the St George^s Chess Clnb, 20, 
King Street, St. Jameses. 

Lord Lyttelton presided, and in opening the meeting, took the oppor- 
tonity of congratulating the members upon the sacoess wliich lias followed 
the efforts of the committee in making the arrangements for tlie viirioiis 
contests. He read a letter ftom Lord Dartrey, expressing that nobleman^s 
regret at being unable to take part in the present proceedings on account 
of his engagements in Ireland. 

His lordship concluded by calling upon the Hon. Secretary, Mr. Medley, 
to explain to the meeting the details of the arrangements determined upon. 
Mr. Medley had great pleasure in announcing that for the Challenge Cup, 
11 players had entered; 19 for the Mongredien Prize of 20 guineas ; 20 for 
the Otounearm Prize of £26; 24 for the Handicap Toumay— four prizes 
amounting to more than £40 ; and several for the blindfold matches. He 
stated that it has been determined that for the Handicap Tournament and 
Oknotoorm prizes only one game should be played by each competitor. 
For the Challenge Cup, the same arrangements will be continued as those 
under which Mr. De Vere won what may be considered a half -right to the 
Cup. A sub-conunittee was then appointed for the purpose of handicapping 
the competitors in the Toumay. In this handicap the winners in the first 
round have to play for the first and second prizes, and the losers to contend 
for the third and fourth. Each pair will play one game only, exclusiye of 
draws, and the average time allowed over each more will be four minutes. 
The entries were : Messrs. Green, Mongredien, Baxter, De Yere, Stdnitz, 
WIsker, Selfe, Bayliss, MacDonnell, Newham, Chinneiy, Gossip, Lord, 
Lowe, Franklin, Harper, Murphy, McComas, and Blackbume. The 
following is the handicap. First class — ^First divifflon : Bev. G. A. Mac 
Donnell, Steinitz, Blackbume, De Vere. Second division : Lowe, Wisker, 
Lord, Kewham, and Grossip. The first division to give move to the second 
division, and the whole class to give a pawn and move to second class, a 
pawn and two moves to third class, and a knight to fourth class. Second 
class : 8. Franklin V. Green, T. Harper, Dr. Murphy, W. M Chinnery — 
to give pawn and move to third class, and pawn and two moves to fQurth 
class. Third class : S. J. Green, H. 8. Selfe, W. Bayliss, McComas, W. 
Franklin, and Baxter — ^to give pawn and move to fourth class. Fourth 
class: A. McDonnell, Haggard, Ida, Mongredien, jnn. 

The pairing for the Glowworm prize then took place as follows, the 
terms being that the losers in the first mnnd should go out, and the winners 
to play on until two remains, who will contend for the first and second 



THE CHESS WORr,P. 346 

prizes. One game to be played between each p^iir. Average time allowed 
over each move, four minutes : —

In regard to the Mongredien prize, it was arranged that the mode 
of displacing the pieces should be announced at the various Clubs. The 
terms were — 

1. Both Knights on the King's side, and both Bishops on the 
Queen's side. 2. King and King's Rook to change places, as also Queen 
and Queen's Rook. 3. King and King's Bishop to change places, as also 
Queen and Queen's Bishop. 

The following is the pairing of the combatants for the first round 
in the Mongredien prize: —
