Dr. Murphy. 



Resolved— That five minutes be allowed for the consideration of 
each move to each player; but that in the consultation games there 
shall be no limitation of time. 

It was also resolved that the exhibition of blindfold play should 
take place at St. James's Hall this week, on Monday, Tuesday, Thursday, 
and Friday, on which days the consultation games will also be played. 



Bides and RegulaMons to he observed by Players m (he Tournaments. 

1. In the contest for the Grand Challenge Cup, the maximum time to 
be allowed each player for making his moves shall be two hours for 
twenty moves ; and in the other contests, two hours for thirty moves, 
antagonists being at liberty to restrict the time still further, should they 
agree to do so. 

2. The time occupied over the moves shall be mesuiured by sand-glasses, 
one for each player. When it is a player's turn to move, his sand-glass 
T3hall be set running, and shall continue so until he shall have completed 
his move, when it shall be stopped, and his opponent's glass set running 
in the same manner; and so on to the end of the game. 

3. The game shall be divided into periods of twenty moves, or of 
thirty moves, according to the term of the contest, cominencing with the 
first move on each side. Should a player not have made twenty moves, or, 



346 THE CHESS WORLD. 

as the case may be, thirty moves, within two hours, or the corresponding 
number of moves within four hours, and so on, he shall foi*feit the f^asne. 

4. The games shall be played out at a sitting; no adjournment shall 
take place, and no player shall leave the room without the consent of his 
opponent, except for a reasonable time for refreshment. Any player 
analyzing an interrupted game by moving the pieces, either by himself or 
with others, shall forfeit the game. 

5. Combatants pidred off shall agree upon the hour at which their 
next game shall commence, such agreement to be entered in a book to be 
kept for that purpose. Should a player not appear within one hour of 
the appointed time, he shall forfeit the sum of five shillings. At the 
expiration of the hour his sand-glass shall be set runniDg, and the time 
thus lost shall be considered as wasted by him. Should he have only 
thirty minutes or less time left, in which to make his first twenty moves, 
or thirty moves, according to the case, he shall then be allowed to make 
his first forty moves, or sixty moves, in two hours, plus this remaining 
time. In any case he must complete forty moves, or sixty moves, within 
four hours of his glass being set running. Should he not make his 
appearance within five hours of the appointed time, he shall forfeit the 
game. Should neither player appear within five hours of the appointed 
time, each shall forfeit the sum of one pound. 

6. In the contest for the Challenge Cup. the privilege of moving firet 
shall be so arranged that each player shall exercise it against one-half of 
his antagonists. 

7. JQach player shall be bound to play five games per week, failing 
which, the committee will decide what penalty shall be inflicted. 

8. The second player in each game shall furnish to the secretary or 
manager a copy of the same within a reasonable time after its termination, 
under a penalty of ten shillings. 

9. The games being the property of the association, none shall be 
published or given for publication, without the consent of the managmg 
committee. 

10. All fines shall be paid to the funds of the association. 

11. All disputes shall be referred to the managing committee, whose 
decision shall be final. The committee reserve to themselves the power 
to modify any of the foregoing regulations. 



Erbatum. — The friendly correspondent who did ns the favor to send the chief 
games played at the Rhenish Chess Meeting, made the mistake of transposing the 
names of the players in Game No. LVl, p. 219. From the Berlin Sckachzeitung, 
we haye just learned that the first player in that game was Mr. W. Panlsen, and 
that it was his opponent, Mr. Zokertort, who won, and not Mr. W. P.

Political Checkmate. — It is said that Mr. Disraeli has discovered 
a remarkable move in Chess, a move which will doubtless receive the 
name of its great inventor. It enables any young beginner to disconcert 
and vanquish the most skilful and accomplished player. It may be freely 
described as follows : — If you find yourself hopelessly beaten, look your 
adversary coolly in the face, turn the board round as unobservedly as you 
can, so that his pieces appear to become yours, and say " Mate ! " before 
he can protest.

CHESS ON THE MISSISSIPI. 

A Sketch. 

In the year 1844, I found myself on board the good steamer "Mound City,'* 
bound for that great western metropolis. Our boat was named from St. Louis. 
The captain was an ardent Chess-player, and, having so strong a bond in 
common, we soon became intimate. Yet the gallant skipper made but a poor 
score, for he was too good an officer to tender to Caissa tl?at exclusive worship 
the coy nymph requires from those who seek her favors. If a floating log 
struck the boat ; if some dispute, likely to end in a quarrel, was heard in that 
Pandemonium, the lower deck ; or the pilot's voice rung aloft, answered by 
"4 feet 6" from the bows, the skipper was on the hurricane deck in twenty 
seconds, and poor Caissa forgotten for the time. Little disturbed the monotony 
of that most monotonous of rivers, or the careless, easy life on a western 
steamboat, until at Memphis we took on board a young gentleman, who 
seemed to have just stepped out of Broadway, sent, I suppose, by some New 
York house to accomplish the easy task of getting new orders, and the 
somewhat more difficult duty of obtaining returns for old ones. Among a 
certain class of New Yorkers (as one of the Manhattanese, I may say it), there 
is an intolerable assumption of superiority, a vulgar pretension that I have 
never seen equalled except by that quintessence of snobbishness, the London 
Cockney. This one was a rare specimen. His elaborate toilet (he seemed to 
have just left Christodoro's) contrasted with the careless apparel of the 
gentlemen on board as much as his supercilious air differed from that of 
the quiet, gentlemanly planter, or the frank, manly homeliness of the western 
farmer. Seeing me engaged in play, he stepped up to the table, and began 
to make his comments in a tone of great self-confidence. He soon informed 
us that he belonged to the New York Chess Club, had played with Mead and 
Thompson, and that Stanley was not able to give him much odds.* But 
what the odds were he did not volunteer to tell us. Stanley was then in 
his prime, and the acknowledged champion (about this time he conquered 
Rousseau). I then recognized the gentleman as one of the habitues of the 
Carlton, where my uncle used to give him a Piece, and then make him feel 
the weight of that terrible Evans attack, which no one forgets who has ever 
played the defence against it. 

* At the time this anecdote was written for the American Chess Monthly ^ these players 
were well known frequenters of the New York Chess circles. 

VOL. IV. — NO. X. 2 E 



354 THE CKE8S WORLD. 

I invited him to take my seat, and as the Captain miwarily accepted the 
gambit, he was check-mated in a dozen moves. Several other gentlemen 
encountered him during the next two days, but with very little success, for I 
soon saw he had Walker's Treatise (the Handbook was not then published) at 
his finger's end, and no one on board but he and I had ever looked into a Chess 
book, or seen any real Chess-play. I declined playing, under pretence of 
headache, witil I had studied the gentleman's play. I saw he was a Chess 
parrot^ for if he did not obtain an easy Avinning position in the opening^ or else 
get a Pawn ending, to play mechanically, he usually lost the game. But it was 
seldom that the game was not decided in the opening. Like Mercutio, his 
adversary was killed " by the book of arithmetic." Then he began to give 
odds ; but that made little difference, for he generally won before his opponents 
could use their superior force. It was raw militia against r^;ular drilled troops ; 
and Walker's tactics were as good as Scott's. 

Feeling sure of my man, I approached him as he sat, solitary and alone, at 
the Chess table, with his arms over the back of the chair, smoking his regalia, 
and no doubt thinking liimself a second Philidor. No one seemed inclined to 
encounter him, and he had the field to himself. The truth was, that though 
they were surprised at his apparent skill, and most of then anxious to learn 
from him, his intolerable, patronizing manner, and ill-concealed contempt for 
their play, had driven every one from the table. 

Determined to take advantage of my boyish appearance, and putting an 
extra dash of hesitation into my address, I hurriedly stated that I should like 
to try a game, as I was anxious to play with a scientific player who imderstood 
book play. 

•* Very well," said he, ** sit down, and I will give you a Rook." 

" Nay, sir, if you will play me one game, only one, even, I shall be much 
obliged; after you beat me I will take odds: games at odds are so irreg^ar that 
I cannot learn the opening from them. Will you be kind enough to obh'ge me ?" 

** Oh, certainly," said he, with an air that I vowed I would pay him for 
before I was done with him. 

We set up the men, and he looked round at the company gathered at the 
table, with a smile that said plainly: ** Come, gentlemen, and see me demolish 
this greenhorn." 

We drew for the move; it fell to me. He seemed disappointed, but 
brightened up when I (having the White men) played: — 
(White,) (Black.)

2. P. to K. B. fourth P. takes P. 

3. Kt. to K. B. third P. to K. Kt. fourth 

4. r. to K. R. fourth P. to K. Kt. fifth

"How unfortunate! What a confounded oversight! There is luck in 
Chess, after all. I had a dead won game; now I must get the most I can for 
the Queen, and make a draw." And he played — 
r4. Kt. to Kt. fifth 

" Suppose I did not take your Queen, could you win the game?" 

" How silly," said he, " don't you see my Queen. Knight and Pawn must 
win against everything?" 

"If I pass your Queen the next two moves, and you are beaten after that, 
will you pay forfeit. Western fashion?" 

"Oh," said he, "if I lose I'll treat the crowd with all my heart. If my

" He taught me," was the larconic answer, as in the South- Western fashion 
we all adjourned to the bar, and smiled at the young gentleman's, expense 
in more senses than one.

THE "CHESS WORLD" PROBLEM TOURNEY. 

In the January Number of the " Cabss WoblD,** for the year 18(56-6, 
when our magazine was, so to speak, quite in its infancy, we laid before 
our readers the conditions of a Problem Tourney. 

The prizes offered were of no inconsiderable value, and we hoped they 
would prove sufBlciently inviting to composers to provoke a keen contest, 
and furnish some excellent stratagems. We were disappointed, and before 
the twelve months, over which the competition was to extend, had 
elapsed, it was resolved on account of the limited number of the com- 
petitors, and the general poverty of the problems sent in, that the affair 
should be shelved until. the "Chess Would" was more extensively 
known. 

Now that it has become the only recognised English organ devoted 
exclusively to Chess, we propose to re- open the lists on precisely the 
same terms as before ; we trust with a better result The arrangements 
of the Tourney will be confided to Messrs. J. Q. Campbell, Q. McArthur, 
W. S. Pavitt, and J. W. Abbott, who will examine the problems subr 
mitted for competition, and decide upon their respective merits. We 
append the conditions. 



THE CHESS WORLD; 383 

1. Each competitor to send six problems — ^three in three moves ; two 
in four moves ; one in five moves. 

2. Each contributor of six or more problems, of the description 
specified, to the " Chess World," will be considered a candidate for the 
prizes. 

3. The problems to be sent in two at a time, and at intervals of not 
less than a month between each contribution. 

4. Each problem to have the name of the author legibly inscribed at 
the top of the diagram. 

5. All the problems so contributed which are thought deserving of 
publicity will be inserted in the " Chess World," as nearly as is prac- 
ticable at the time they are received, and will subsequently be published in 
a book, of which every competitor who has three of his problems printed 
in the " Chess World " during 1869, will have the right to a copy. 

6. All problems which are found to be false, as admitting of more 
than one solution, or as being impracticable in the number of moves 
specified for their solution, or as being impracticable in the number of 
moves specified for their solution, or as contravening in any way the 
Laws of Chess which are to regulate the Tourney, will, at the final 
adjudication by the Editors of the " Chess World," be withdrawn from 
the competition. 

7. The problems to be all ordinary mates; that is to say neither 
fettered by fancy stipulations, nor what is called suicidal. 

8. The problems to be composed in accordance with the Rules of 
Chess in the Chess Praxis. 

9. The copyright of the competing problems to be the property of the 
Proprietors of the " Chess World," without whose express permission 
they are not to be published elsewhere. 

10. The problems, after having undergone the ordeal of public 
examination by their insertion in this Magazine, will be examined care- 
fully by the four gentlemen already mentioned, who will award the prizes, 
impartially, to the most worthy sets, and will publish their report in an 
early number of the " Chess World " afterwards. 

11. Any composer who submitted problems for competition when 
the Tourney was first opened, will be permitted to select the whole, or a 
part; of his set from the problems sent in then. 

Prizes. — 1st Prize— A. Ten-Guinea Set of " Staunton" Chessmen, in 
Ivory. 

2wtf Prize — A Five-Guinea Set of " Staunton " Chessmen, in Ivory. 

Zrd Prize— K large Club-size Set of " Staunton " Chessmen, in Ebony, 
and Boxwood. 



384 TUS CHESS WORLD. 

INTERNATIONAL CHESS CONGRESS AT BADEN, 1869. 

Abbanobmentb have been made for holding a Grand Chess Gathering 
at Baden in the sammer of this year, under a directing Committee, com- 
posed of: — ^His Highness Prince Stonrdza, President; M. de Tourguenjiff, 
Vice-President; M. Kolisch, Secretary; K T. Weih, Treasurer; M. M. 
Grund, Henri de Haber, Baron Maytheny, and Colonel Morgan, Com- 
mittea The following is the Provisional Programme : — 

I. — Revision and Settlement of the EtUes, 

The Congress will hold two sittings for the definitive fixing of the 
rules of Chess. The first, held the 15th of June, will vote a rough draft 
of a code, which will be sent during the sitting of the Congress to all 
the European Chess celebrities, inviting them to approve of it, and add 
all the remarks which their experience may suggest to them. 

The second sitting will be held after the end of the tw^o tournaments. 
The replies obtained will be then read and discussed, and the Chess code 
submitted to a definitive vote. 

To be a member of the Congress, to have a right to vote, and to assist 
at the tournaments and banquet, the sum of 30 francs must be sent to the 
Treasurer, 

IL — Grand International Tournament— Prisse of Baden 3,000 francs. 

Players of all countries will be admitted to compete for this prize, 
offered by the administration of Badeu, on paying an entrance fee of 
50 francs. Each player will play three games with each of his com- 
petitors. Whoever shall have won the greatest number of games, after 
the deduction of lost ones, will be the conqueror. 

Drawn games will be counted as a half game to each of the adver- 
saries. 

The fees of this tournament, and the disposable resources of the 
treasury, will be applied to the formation of second and third prizes. 

Ill Handicap Tournament, 

Amateurs of all countries who wish to assist at the Handicap, will have 
to pay a sum of 20 francs, which will be destined to form the two prizes. 
The players will be divided by the Committee into four classes ; the first 
will give to the second the advantage of the Pawn and move; to the third, 
of the Pawn and two moves; to the fourth, of the Knight. 

The second-class will give the advantage of the Pawn and move to the 
third, and of the Pawn and two moves to the fourth ; and the third-class 
will finally give the Pawn and move to the fourth.

TRANSACTIONS* 

Amoko othei* expedients adopted by the Managing Committee of " The 
British Chess Association *' for eliciting Five Shilling Subscriptions from 
unwilling pockets, has been the promi'^C) repeated from time to time^ of a 
Report of the Association's proceedings, With the Qames played, and the 
Problems submitted at the various meetings. The publication of the 
present volume has been delayed without any apparent good reason. 
The main reason seems to have been— indeed this is avoWed— the diffi- 
culty of raising sufficient subscriptions. But persons who did subscribe 
have had just cause to complain, that promises, not demanded, but volun- 
teered, have, by this system of continued delay, been needlessly broken. 
In reply to those complaints It may, however,, be said that subscribers 
ought to have known better than expect anything else, and also that now, 
on the appeaittnce of this volume, the pains of prolonged expectation will 
be allayed by the discovery that they have been but wsdting for what has 
been given them before. 

The book contains the record of What the Association has done since 
1862. It turns out, however, that the transactions from July in that year 
to March 1866 have to be detailed as Irish snakes have been described. 
At a meeting in June last when the appointment of a Manager was 
discussed, one ^' took occasion to render justice to the efficient manner in 
which Mr. LoWenthal had, for the last seven years, discharged the 
duties." If we may rely on this report it seems that for nearly four of 
the seven years the duties were nil. We believe that nothing was done 
during the four years except the getting out of another over-due book, 
and the getting up of a testimonial to Mr. LowenthaL Within a little 
over two years, there have been two meetings or " congresses " and one 
Problem Tourney, of which this book is the brief chronicle. 

The work has very few of the charms of novelty. It is in a great 
measure a reprint of what has been already published. The Association 
has been in the habit of asserting its property in the games played at its 
meetings, and in the Problems submitted for competition. They must

WHAT ROME MISSED. 



Ax August sun shone down upon the city of Pompeii ; upon temple 
and mart, upon marble portico and bronze statue, upon the crowded 
dwellings of the Via Domitiana full of life, and upon the glaring monu- 
ments of the Street of Tombs, full of death. It shone down with oppres- 
sive heat upon the motionless waters of the Cumsean Gulf, and upon the 
sleeping plains of the Campania. Outside of the houses there was 
scarcely a sign of life. The Forum was nearly deserted. The marketmen 
had long since returned to their suburban gardens ; the public offices in 
the Basilica were closed; the shops of the money-changers were empty, 
and only an occasional priest passed in or out of the portals of the 
Pantheon, or under the arches of the fanes of Jupiter and Venus. But, 
shaded by the Doric colonnade that ran around three «ide3 of the public 
square, a few groups of idlers were still engaged in discussing the events 
of the day, in criticising the last judicial decision of the ^dile, or in cal- 
culating the probable issue of the next gladiatorial combat. But just as 
the sun^s rays seem hottest, the attention of these knots of gossipers is 
drawn to the figure of a man, who, coming from the direction of the 
Herculaneum gate, descends the flight of steps at the north-west comer, 
and enters upon the Forum. While he suddenly pauses and gazes, with 
the keen and curious glance of a stranger, upon the stately architectural 
splendor about him, various comments, some of wonder and some of 
raillery, are made upon his appearance and garb. His dress and manner 
prove him to be no Roman, whilchis bronzed features, and his indifference 
to the summer heat, attest the influence of even a warmer climate than 
that of Southern Italy. 

" By the Gk)dsl " said one of the members of the nearest group, ** Yon 
Spaniard would be a rare assistant for Vulcan. Kot the hottest forge in 
the divine workshop would draw the sweat from his brow." 

'' Kay, CsBcilianus," replied one of his companions, " call him not a 
Spaniard: he is one of the obstinate swine-hating people of Judsea. 

VOL. IV. — NO. XI. 2 H 



394 THE CHESS M'ORLD. 

They say that some of his race have made old Sol stand still; perhaps 
this Jew exercises a like power to repel the scorching beams of the god 
of day." 

" How can both of you, who spend more than half the year in Rome, 
fell into such an error? " asked the third person of the group. " Can you 
not see that his swarthy features and long ungraceful poenula could 
belong to no other than to a son of Egypt? " 

But the approach of the stranger put an end to their discourse, for 
upon a nearer view, there was that in his dignified bearing, his com- 
manding form, and his dark, speaking eye, which silenced ridicule and 
rebuked cariosity. After a courteous salutation, he began, with a marked 
but not unpleasant foreign accent, to question the three Pompeians con- 
cerning the use and character of the buildings which surrounded the 
Formn. When they had pointed out the various objects of interest in 
sight, and answered his queries at some length, Csecilianus ventured to 
ask in return, whether he had been at Rome. 

" No," said the stranger, " but I go. I came very lately from Alexan- 
dria, and more remotely from my native India." 

The Italians started, for an inhabitant of that distant and to them 
almost mjrthical region was indeed a wonder. Roman geographical 
knowledge at that time, like Roman conquest, had barely reached the 
confines of Persia. Of all the Asiatic world beyond, Europe possessed 
only the inconsistent and unsatisfactory accounts brought by a few 
illiterate and credulous traders. As soon as the first momentary surprise 
had, in a degree, subsided, they overwhelmed the foreigner with a 
thousand eager inquiries about his country and its inhabitants, at many 
of which the Indian smiled, but to all of which he replied with unvarying 
good humour, and with that calm indifference to the prejudice and 
ignorance of his auditors which characterises the true traveller. Did his 
countrymen burrow in caves like the Scythians, or dwell in huts of reeds 
like the sable tribes of Africa ? Did they worship Isis of Egypt or the 
fire god of Persia ? Did they congregate in towns or were they a nation 
of wanderers like the rude Germans ? Was it true that they destroyed 
their children and burned their wives, for such rumours had reached 
Romans ears ? Was it true that in their quarter of the earth the moon 
was visible only eight days in the month, as some of the geographers 
related, and that the Great Bear was never seen at all ? Was it true that 
in farthest Taprobane the worms were taught, by cunning art, to manu- 
facture the richest silks ? At length Cfficilianus glancing at a dial which 
stood near them changed the theme. 

" Nay, Scaurus and Calventius, remember you are both to dine with 
me to-day, and we have scarcely two hours left for the bath. If this 



TUB CUBSS WOKLD. 395 

engaging stranger will do my poor house the honor to meet us at the 

coena, we can listen further to his marvellous tales." 

The Indian accepted the invitation with the same frankness with 

which it was given, and after Csecilianus had' indicated with distinctness 

the locality of his dwelling, and the hour of the repast, the four speakers 

separated. 

********* 

No writer of these days can do justice to the luxury and splendor of 
an Italian banquet in the time of imperial Titus. No pen can accurately 
describe its gastronomic delights. The wealthy Romans, not alone in the 
capital, but in the chief provincial towns, were accustomed to cover their 
tables with fish, fruit, aad fowls, collected from the most distant parts of 
the empire. The dominion of Neptune and the Naiads yielded oysters 
that had been produced on the wild coasts of Britain, and had after- 
wards been artificially fattened in the Lucrine Lake; salmon from the 
limpid waters of the picturesque and beautiful Aquitaine; turbot that 
originally swam about those islands where now arise the famous tower of 
St. Mark^s and the elaborate front of the Doge^s palace, and lampreys, 
upon whose native element cloud-capped Etna looked down. The tangled 
Leucanian forests, and the broad woods of Tuscany, rewarded the skill of 
the dexterous huntsmen with the favorite wild boars; while kids, reared 
by the Corinthian settlers of Ambracia, found their way in abundance to 
the Italian markets. From the air — Nature's great aviary — came the 
tender ortolans that, in their brief lives, had hopped from tree to tree on 
the banks of the smooth-flowing Amo, the delicate woodcocks of the 
Phrygian hills, and the sweet-flavored Melian cranes. The fruitful earth 
was made to give up its treasures. Dates were sent from the sunny 
mountain sides of Syria; pomegranates were brought from the wide 
plains around Carthage, where they were cultivated by the old Punic 
foes, now the subservient subjects of the Eternal City; olives grew 
nowhere better than on the loamy soil of Sicily; and apples, of a color as 
enticing as those of Paris, were imported from the land of the frugal 
Epirots. When the palates of Csecilianus and his guests had grown 
weary of enjoying such dishes as these, they adjourned (after two or three 
hours given to digestion and the bath), to a second triclinium for the 
purpose of passing the hours of the night in a social commissatio. The 
obedient slaves had meantime placed upon the polished board, wmes from 
the rocky bluffs of Homeric Chios, from those green Setian hills which 
overlook the Pontine marshes, from the famed Lesbian vineyards that 
surround pleasant Mitylene, and beside them amphorsB filled with the 
generous, heady, full-bodied Falernian, clear and translucent as amber. 
While profiting by this Bacchanalian wealth, the well-informed Indian 

2 H 2 



396 THE CHESS WOKLD. 

portrayed to his new friends the characteristics of his Eastern home. 
Many were the stories he told of the Indian gods, of all-powerful Brahma, 
of destroying Siva, and of the mild and blessed CamadeTa. Many were 
the scenes he pictured, in poetic words, of the domestic life of the Hindoos, 
and many the chapters he repeated from the sacred Yedaa To the en- 
quuy of Scaurus, as to his object in coming to the West, he at first made 
no reply, but drew from a pouch concealed by the ample folds of his 
robe, a small box and a roll of thick and heavy papyrus. Unfolding the 
latter, the Pompeians beheld a mimic pavement, composed of small 
squares, such as adorned many an atrium in their city. The squares were 
all of the same color, but were divided from each other by delicately- 
traced lines of black. Then opening the casket, the stranger took from 
it a number of images, carved with exquisite workmanship, out of the 
whitest ivory, and the darkest eboAy. The Italians, with wondering eyes, 
watched him arrange these little statues, partly on one side of the board 
and partly on the other. At last he turned, and thils addressed them: — 

" This, O courteous Romans, id not the least remarkable production of 
the land of the Ganges and Indus, and is called the Chaturanga. It is a 
representative contest, a bloodless combat, an image, not only of actual 
military operations, but of that greater warfare which every son of the 
earth, from the cradle to the grave, is continually waging — ^the battle of 
life. Its virtues are as innumerable as the sands of African Sahara. It 
heals the mind in sickness, and exercises it in health. It is rest to the 
overworked intellect, and relaxation to the fatigued body. It lessens 
the grief of the mourner, and heightens the enjoyment of the happy. It 
teaches the angry man to restrain his passions, the light-minded to become 
grave, the cautious to be bold, and the venturesome to be prudent. It 
aflfords a keen delight to youth) a sober pleasure to manhood, and a 
perpetual solace to old age. It induces the poor to forget their poverty, 
and the rich to be careless of their wealth. It admonishes kings to love 
and resi)ect their people, and instructs subjects to obey and reverence 
their rulers. It shows how the humblest citizens, by the practice of virtue 
and the eflforts of labor, may rise to the lof tieet stations ; and how the 
haughtiest lords, by the love of vice and the commission of errors, may 
fall from their elevated estate. It is an amusement and an art, a sport aud 
a science. The erudite and the untaught, the high and the low, the 
powerful and the weak, acknowledge its charms and confess its entice- 
ments. I learned to like it in the years of my youth; but as increased 
familiarity has developed its beauties, and unfolded its lessons, my 
enthusiasm has grown stronger and my fondness more confirmed. At 
length I casually heard from some merchants who traded with your 
Eastern provinces, that the mighty nation whose arms are fast conquering 



THE CHESS WORLD. 397 

a world larger than that of the Macedonian monarch, knew nothing of the 
existence of the Chaturanga, and I resolved to inform your countrymen 
of an art so pleasing and instructive. It was for this that I left my tropical 
birth-place, and travelled over seas and deserts far towards the setting sun. 
It was for this that I passed several months in the schools of Alexandria 
that I might accquaint myself with the accents and accidence of your 
tongue. It is for this that I am here to-day, and it is for this that I hope 
in a week to be among the palaces and the temples of earth^s mistress." 

The Indian then proceeded to explain the powers of the pieces and 
pawns, and loud were the expressions of praise and pleasure that escaped 
from the lips of his listeners. They declared that the fascinating dice, 
the complex duodecim scriptm and the agreeable Indus latruncuhrum were 
unworthy to be mentioned beside this new amusement. Growing com- 
prehension increased their plaudits, and when every featm-e had been fully 
described, when they thoroughly understood the march of every officer 
and all the movements of the foot-soldiers, they longed to attempt its 
practice and taste its enjoyments. Under the guidance of their sage and 
zealous master they began. The delicious wines were forgotton; the 
couches of the triclinium were abandoned; the slaves stood unaddressed 
at their posts. Morning came, and noon, and those eager disciples still sat 
at the board, studying and enjoying the novel charms of the glorious 

Oriental pastime. 

********* 

Suddenly burst upon ill-fated Pompeii that terrible misfortune of 
which, happily for the human race, history furnishes few examples. The 
beautiful city, the inhabitants who made it their home, and the strangers 
who sojourned within its walls, all perished. And Rome, and the 
Roman world remained, until their imperial power had passed away 
for ever, utterly ignorant of the unalloyed and unending delights of 
the ChaXuiajigsL^American Chess MonUdy,

4. P. takes P. Kt. takes P. 

By exchanging these Pawns, White enables his opponent to renounce 

the close defence, which he should do only when the result is decisive. 

Here, so far from this being the case, the exchange only gives White a 

short-lived attack, very easily to be parried. On this account, authors, 

with the exception of Messrs. de Riviere and Morphy, scarcely 

mention it These writers, on the contrary, think this line of play

42« THK CHESS WORLD. 

CHESS MATCH BETWEEN THE MELBOURNE AND CITY 

OF ADELAIDE CHESS CLUBS. 



A record of this interesting contest wliich was played by means of 
Electric Telegraph in September la^t, has been published by the Com- 
mittee of the Melbourne Club, and from this we take the following 
account of the preliminary details, and the Gkunes in the Match. * 

The Melbourne Chess Club haying received and accepted a challenge 
from the City of Adelaide Chess Club to play a Match of seven games by 
means of the Electric Telegraph, the following conditions were mutuaUy 
agreed to: — 

1. That the number ofplayers on each side be seven. 

9l That each player play a separate gama 

8. That the Boards be numbered 1 to 7, and that the players be 
arranged at the Boards by lot ; the Adelaide player, who draws Board 1, 
pl^jring agidnst the Melbourne player drawing the same number, and so on. 

4 That the Melbourne players, at Boards 1, 8, and 5, and the Adelaide 
pilfers, at Boards 8, 4, and 6, have the first move, and that the move at 
Board 7 be drawn fbr by lot 

0, That no consultation or reference to books be allowed. 

(L That the rules to be observed be the same as in play over the 
Board, as laid down in Staunton's Hand-Book ; especially that in regard 
to ** touch and move." 

7, That no game be a(^oumed without the consent of both players, 
and any adjourned game shall be played out on the following evening, 
unless special arrangements be made at the time for its resumption ; and 
if either player fidl to keep the appointment made to resume such game, 
he shall fbrf eit it 

8l That in the event of a game being adjourned, no analysis or 
examination of such game be allowed till its resumption. 

9. That in order to save expense the players should endeavour to 
finish the games at one sitting, and that each Club pay its own expenses. 

10. That a representative of either Club be in the room of the other 
during the progress of the games. 

The players on each side having, in accordance with these conditions, 

*See P»ge 422 of the Present Number:

drawn for Boards, were found to be paired against one another in the 
following order : —

Mr. G. W. Rusden of Melbourne, and Mr. Ingleby of Adelj^ide, were 
appointed umpires. The Melbourne p^yer won the first move at Board 7. 

Play began at 8.50 on the evening of September the 2l9t, but owing 
to unavoidable interruptions, the last game was not finished till 6.80 a.m., 
of Tuesday the 29th. ^hQ result was then found to be, that Melbourne 
had scored 5 games ; Adelaide 1 ; f^nd t^at there was 1 dr^wn,

THB CHESS WORLD. 451 

A PLEA FOR QUICKER PLAY IN CHESS TOURNAMENTS. 



To THE Editors of the " Chess World." 

Ototlemen — It cannot, I think, be denied that the principal cause 
which, liitherto, has retarded the spread of Chess in Great Britain and 
Ireland, is the impression which has gone abroad amongst general Chess 
Society, as well as amongst a portion of the uninitiated public, that to 
play a game of Tournament Chess requires, under the usual time limits, 
several hours of continuous sitting. 

Tales, and true ones, of 10, 12, perhaps 15 hours, without interruption 
— vptoa/rds of a whole day/— over a single game, have startled and dismayed 
many a would be player, who not having the time or physique for so 
terribly prolonged sittings, has recourse to offer recreations, not involving 
so great a tax on his time, patience and strength. Many an amateur 
player knowing this, and who otherwise would win prizes, ceases to 
practise, or go in for them ; and many a non-player also knowing this, 
refrains from learning the game. 

Now the remedy for this state of things is very easy, and is in the 
hands of Chess-players themselves, particularly of the Committees who 
organise Tournaments. 

Chess, since the adoption of Sand-glasses, has been recovering, yet 
slowly, from the shock it received during many previous years by the 
disease of very slow play. 

The existence of this disease, and the necessity for its removal, have 
been already admitted by the introduction of these glasses, but the remedy 
up to this has not been fully enough applied. 

The London Congress Committee of 1862 fixed ten moves per hour as 
the time limit ; the Dublin Challenge Cup Committee, in 1868, improved 
on that, by fixing upon iwdve moves ; and the British Chess Association, 
in 1868 also, to its credit, fhrther improved on that in some of the 
Tournaments, by fixing upon a still faster rate of play, mz.^ fifteen moves 
per hour. 

But the Chess Surgeons will have to go yet deeper with the knife ! 
Ten, twelve, or even fifteen moves per hour, I submit, is much too slow 
a pace. Chess Committees will, I hope, go on further improving, by 
lessening the time limit, until they ^x upon 20, 24, or perhaps 30, moves 
to be made per hour. When they do tliis, they will find that Chess 
Tournaments will be terminated much quicker than they are at present, 
and that many players who do not now enter them for want of the time 
demanded, and in dread of such prolonged sittings, will do so in future. 

The fact, moreover, of the London Committees fixing upon a more 
rapid rate of play, will strengthen the hands of those players in the 
provinces and the sister countries who are willing to adopt quicker play< 



452 TUB CHESS WOBLD. 

as they can then point to the new and faster rate adopted by the chief 
London Chess Circles. 

Besides, slow Tournament Chess produces slow Club Chess, slow 
Club Chess causes slow priyate Chess, and slow priyate Chess frightens 
off recruits from the army of Chess-players. 

Popularise the Chess army, by making its rules and its practices 
compatible wilh the claims on every day life, and its ranks will always be 
full Thus is it, that things and persons act and re-act on each other ; 
thus is it, that games and amusements flourish or decay. 

It may be said that some players are naturally slow, and would 
unjustly suffer if too rapid a pace of play were In yogue ; but the answer 
to this is, that some players are naturally rapid ones, and could play 
as well — perhaps better — at a pace of 30 moyes per hour as at 12. It is 
these latter players who now unjustly suflfer, and why should they be 
called on almost altogether to giye way to the wishes of their slower 
brethren. Let each party, the slow as well as the quick player, giye way 
a little in their respectiye yiews. 

The fair way, then, would be to adopt a much quicker mean than the 
present one, between the rapid player and the slow one, by fixing 
the pace for play in all leading Chess Tournaments for the future at 
ttoerU^'four moves per hour for each player. 

Quick perception at Chess is one of the greatest qualities of a Chess- 
player I Why, then, should it be so much ignored as it is at present by 
pulling it down too low to meet the leyel of the yery slow player? 
Lower the fast player a good deal by all means, but also raise the slow 
player by compelling him to meet the fast one half way, to split the 
difference, to play faster. 

If one resist the temptation to play too slow, he will fall into the 
right and fair pace, the medium one, which will soon come naturally to 
him. Quick thinking and prompt action can be brought about by training 
and determination. Gather up the threads of your reasoning promptly, 
and when you haye hold of them don't let them go, and then there will 
be no necessity for looking after them again; in other words, of 
re-perusing your variations, dreaming them oyer, and Chessplaying is but 
a series of reasonings. 

Slow play is as much a formed habit as a real necessity with many 
players. It grows on one ! Many slow players mentally go oyer the same 
variations several times, and they stop too long to test lines of play, as if 
it were a game by coirespondence that they were playing, in place of a 
game over the board. 

But, after all, it is not so much a question between the fast and the 
slow player that we have to regard, as the expediency and necessity of 
looking at tho game of Chess in a practical point of view, so as to suit 



THE CHESS 'WORLD* 453 

the canvenience of tbe many, without lowering the average standard of 
excellence of the games produced. In the interests of Cliess is it not high 
time that we should do so, that we should considerably curtail the number 
of hours at present granted to play a game of Chess, when contending for 
the principal prizes and Chess honors in these countries ? 

We have occupations to attend to, other interests to loolc after, homes 
to claim us for at least a fair proportion of our leisure hours. We cannot 
make the 8uA stand still until our stretched out game is over. Time and 
Tide wait for no man I Most Chess-players have two or three hours 
frequently to spare — and rightly so, for we busy heads must at times 
have recreation of some kind or another — ^but they have not from eight to 
fifteen hours to spend on a single game in a single day, and thus numbers 
of players are debarred— '■against all the interests of Chess-^from entering 
the Tournaments for our Chief ClUb and National honors. 

Nor is there, as I trust I will be able to show further on, any necessity 
for too slow a pace. 

Many players may, perhaps, think that I am propbsing too ^pid a rate 
for serious Match or Tournament play, for what is called " difl3cult (>heBS : *' 
but I would ask such, what was the rate of play in the cdebrated Anderssen 
— Morphy Match in Paris, 1858, between two first-rate players, with the 
eyes of the whole Chess World upon them, with their respective chess 
reputations at stake, in a Match producing first-class Chess ? It was — 
fractions omitted — Twenty-four moves played by each player per hour/ 
i.e., a game of say 24 moves on each side-*-total 48 moves— lasted but 
two hours, twice as fast as the mean of ordinary time limits for Tourna- 
ments. 

Now, probably, most players are not aware of this, and may, at least 
I hope they will, hold my view when they hear of that fact. 

It was only the other day that I for the first time knew of it, as I came 
across a Chess Book which gave me this^ intelligence in regard to the time 
spent on the games in the above Match, and I was glad to see it, because 
it proved to me that my idea of more rapid play is not a chimerical one 
for Tournament and good chess, and that players of the highest order 
have already, in an important Match, adopted the rate I have suggested. 

Messrs. Morphy and Anderssen played, in their set Match, Eleven 
games. I do not include any of their subsequent off-hand ones. 

The average number of moves in each game was 88 (44 on each side). 
There were two very long games, between 70 and 80 moves on each side, 
and yet these 11 games were played in 48 hours, or a little over an average 
of 4i hours to each game, or about the scale at A below. 

Now the average duration of a Tournament game of this mere ordinary 
length of 44 moves, would on the different usual time limits, be as follows, 
fractions omitted, viz : — 



464 TUB CnES8 avokld. 

A. 44 moves on each ride, at 24 mores p^r hoar, would be about 4 hours per g/aaae,

If no ^me surpassed 44 moves on each side, we should know that a 
game imyer would' go beyond nine houri ;that would be a long sitting), 
oven if we adopted the datoest scale above, viz., ten moves per hour for 
each player. But, unfortunately, we never can tell the extent a game 
may go to. There were two games in the Morphy-Anderssen Match of 
70 and 80 moves each, and in a crowded Tournament there might be many 
such long games, and a possibility of some longer ones. 

Supposing wo take game the tenth in the match, as an example, it was 
77 movos on each side. 

77 Moves on each side, were played by Anderssen and Morphy in 6 hours.

Such long periods of time, es fh)m ten to fifteen hours, can be spared 
but by few players in a single daj/ 1 I say single day, because I assume 
that the wish of tlie players would be to comply with the usual Tourna- 
ment Rules, laid down to play the game out at a sitting^ and rightly so, 
since adjourned games are no longer games over ihe boards and conse- 
quently not mental Choss. 

To bring the case of a long game homs, and make it more apparent, 
suppose two players meet at 8 o'clock in the afternoon — an average hour^ 
not very early nor very late — to play their Tournament game. A long 
tough game ensues of the length above quoted, 77 moves on each side. 

Tlie game would not be concluded. 
On the 1ft more per hour scale, tiU One o'Clook the next Morning— 10 hours duration I

Comimre such lengthened games of 10, 13, and 15^ hours duration — 
and they might possibly be longei^— with the following observation from 
Mr, Walker's admirable little Chess Treatise, 8rd Edition, page 33, where 
It Is said, ^^ an hour to an hour and a half is the fair average time of 
dunUiou for a game. Two such games are Chess enough for one day." 

Now the above long game of 77 hours, commenced at 3 o'clock in the 
Afternoon, would, on the scaled I suggest— 24 moves per hour— be com- 
pleted at half-past Nine ©'Clock in the Evening— 6J hours— or for players 
otherwise engaged during the day, and who could not commence till 



THE CHESS WORLD. 45'6 

fire o'clock, at half -past Eleven in the Evening. This is the long 
game, but the average game in the Morphy — Anderssen Match, of 44 
moves on each side, would in the one case be over at 7 o^Clock, and in 
the other, at 9 o'Clock — 4 hours. 

So that the summing up of the argument is this, let the average 
Tournament game be completed in one sitting in about 4 hours, and the 
probably longest, 6^ hours, instead of as at present from 6 to 9 hours for 
the former, and from 10 to 15 hours for the latter I with a possibility of 
having to play five such games per week, in pursuance with the usual 
rules of the Tournaments. 

Even in a game by correspondence, after hours have sometimes been 
spent over a single move, t!ie best move is not always made, showing 
how hopeless it is in a game over the board to expect to make the very 
best move always. The Chess-board, it is said, could give the finest 
player that ever moved a Knight the odds of that piece, and therefore, no 
matter how long we took to calculate our move over the board, we could 
not hope to play the game throughout to perfection. Therefore is it 
incumbent on all Chess-players to meet the question of obviating slow 
play, guided by the following three main considerations: — 

1. — To fix upon a time limit of general pmctical average convenience, 

in point of duration of game. 
2 — An average time limit that will be neither too slow nor too fast, but 

that will give a fair time to examine the various positions that 

arise, long enough to produce a good game, and to suit the 

average Chess capacity. 
3. — To bear in mind that the player who can produce a game nearest to 

Chess perfection, in the shortest time, is the party to whom Chess 

prizes and honors should most equitably fall. 
I think that the justice of these three positions will be admitted, and I 
opine that the time limit I suggest in this memomndum will meet the 
case, because it has already fulfilled all the above conditions, as shewn by 
the Morphy- Anderssen games. 

Why should Chess be limited to the comparatively few by reason of 
these unnecessary prolonged time limits, when by difflerent and more 
salutory regulations it would, in all probability, become the game, I will 
not say of the many, but at least of many more persons than at present? 
And if it even did not quite fulfil our expectations on that head, we should 
at all events have the consolation of knowing that we removed one of the 
greatest bars to that consummation. 

From the foregoing reasons, and fix)m the fact of Messrs. Morphy and 
Anderssen having played in six hours, a game which on some of the 
usual time limits might have lasted 13 or 15 hours, I think that it will be 
admitted, that there will no longer be any necessity to retain the standards 



456 THE CHKSS WORLD. 

of 10, 12, or 15 moYes per hour limits, which are all too slow, and that it 
is expedient and advisable to qoicken the pace. 

If otherwise, many devotees of Chess will have reconrse to other 
games ; Chess Tournaments will oontinae to be hard to be got through 
from want of time, and games will be adjourned. But let the time 
allowed for playing a game be shortened, let players have to make Zi 
moves per hour each, and all will be changed for the better. The Eighth^ 
hour game — on the 13 moves per hour limit for example — ^will then 
become the four-hour one, the four-hour the two. Players, generally, 
will then be much more willing to enter Tournaments so organised, than 
they are at present, and the tourneys themselves wUl be finished off in 
about half the present Ume: the standard of Chess excellence will not be 
lowered, as shewn by the Morphy-Anderssen Match— and, as some of 
the greatest oversights have occurred in long protracted sittings. A better 
and more popular, because more practical, character, would thus hence- 
forward be given to Chess, and the numbers of Club Members, would be 
increased in consequence of the stimulus thus given to quicker play, and 
from the certainty that a good game of Tournament Chess would be 
played within the rational Units of the spare hours of a day. There 
would be more Matches, more Tournaments, more practice, in short — 
more Chess, induced by the new character that it would obtain in society 
generally, no longer set down as the game of a few only, because held to 
require so great an expenditure of time over a single game, which few 
relish, and fewer still can command. 

In conclusion, I would observe that some of the best Chess-players 
have been the quickest La Bourdonnais, one of the finest players that 
ever scanned a board, was also one of the most rapid. 

The time limit, that I suggest of twenty-four moves per hour for 
each player in Tournament games for the future, cannot be said to 
even border upon that usually spent upon what are called '^skittling 
games,^' which I fancy are generally three or four, perhaps five 
or six times faster. I advocate not these games, which are always 
full of mistakes, but apart from tins, I am fortified in my view by the 
fact above quoted that the mean of the time adopted in the memorable 
match of Mr. Morphy against Herr Anderssen was 24 moves per hour, and 
that was no "skittling" match, nor were the games poor ones in pro- 
portion to the chess strength of the players. An example worthy of 
imitation by all Chess-players, safe for adoption even in a match, free 
from the danger of necessarily producing more inferior games than at 
present, and a matter of vital necessity, if chess is to prosper and spread 
amongst the peoples of the Britishi Isles, nay, even to hold its own amongst 
their many other recreations and amusements — I remain, Gentlemen, very 
truly yours, . " LAMBDA."
